# Changelog

All notable changes to PMetal will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- **Datasets load from JSONL, JSON, Parquet, Arrow (IPC file or stream) and CSV/TSV, and from a directory of shards.** `pmetal_data::rows::RowReader` streams any of them as JSON objects, a record batch or a line in memory at a time; nested columns arrive as nested JSON (a Parquet `messages` column is the array a JSONL row holds), nulls are absent keys and raw-bytes columns are left out. `rows::resolve_files` picks a directory's files as a Hugging Face repository lays them out: the `train` split (or the files that name none), every shard in path order, one container kind, the newest snapshot of a hub cache entry, metadata and `cache-*` files skipped. A file whose name says nothing is read by its first bytes. Five commands and the GUI each had their own "try the `text` column, then `content`, then convert to a temporary JSONL" fallback; they are one call now, and the TUI and GUI dataset previews read rows through the same reader (they parsed raw lines, so a Parquet dataset showed nothing).
- **`DatasetFormat::PromptCompletion`, and a loader that reads real-world rows.** `{"prompt", "completion"}` rows (also `question`/`answer`, `input`/`output`, `query`/`response`), each a string or a list of messages, train the completion only, even when a conversational prompt holds assistant turns. OpenAI-style rows read content-part lists, `tool_calls` (OpenAI or flat), `tools` (a list or a JSON string), a `system` column, `reasoning_content` and a per-message `weight` (0 keeps an assistant turn out of the loss); ShareGPT roles `human`, `gpt`, `function_call` and `observation` map to chat roles; an Alpaca `input` may be null. `TrainingDataset::load` with `DatasetLoadOptions` is the entry point (`from_jsonl_tokenized` and `from_parquet_tokenized` call it, and read every container now); it reads, renders and tokenizes bounded chunks of rows in parallel. `AssistantLoss::{AllTurns, LastTurn}` chooses which assistant turns carry loss.
- **`DataLoader` and `PackedDataLoader` resume mid-epoch and shard exactly.** `state()` returns a `LoaderState` (the epoch's seed and how far into it) that serializes; `restore()` on a loader over the same data resumes into the rest of that epoch. An epoch's order is a pure function of its seed, drawn from an explicit SplitMix64 Fisher-Yates rather than a `rand` generator whose stream is not guaranteed across releases. `DataLoader::with_length_grouping(megabatch_batches)` batches similar lengths together (megabatches sorted by length and dealt into batches, the batches shuffled, the one with the longest sample first), cutting the padding of a variable-length set about five-fold.
- **`TrainingDataset::mix` and `dedup`.** `mix` combines datasets at set ratios into a mixture of a given size (or the largest that repeats nothing): sources are drawn from by largest shortfall, so a small mixture matches its ratios to within one sample, a short source is cycled in a fresh shuffled order each pass, and the result is shuffled by the seed. `dedup` drops exact repeats (same tokens and labels), keeping the first.
- **Packing: best-fit decreasing, real padding, and per-batch statistics.** `PackingAlgorithm::BestFitDecreasing`; `PackerConfig::pad_to_multiple` now pads each row with `pad_token_id` as a block of its own (labelled `-100`, positions restarting, attending to itself only, so no attention row is empty); `PackingStats` counts padding, truncated and dropped sequences. First-fit and best-fit are `O(n log n)` (a segment tree and an ordered set) where first-fit scanned every open row per sample.
- **`streaming::tokenize_corpus` tokenizes any dataset into pretraining shards** (`pmetal tokenize` is a shell over it): Parquet, Arrow and directories as well as JSONL, parallel over bounded chunks, counting the rows it skips and refusing a corpus with no usable document. `StreamingShardReader::try_next_rows` returns each row's document lengths, the boundaries a block-diagonal mask and per-document positions need, and `try_next_batch` returns shard errors instead of ending the stream.
- **`pmetal_data::sample_row_chars`** estimates the text in the first rows of a dataset in any container, nested chat messages included.

- **`pmetal_gguf::GgufModel` opens a GGUF model, split or not, with its tensor data memory-mapped.** Checkpoints above a few GB are published as `name-00001-of-00003.gguf` sets whose first file often holds only metadata; the loader read only the file it was given, so such a model came up with no tensors. `GgufModel::open` takes any shard, opens the set named by `split.no` / `split.count` / `split.tensors.count`, checks that the shards agree (a missing shard, a wrong index, a tensor total that does not add up or a tensor in two shards is an error naming the cause) and presents one tensor namespace. `tensor_data` borrows a tensor's bytes from the mapping, so `dequant::dequantize_into` decodes straight from the page cache into the destination with no intermediate copy. `WeightLoader::load_gguf` and the GGUF config readers use it, so a split model loads, and a directory holding a split model and its multimodal projector (`mmproj-*.gguf`) is one model rather than "multiple .gguf files". Opened against real splits of IQ1_M, IQ4_XS, Q5_K_S and Q4_0 models (up to 100 GB), every tensor maps inside its shard and one tensor of each type decodes to a plausible weight distribution (`tests/real_files.rs`, run with `PMETAL_GGUF_FILES`).
- **GGUF quantization takes an importance vector and quantizes IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS.** `quantize::quantize_tensor(data, dtype, n_per_row, importance)` minimises the importance-weighted error where the format supports it (Q4_0, Q4_1, Q5_0, Q5_1 and every K-quant, IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS): per-column weights, usually an imatrix's mean squared activations, steer the scale search and the joint re-quantization of the sub-block scales so that the few levels go where the activations are large. A tensor made of stacked slices (the experts of a mixture-of-experts layer) takes one vector per slice. Rows and blocks are quantized in parallel with a result that does not depend on the thread count. IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS (4.5 and 4.25 bits per weight, the non-linear table that beats Q4_0 at the same size) can now be written, where only reading them worked. On a Gaussian tensor the error relative to the standard deviation is 0.084 for Q4_0, 0.075 for IQ4_NL, 0.070 for Q4_K, 0.035 for Q5_K and 0.017 for Q6_K.
- **`pmetal quantize --imatrix` weights the quantization, and reads the GGUF imatrix files current tools write.** The imatrix only ever steered which tensors got more bits; the quantization itself ignored it, and a fixed-type method (`--method q4_k_m`) dropped it altogether. It now supplies the per-channel importance to every format that uses one (see above), and the KL calibration scores each candidate type under the same weighting (the output error the layer's activations would see) on a sample of whole rows spread through every expert, instead of a prefix of the flattened tensor. `IMatrix::load` reads both the legacy `.dat` and the GGUF format (`<name>.in_sum2` / `<name>.counts`, divided per expert), `IMatrix::importance` gives the mean squared activation, and saving is atomic and in name order.
- **The GGUF writer streams and is atomic.** `GgufStreamWriter` takes the metadata and the tensor declarations first and the data one tensor at a time (`write_tensor` checks name order and size, pads to the alignment), so a multi-gigabyte quantized model no longer has to sit in memory twice; `GgufFileWriter` and `GgufBuilder::write_file` write it to a temporary file next to the destination, sync it and rename it into place, so a failure or a crash leaves the previous file untouched (`pmetal quantize` uses it). The builder is now a thin layer over the same code and produces the same bytes.
  - The writer refuses what the reference loader would: tensor names over 63 bytes or empty, more than four dimensions, keys over 65,535 bytes, quantized rows that are not whole blocks (a `[4, 40]` Q4_0 tensor has 5 blocks' worth of data but cannot be read), a non-`uint32` or non-power-of-two `general.alignment`. `general.quantization_version` is added when any tensor is quantized, every tensor is padded (the file ends aligned, as the reference writer's do), and `write` no longer needs `Seek`. A file with strings, nested arrays, every scalar type, a custom alignment and a Q8_0 tensor is read back correctly by the reference Python reader.
- **Fuzz targets for the GGUF decoders**: `gguf_dequant` decodes arbitrary block bytes of every tensor type, and `gguf_reader` now reads every tensor the header admits and decodes it (it used to skip anything over 1 MB). Two million runs of each found nothing.
- **A training config file is checked when it is loaded, and every problem is reported with its key.** `FullTrainingConfig::from_yaml` (moved into `pmetal-core` from the trainer's orchestrator, which re-exports it) refuses a key its section does not have, naming it and listing the real ones, so a misspelt `learning_rat` can no longer leave the default in force; a wrongly typed value is reported as `training.batch_size`, not just "invalid type". `ConfigValidator`, a trait nothing implemented, now is, for `TrainingConfig`, `LoraConfig`, `DistributedTrainingConfig` and the whole file, and lists every problem at once: non-finite or non-positive rates, zero batch sizes, a `min_lr` above the peak, a `wsd_stable_ratio` under a schedule that is not `wsd`, both `warmup_steps` and `warmup_ratio`, empty or duplicate `target_modules`, rank 0, dropout outside [0, 1). The orchestrator validates the config after the command line has been merged into it.
  - Files from earlier releases still load. `epochs`, `lr_schedule`, `cosine_num_cycles`, `name` and `path` are accepted for `num_epochs`, `lr_scheduler`, `cosine_num_restarts`, `model_id` and `dataset_id`, which the example in the docs used and which were silently dropped. The keys nothing ever read (`model.dtype`, `device`, `quantization`, `trust_remote_code`, `revision`, `hf_token`, `dataset.split`, `text_column`, `max_samples`, `seed`, `format`, `training.gradient_checkpointing`) are dropped when they hold the value `pmetal init` wrote, and are an error with a reason when they hold anything else (`dataset.max_samples: 1000` used to train on everything); a token in a file is refused without being echoed.
  - A file written by the old `pmetal init`, the documented example, a misspelt key, a retired key with a real value and an empty file are all tests; removing `deny_unknown_fields` from `TrainingConfig` fails two of them.
- **`wsd_decay_style` picks the shape of the WSD schedule's cooldown**: `linear` (the default, so existing runs keep their curve), `cosine` or `one_minus_sqrt` (`1 - sqrt(p)`, which Hägele et al. 2024 find reaches a lower loss at equal length). Warmup, plateau, each cooldown and the floor after it match transformers' `get_wsd_schedule` at every step of a run.
- **Muon, schedule-free AdamW and 8-bit AdamW are named in `OptimizerType`, and `LoraInit` names PiSSA and LoftQ, but a config that asks for one is refused until a trainer builds it.** `OptimizerType::{Muon, AdamWScheduleFree, AdamW8bit}` (`muon`, `adamw_schedule_free`, `adamw_8bit`) parse and round-trip, `is_supported()` and `OptimizerType::SUPPORTED` say which a trainer runs (the surfaces list `SUPPORTED`), and `init_lora_weights` reads `true`, `pissa` or `loftq`; validation refuses the others with the supported list rather than training something else. `bias` other than `none` is refused the same way, since nothing trained LoRA biases.
- **Job specs for `train-mtp`, `train-draft` and `train-diffusion`** (`TrainMtpSpec`, `TrainDraftSpec`, `TrainDiffusionSpec`, `JobKind::{TrainMtp, TrainDraft, TrainDiffusion}`), so the TUI, GUI and MCP can build the jobs the CLI runs. A CLI test parses each spec's argv back through clap, for every MTP family and quantization choice.
- **`TrainSpec::{lora_config, training_config, distributed_config}` build the configs a spec asks for**, and `LrSchedulerType` and `DistributedCompression` have `as_str`, `Display` and `FromStr`, so a surface no longer maps option strings by hand. A test ties the spec's option lists to the enums.
- **`JobStatus::apply_event_with` completes the status on `JobEvent::Completed`** with the result the surface builds from the summary, and `JsonlSink::take_error` returns the first write error.

- **An offline top-k teacher is used as stored, never densified.** `LogitCache::load_sequence_sparse` returns the kept logits and token ids (`SparseLogits`, collated with `SparseLogits::stack`), `SparseKlLoss` computes `KL(top-k teacher || full student)` from a gather and one log-sum-exp, and `Distiller::compute_loss_sparse` runs the whole distillation step (T² scaling, alpha blend, ignore index, progressive schedule) on it. Until now every cached sequence was expanded back to `[tokens, vocab]` and the loss ran several passes over it, so a 128-entry cache cost 150k floats per token again at train time. The sparse loss equals the dense one on the densified teacher, and its gradient is `(q − p)/(T·N)`; the top-k selection that writes the cache now runs on the device (`argpartition` plus a sort of `k` entries) and its result is checked against a host sort. Only the forward KL (or soft cross-entropy, which has its gradient) has a sparse form; `compute_loss_sparse` refuses any other configured loss instead of substituting one.
  - `cargo bench -p pmetal-distill` is a real benchmark now: it evaluates forward and backward and reports wall time and peak device memory for each loss, dense and sparse. The old criterion benchmark dropped the lazy graph it built and timed graph construction.
- **Offline caches can store Random-Sampling KD draws** (`CompressionMethod::Sampled`; Anshumann et al., ACL 2025). A top-k cache is a biased estimate of the teacher, over-weighting its head; a handful of draws from the teacher's distribution, kept as the distinct tokens with their frequencies, is an unbiased one, and the paper reports about twelve of them matching full distillation. `LogitCompressor` takes the draws on the device (`top_k` is the draw count; `with_sampling_temperature` the temperature), `LogitCache::load_sequence_sampled` returns them as `SparseTargets`, and `Distiller::compute_loss_sampled` trains on them; it refuses a loss temperature other than the one the draws were taken at, because they estimate the teacher at that temperature only. Tests check that the frequencies of 5,000 draws are the teacher's softmax to five standard errors (at two temperatures), that the mean over 400 twelve-draw caches is unbiased, the loss and its gradient `(q − p̂)/(T·N)` against float64, and the distiller against its dense path.
- **MiniLLM's policy-gradient objective (Gu et al., ICLR 2024) is `MiniLlmLoss`.** It takes teacher-forced logits on sampled responses and returns the surrogate whose gradient is the paper's single-step term (the per-position reverse KL summed exactly over the vocabulary) plus the length-normalized long-term term with a clipped importance ratio against the teacher-mixed behavior distribution (`mixed_log_probs` is what responses are sampled from). A float64 evaluation of the same surrogate gives the value and, by central differences, the gradient for several settings; a student equal to the teacher has zero loss and gradient. The rollout that produces the responses is the training loop's.
- **On-policy distillation from a teacher that reports only the log-probability of each sampled token** (`SampledTokenKl`). With tokens sampled from the student, three single-sample estimators of the KL differ in the gradient they carry: `k1 = Δ` is unbiased for the reverse KL but has zero expected gradient, `k2 = ½Δ²` has the reverse KL's, and `k3 = r − 1 − log r` the forward KL's (Schulman, 2020). The loss back-propagates `k2` or `k3` (`SampledTokenKlTarget::{Reverse, Forward}`) and returns `k1` to log. The tests enumerate a five-token vocabulary, so the expected gradients are exact sums over every possible sample, and match `∇KL(q ‖ p)` and `∇KL(p ‖ q)` to 1e-4.
- **DistiLLM's skew KL (Ko et al., ICML 2024) is `SkewKlLoss`**, forward `KL(T || αT + (1−α)S)` and reverse `KL(S || (1−α)T + αS)` (YAML `skew_kl: {alpha, reverse}`; the paper's best α is 0.1). It replaces `MiniLlmLoss { mix }`, which computed exactly the skew reverse KL and credited it to MiniLLM.
- **GKD takes a divergence, masks and a sampler.** `GkdLoss::with_divergence` swaps the forward KL for `JSD(β)`, reverse KL, skew KL or any `DistillLoss`; `compute_full_weighted` masks padding on each pair; `use_student_data(u)` is the paper's per-batch coin and `TemperatureSampler` samples a student's logits at a temperature with an optional top-k (its frequencies match `softmax(z/T)` within five standard errors over 20,000 draws).
- **`LossConfig::attention` does something.** The attention-transfer configuration was parsed and read by nothing; `Distiller::compute_attention_loss` builds the loss from it and returns the weighted mean over the mapped layer pairs times the configured weight, sharing its pair averaging with the hidden-state term. `align_vocab_truncate` cuts teacher and student to their common vocabulary for models that differ only in embedding padding.
- **`consensus` merges with TALL masks, and parameters take layer gradients.** Consensus merging (Wang et al., ICML 2024) keeps only the coordinates that at least `min_tasks` task vectors claim, a task claiming a coordinate where `|τ| ≥ tall_lambda·|τ_MTL − τ|`, which drops both the weights only one task relies on and the ones nobody does; it is `pmetal merge --method consensus`. Any of `weight`, `density`, `t` and `lambda` may now be a list, a gradient whose anchors are spread over the layers (`t: [0, 0.5, 1]`), alone or inside a `filter` entry (`- value: [0, 1]` / `filter: self_attn`); tensors without a layer index fall through to the next entry. A hand-computed six-coordinate TALL example, the interpolation at, between and past the anchors, and an end-to-end SLERP ramp over three layers check them.
- **FP8 (E4M3) checkpoints merge.** The loader rejected `F8_E4M3` tensors, so the FP8 releases of current models could not be merged at all. A weight and its `<name>_scale_inv` (one inverse scale per 128x128 block, or per row, column or tensor) are now one tensor, dequantized on load, and the scale tensors never reach the output. The merge runs in f32 and writes the configured dtype; the output is not re-quantized to FP8. A block-scaled weight and a two-model FP8 merge check it.
- **Merges are reproducible and configurable end to end.** `seed` fixes the DARE and DELLA masks (default 0, each tensor and model slot draws its own stream), `vocab_mismatch: truncate | extend` reconciles models whose embedding or head row counts differ (the output `vocab_size` follows), `max_shard_size` sets the shard size in bytes, and `tokenizer.source` picks `base`, `first` or a directory (`union` is rejected, where it used to be ignored). A model directory's hidden files are ignored when looking for weights, so an AppleDouble `._model.safetensors` stub no longer fails a good checkpoint.
- **`pmetal_runtime::MetalKernel` runs a custom Metal kernel from Rust.** `mlx::core::fast::metal_kernel` was reachable only from C++, with each kernel hard-wired in the runtime's C++ sources. A caller now gives the kernel body, the names of its inputs and outputs, and per call the output shapes and dtypes, the launch grid and threadgroup, and template arguments (integers, or dtypes used as types in the body). The kernel is built on first use and cached by name process-wide, so registering different source under a name already in use is an error rather than a silent run of the old kernel; the result is an ordinary lazy array in the same graph as every other op (it has no gradient, which the type documents). Argument-count mismatches and kernels that do not compile arrive through the usual error channel.
  - The tests run kernels in float32 and float16 with different template values, compose a kernel's output with ordinary ops, and cover the three error paths (a compile failure reports MLX's message)
- **`pmetal-vocoder` trains BigVGAN: `train::GanTrainer`.** It runs the reference training script's recipe: per step a least-squares GAN step on the discriminators with the generator's detached output, then a generator step on the mel loss plus (after `freeze_step` warm-up steps, in which the discriminators neither train nor score) the adversarial and feature matching losses scored by the *updated* discriminators; AdamW (betas 0.8 and 0.99) on both networks with the learning rate multiplied by `lr_decay` per step, global-norm gradient clipping per network (the period and spectral discriminator banks separately, as the reference clips mpd and mrd), and the v1 single-scale or v2 multi-scale mel objective weighted by `lambda_melloss`. `TrainingConfig` deserialises from a release `config.json`. A non-finite gradient is an error instead of a corrupted weight.
  - A checkpoint is one safetensors file with the generator and both discriminator banks in the reference's tensor names and layout, the optimizers' moments and the step counts; resuming at step 3 and training two more steps gives the same losses as the uninterrupted run
  - Released models fine-tune: the base 24 kHz model with its MPD and MRD, and v2 24 kHz with its MPD and CQT discriminator, three steps each at a learning rate of 1e-6 on 8192 samples (mel error 0.172 to 0.166 and 0.95 to 0.84, 0.45 s and 0.75 s per step on an M4 Max under load); a model prepared for inference trains after `prepare_for_training`
  - On tiny models: every tensor of both networks moves on the first step, the warm-up trains only the generator and its loss equals `lambda_mel` times the mel error to 0.1%, the learning rate follows `lr * decay^step`, the generator fits a fixed batch, and the multi-scale objective trains
- **`pmetal-vocoder` has the discriminators BigVGAN-v2 was trained with.** The multi-scale sub-band CQT discriminator (`MultiScaleSubbandCqtDiscriminator`) and the multi-band discriminator (`MultiBandDiscriminator`) join the MPD and MRD, selected from a release `config.json` the way the reference's training script selects them (`BigVGANDiscriminator::new(&DiscriminatorConfig::from_json(json)?)`). They stand on `audio::Cqt` (the constant-Q transform by recursive downsampling, built like nnAudio's `CQT2010v2` and differentiable), `audio::Resampler` (windowed-sinc sample-rate conversion with torchaudio's kernel, usable on its own), and `nn::filter::firwin2`. A checkpoint's derived buffers (CQT kernels, lengths, filters, the resampler's bank, the MBD's STFT window) are compared with the ones computed instead of loaded, so a checkpoint made for other sampling rates fails to load instead of producing nonsense.
  - Logits, every feature map and the gradient of the generator-side loss match the reference modules (1e-4 or better) for the MPD, MRD, MBD and CQT discriminators on random weights; the CQT matches nnAudio with and without early downsampling and with a partial top octave, and the resampler matches torchaudio for 24 to 48 kHz, 44.1 to 48 kHz and 48 to 16 kHz
  - The CQT's octave step is frames times a kernel matrix rather than a strided convolution with 512 to 4096-tap kernels, whose backward pass builds an im2col buffer thousands of times the signal's size: forward and backward through a 9-octave, 48-bins-per-octave CQT of 0.3 s of audio peaked at 6.9 GB and now takes 0.04 GB, the three-scale discriminator went from 12.8 GB to 0.94 GB, and a fine-tune step of the released v2 model (90 mel frames) from a 59 GB process footprint and 1.32 s to 21 GB and 0.65 s (provisional, M4 Max under load)
  - On the released checkpoints (`bigvgan_discriminator_optimizer.pt`) the base model's MPD and MRD agree with the reference to 1.7e-5 and the v2 model's MPD and nine-octave CQT discriminator to 1.2e-5, with every derived buffer in the checkpoint equal to the one computed here
- **`pmetal-vocoder` synthesises as a stream.** `StreamingSynthesizer` takes mel frames as they arrive and returns the audio that is final (everything whose right context has arrived), and `BigVGAN::synthesize_chunked` bounds peak memory by the chunk instead of the recording; both reproduce `forward` on the whole mel to 1e-5, because the generator is convolutional with a receptive field that `receptive_radius_samples` computes and a test proves (no output sample farther than the radius from a perturbed mel frame changes, and the bound is within 4x of the true reach).
- **`pmetal-vocoder` converts waveforms to and from 16-bit PCM** (`audio::to_pcm16`, `from_pcm16`): scaled by 32767 and truncated toward zero as the reference's export does, so a full-scale sample cannot overflow, with out-of-range samples (a v2 model has no `tanh`) clamped instead of wrapped.
- **`pmetal-vocoder` loads the checkpoints NVIDIA releases.** They are PyTorch `.pt` files, which the loader could not read (it looked for a `model.safetensors` that no release ships, and filled in only the input, output and upsampling convolutions). `checkpoint::load_state_dict` reads `.pt` with a restricted unpickler (an allow-list of globals, no code execution) or safetensors, strictly: every tensor must find a parameter and every parameter a tensor. `BigVGAN::from_pretrained` takes a Hugging Face id or a local directory, and `save_safetensors` writes the reference's names and layout back.

- **Array constructors and casts take the typed `Dtype`.** `zeros`, `ones`, `full`, `arange`, `eye`, `tri`, `linspace`, the random samplers, `as_dtype`, `view`, `from_fp8`, `from_u16_bits_slice` and `scalar_with_dtype` accept `impl IntoDtype`, implemented for `compat::Dtype` and for the raw `i32` code, so a call site writes `x.as_dtype(Dtype::Bfloat16)` instead of passing a number whose meaning (`0` is `Bool`, not `Float32`) is easy to get wrong; every call that passes a code compiles as before.

- **`pmetal_runtime::checked(|| ...)` runs any chain of ops and returns the first error one of them raised.** An op that throws hands back a placeholder and the ops after it carry on with it, so by the end of the chain `check_last_error` (the last op's slot) is clean again; `checked` reads the unobserved channel, which holds the first failure until someone reads it, so `x.matmul(&w).add(&b).softmax(-1)` reports its matmul's shape error however many ops follow. Until now only the 35-odd hand-written `try_*` methods did that, one op at a time. An error nobody read before the call is returned too, so none is lost. A test builds a chain whose middle op fails, shows `check_last_error` calling it clean, and `checked` naming the matmul.

- **Loading safetensors says why it failed.** `try_load_safetensors_shard` and `InlineArray::try_load_safetensors` return a `RuntimeResult` whose message names the file and the reason (cannot open, unreadable header, a key that isn't there, MLX's own error plus the per-tensor fallback's); `load_safetensors_shard` and `load_safetensors` keep their `Option` signatures on top of them, so each caller can move over when it wants the reason instead of "failed to load". The fallback also stopped narrowing: an `i64` value that doesn't fit the `i32` it is held in is an error rather than a wrapped number, and a misaligned tensor is named instead of failing the whole shard silently.

- **The runtime exposes MLX's memory limits and describes the GPU, and a generation stream is a guard.** `get_memory_limit` / `set_memory_limit` and `set_cache_limit` (each setter returns the previous value; a cache limit of 0 keeps no free buffers) join the wired-limit and cache queries, and `device_info()` returns the device's name, Metal architecture, RAM, recommended working-set size, largest buffer and resource limit as a typed `DeviceInfo`, read from the device rather than estimated from `hw.memsize`. `GenerationStream::enter()` makes the thread's generation stream the default and, when dropped, waits for it and restores MLX's default stream: the four-call pairing (`new_generation_stream`, `set_generation_stream`, `synchronize`, `reset_default_stream`) that an early return or a panic used to leave unbalanced, with the SIGSEGV at teardown that comes with it. Guards nest, and each thread gets its own stream.
  - The limits round-trip against the previous value, a cache limit of 0 leaves the cache empty after a freed buffer, `device_info` agrees with `get_max_recommended_size` and the machine's RAM, and the guard works from spawned threads and nested.

- **Qwen3-MoE (`model_type: qwen3_moe`, Qwen3-30B-A3B and its family) runs on the native Qwen engine.** It took the generic `DynamicModel` path; the native engine's Qwen3 attention, routed layers, compiled single-token graphs, packed experts and prefill in passes now cover it too. A sparse layer is the routed experts alone (no shared expert), `norm_topk_prob` defaults to false as in transformers, and `decoder_sparse_step` and `mlp_only_layers` pick the dense layers. Its checkpoints load in the fused, one-module-per-expert and `mlx_lm` stacked layouts, quantized or not (the previous entry). Against transformers' `Qwen3MoeForCausalLM` on two small fp32 models (8 experts, 2 per token; one with the top-k weights renormalized, one without them and with a dense layer), over a 40-token prompt, the logits match to the parity tolerance on an uncached prefill, on a cached decode one token at a time, and on prefill in passes of 1 to 40 tokens
- **The native gpt-oss engine runs MLX's `gpt-oss-20b-MXFP4-Q8` conversions.** The engine refused any checkpoint with a `*.scales` tensor, which is how MLX stores them: the experts stacked as `experts.{gate,up,down}_proj.{weight,scales,bias}` in `mxfp4`, and the attention projections, router, embeddings and head 8-bit affine, each module's mode and widths read from the config's `quantization` block. All of it stays packed and runs on MLX's quantized kernels (the compiled single-token graph takes dense matrices, so packed attention decodes through the per-op path). A nested config's `tie_word_embeddings` now reaches the text model too
  - `mlx-community/gpt-oss-20b-MXFP4-Q8` picks transformers' bf16 16 greedy tokens on the harmony prompt, over a cached decode and one uncached forward, and sits closer to transformers' fp32 than transformers' own bf16 does (minimum cosine 0.9925 against 0.9833, max logit difference 5.1 against 7.7)
  - A small fp32 model with the release's random MXFP4 experts re-laid out as MLX stacks them matches transformers to 1e-6 over a prompt, a cached decode and chunked prefill; with the attention, router, embeddings and head also packed it gives the dense twin's logits to 1e-6
- **`pmetal_runtime::distributed::average_gradients` averages a data-parallel step's gradients over an MLX group without leaving the device.** Gradients are packed into buckets of up to 32 MiB (one dtype to a bucket), summed with one lazy `all_sum` per bucket, divided by the group size and split back to their shapes, so a ring or RDMA link carries a few large messages instead of one per parameter, and nothing is read back to the host. A group of one is a no-op. A test packs gradients of two dtypes into several buckets with a stand-in collective for three ranks and checks that every parameter comes back to its shape, dtype and average; a real multi-process check needs a launched group.
- **Muon is available as `pmetal_runtime::compat::optimizers::{Muon, MuonAdamW}`.** Momentum orthogonalized by five Newton–Schulz iterations (Jordan et al., 2024), with `torch.optim.Muon`'s update and defaults (momentum 0.95 with Nesterov, coefficients 3.4445, −4.7750, 2.0315, decoupled decay at the unadjusted rate) and its three learning-rate adjustments, including Moonshot's `0.2·√max(rows, cols)` that gives the update AdamW's RMS (arXiv 2502.16982). Muon holds one buffer per matrix against AdamW's two. It is defined for matrices only, so `Muon` refuses anything else and `MuonAdamW` trains a whole model: hidden matrices with Muon, embeddings, the output head, norms and biases with AdamW. Tests follow two update steps against the reference algorithm written out in f64, the Newton–Schulz iteration on wide, tall and square matrices, the flattening of a spectrum spread over three decades into singular values in [0.5, 1.5], and the routing. It is not yet a `--optimizer` choice.
- **`pmetal_runtime::training::CrossEntropy` takes label smoothing, a z-loss and an ignore index, and reduces to a sum and a count as well as a mean.** Label smoothing `ε` follows `torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss` (the loss against the uniform distribution, weighted `ε`); the z-loss is PaLM's `λ·lse²` (`1e-4` in the paper), which keeps the normalizer near 1 over long bf16 runs. `sum_and_count` gives gradient accumulation what it needs to weight micro-batches by their token counts, since the mean of per-batch means is not the mean over all tokens when the batches differ. `global_grad_norm` returns the clipping norm without clipping. Tests check the loss against its definition in f64 with an ignored token in the batch, and the gradient against `(1 + 2λ·lse)·softmax − (1 − ε)·onehot − ε/V`.
- **gpt-oss runs from the release's MXFP4 checkpoint, packed, on both engines.** `openai/gpt-oss-20b` ships its experts as MXFP4 (`*_blocks` with E8M0 `*_scales`), which neither engine could load: the native one refused it and the `DynamicModel` path dropped every expert tensor. The blocks' bytes are MLX's mxfp4 layout (two E2M1 values a byte, low nibble first, one power-of-two scale per 32), so they load as they are, viewed as `uint32`, and run through `gather_qmm`: 13.8 GB resident instead of about 40 GB unpacked. Both engines read the experts through one type (`pmetal_runtime::gpt_oss_native::GptOssExperts`), which also takes transformers' dense layout and MLX's stacked one, and the `DynamicModel` loader now reads the release's `mlp.router` and fused experts by name and refuses a tensor nothing reads. A loss's gradient passes through the packed experts to the layers below them
  - On the real 20B and a harmony-formatted prompt, both engines pick transformers' 16 greedy tokens, over a cached decode and one uncached forward. In bf16 both are closer to transformers in fp32 (min cosine 0.992, max logit difference 2.8 to 5.8) than transformers in bf16 is (0.983, 7.7)
  - `pmetal infer --chat` reasons in the `analysis` channel and answers "Paris is the capital of France." in the final one. Native decode runs at 82 to 91 tok/s on an M4 Max with the compiled single-token graph on or off, and prefill at 220 to 270 tok/s over 512 tokens
  - A small fp32 model with random MXFP4 experts matches transformers' dequantized forward to 9.5e-7 on both engines; reading the gate's scales from the up rows fails it by 1.3
- **`pmetal serve` returns a thinking model's reasoning apart from its answer.** Chat completions carry it in `message.reasoning_content`, and when streaming in `delta.reasoning_content` chunks ahead of the `delta.content` ones; `/v1/messages` answers with a `thinking` block (streamed as `thinking_delta`s) ahead of the `text` block. `content` is the answer only, its logprobs are the answer's, and tool calls are parsed from the answer. The split is by token, from the markers the tokenizer has: Qwen3-family and DeepSeek-R1 `<think>…</think>`, Magistral's `[THINK]`, Gemma 4's thought channel and gpt-oss's `analysis` channel. The prompt is read first, so a reply to a chat template that opens the `<think>` block itself (Qwen3.5 with thinking on) starts in the reasoning. `pmetal infer`'s streamed `[thinking]` and `[answer]` labels come from the same splitter (`pmetal_data::stream_format::ReasoningSplitter`). Qwen3.5-0.8B answering "17 + 25" over every endpoint, streamed and not, gives the same reasoning and "42" as the engine's own tokens split
- **`pmetal grpo --loss-type gspo` trains with GSPO** (*Group Sequence Policy Optimization*, arXiv 2507.18071): one importance ratio per completion, the length-normalized sequence likelihood ratio exp(mean over its tokens of log π − log π_old), clipped in [1 − 3e-4, 1 + 4e-4] as in the paper's section 5.1 and averaged over completions (`GrpoConfig::for_gspo`, `ImportanceSampling::Sequence`). A test checks the ratio against the sequences' log-likelihoods worked by hand, with masked prompt tokens left out. Like any clipped ratio it only acts once the policy has moved, so it goes with `--num-iterations 2` or more, and the command says so when it doesn't
- **`pmetal preference` trains a LoRA adapter on preference data** (alias `pmetal dpo`; `PreferenceTrainer` and `KtoTrainer` in `pmetal_trainer::preference`; the `preference` MCP tool, a TUI tab and a GUI page). `--loss` picks DPO, IPO, hinge (SLiC), SimPO or ORPO on prompt/chosen/rejected pairs, or KTO on completions labelled good or bad; `--label-smoothing` turns DPO into Robust DPO for noisy labels. Data loads from JSONL, JSON, Parquet or a Hugging Face dataset ID, as plain strings or chat messages, and goes through the model's chat template as `pmetal train` renders it. Preference training had been library-only, and SimPO, ORPO and KTO sat behind a feature nothing enabled
  - The reference model costs no memory: a fresh adapter starts at zero, so the model before training is the reference, and every example is scored once up front. On a small f32 model the first DPO step's loss is ln 2 to 1e-4, which the tests check
  - Each loss matches its paper and reference implementation on hand-computed examples (TRL's `DPOTrainer` for DPO, Robust DPO, IPO and hinge, including IPO's length-averaged log-probs; the SimPO authors' trainer; TRL's `ORPOTrainer` and `KTOTrainer`). KTO subtracts the KL estimate the paper uses, from mismatched completions in the batch, clamped at zero and kept out of the gradient. Defaults follow the same sources: β 0.1 (2.5 for SimPO, with γ/β 0.5) and a LoRA learning rate of 1e-5
  - Steps accumulate gradients over micro-batches, clip them, follow a warmup-then-cosine schedule and log the loss, both rewards, their margin and the share of pairs ranked correctly
  - Qwen3-0.6B on 48 arithmetic prompts, 12 steps of 4 pairs at lr 1e-4: the reward margin goes from about 0 to 13.2 (DPO), 7.6 (hinge), 0.44 (IPO), 15.6 (SimPO), 0.40 (ORPO) and 14.3 (KTO), with every pair in the batch ranked correctly by step 4 for the paired losses, at 500 to 1,600 tokens/s on an M4 Max. `pmetal infer --lora` loads the saved adapter
- **A new ANE inference engine behind `infer --ane` and `serve --ane`** (`pmetal_metal::ane::lm::AneLm`). The whole model runs on the ANE: several layers per ANE program, each taking a batch of new tokens at once against a KV cache that stays in IOSurfaces between calls, with int8 weights by default (fp16 optional) and the LM head split into pieces sized to the model width. The engine it replaces ran one layer per kernel and kept part of each step on the GPU
  - Each step also checks guesses taken from the prompt (prompt lookup). Checking a few extra tokens costs about the same as computing one, so accepted guesses are free tokens, and the output is identical with or without them
  - Qwen3-4B on an M4 Max: 24.5 tok/s plain, 37.6 tok/s with prompt lookup on a repetitive prompt, about 7 s to load once the system has cached the compiled programs (with the checkpoint in the page cache or on an internal SSD). Attention runs one softmax over the cache's and the new tokens' scores, and a pass carries 16 tokens: attention's cost grows with both, and over a 4096-slot cache it had been 60% of a layer. Greedy output matches the bf16 reference implementation on Qwen3-0.6B (fp16) and on a Qwen3-4B chat prompt (int8 and fp16), and int8 perplexity on Qwen3-4B matches bf16 (NLL 2.287 vs 2.294)
  - **DFlash drafting for the ANE engine**: `infer --ane --draft-model z-lab/Qwen3-4B-DFlash-b16` runs the DFlash draft model on the GPU, reading the hidden states the ANE programs output after the layers it was trained on, and the ANE verifies its 15 guesses in one pass. Output is identical to decoding without it. Qwen3-4B goes from 24 tok/s to 59-137 tok/s (3.1-7.1 tokens per pass on three chat prompts). The draft model runs with 8-bit weights, which guess as well as bf16 in half the memory and draft faster. `serve --ane --draft-model` does the same for every request. `--draft-model` still takes a Gemma 4 MTP assistant everywhere else; a DFlash draft model without `--ane` is refused with a pointer to `pmetal dflash`
  - Output streams token by token in `infer`, and the timing line separates loading from generation
  - Qwen3 only for now; `infer --ane` says why it's using the GPU for anything else
- **Decision models: Clef and Clef-flash answer typed questions about a state in one forward pass** (`pmetal decide`, `pmetal serve` on `POST /v1/systemone`, the `decide` MCP tool, `pmetal_models::decision`). A request names a state (text or any JSON value) and questions that are propositions, named choices or ordered scores. The joint schema head reads the backbone's final hidden states and scores every option of every question at once, with no generation. Records are encoded token for token as the release's reference code encodes them, Python's JSON rendering included, and malformed requests get its error messages. Requests may carry images and videos, read by the backbone's vision tower (see the Qwen3.5-family vision entry below)
  - Checked against the release's PyTorch code: the head matches to 5.7e-6 on logits in f32, 14 records encode to the same tokens and spans, and Clef-flash's probabilities on five requests are within 4.2e-3 of PyTorch in float32, with every choice the same (PyTorch's own bf16 run is 2.8e-3 away)
  - Clef-flash answers a 260 to 480 token request in 330 to 590 ms on an M4 Max, on the native Qwen3.5 engine
  - The `decide` MCP tool takes a `port` to ask a decision model already served there (`start_serve`), as `chat` does, rather than loading the model for every call. Without one it loads `model` once for the call, as before. A request the server refuses comes back as an invalid-params error with the server's message
- **`Linear` can hold its weight packed for MLX's quantized matmul** (`Linear::quantize`, `LinearQuant`), as `mlx.nn.QuantizedLinear` does, with the packed weight's `scales` and `biases` beside it in the parameter tree. A packed layer doesn't train, but a LoRA adapter on it does and computes what it would on the unpacked weight, which is QLoRA; merging the adapter unpacks the layer
- **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (`model_type: qwen4_exp`) runs**, text only: the hybrid of Gated DeltaNet and full attention behind a sparse-attention indexer, with four hyper-connection streams, a 512-expert MoE and 51B parameters of hashed n-gram embeddings. `infer`, `serve` and `--experts-dir` drive it like any hybrid model. The vision tower and the MTP head are not loaded yet
  - It matches transformers to 2.1e-6 on a small fp32 model with every feature on, over the whole prompt and through the caches one token at a time
  - The n-gram table is read row by row from the checkpoint rather than loaded (a token reads 16 rows of it), and the NVFP4 release's experts stay packed, 68 GB rather than 241 GB unpacked. That puts the NVFP4 release at about 78 GB resident, which fits a 128 GB Mac
  - The bf16, FP8 and NVFP4 releases' layouts are checked against the loader from their safetensors headers: every tensor is loaded or skipped by name
- **Qwen3.8 (`Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B` and its FP8 release) is checked end to end against transformers.** Greedy replies match transformers token for token with thinking on and off, the first token's top five logits agree to bf16 rounding, `--mtp` accepts 61% of its drafts with unchanged output, and the FP8 release loads with its weights kept in 8 bits. Its chat template, reasoning-effort system prompt included, renders exactly as transformers renders it
  - Both engines read a Qwen3.5-family `config.json` one way now, the way transformers resolves it, and refuse what they can't run by name (an unknown `output_gate_type` or `layer_types` entry, `attn_output_gate: false`, a non-default RoPE type) instead of running it with a default
  - `output_gate_type: "sigmoid"` is supported on both engines; `"swish"`, what the Qwen3.6 and 3.8 releases ship, is SiLU
  - A new parity suite checks both engines against transformers on two small fp32 models covering these keys, over a whole prompt and one cached token at a time, along with the MTP head: every check lands within 1.2e-5
- **Qwen3.5-family models see images and videos** (Qwen3.5, 3.6 and 3.8, and the Clef decision models built on them). The vision tower, the image and video preprocessing, the 3-D positions a prompt with media gets and the merge of vision features into the prompt are ported from transformers, on both text engines, prefill and cached decode
  - `pmetal infer --image <path>` (repeatable) on the native engine. Qwen3.5-0.8B prints the same sentence as transformers' greedy generate for a generated picture, and Qwen3.8-27B describes it correctly in bf16
  - `pmetal infer --video <frames dir>` (repeatable, after the images) with `--video-fps` for the rate the frames were extracted at (24 when not given, as the reference assumes, with a warning, since frames extracted at the model's own 2 per second would then mostly be dropped). The directory's image files are the frames, in natural file-name order; they are sampled and time-stamped by the same code as `decide`'s videos, and only the sampled frames are decoded. Video files are not decoded, so extract the frames first. Qwen3.5-0.8B says a red ball in 16 generated frames moves from left to right, and from right to left when the frames are reversed
  - `/v1/systemone` and `pmetal decide` take a record's `images` (base64, data URIs, and file paths in `decide` only) and `videos` (a list of frames, or `{"frames": [...], "fps": N}`), placed in the prompt as the release's reference code places them. Clef-flash with one image, two images and a six-frame clip lands within 9.2e-3 of the reference's probabilities in float32, every choice the same; the reference's own bf16 run is 1.23e-2 away
  - Preprocessing matches the processor transformers loads by default byte for byte: its torchvision resize rounds its taps differently from Pillow and moves up to 1% of output bytes by one or two levels, so `pillow_resample` gains that variant too. Pixel values, grids, sampled video frames, timestamps and prompt tokens are equal to the reference's
  - On a small fp32 model with an image and a three-frame video, vision features match transformers to 8.1e-6, positions exactly, and logits to 6.8e-6 over the prompt and six decoded tokens, where decoding resumes 16 positions short of the prompt's length
  - `pmetal serve` takes them in chat requests (next entry)
- **`pmetal serve` answers chat requests that carry images and videos**, images and videos for the Qwen3.5-family vision models and images for Llama 3.2 Vision. `/v1/chat/completions` takes the OpenAI content parts, an image as an `image_url` part holding a `data:image/...;base64,` URI, plus a `video` part holding a video's frames; `/v1/messages` takes base64 `image` blocks. Both stream or not. Each image goes to the model's own chat template as an item of its message, so its placeholder lands where the reference processor puts it, and the checkpoint's processor expands it to its tokens. The vision tower loads with the first request that needs it
  - Qwen3.5-0.8B answers a picture and a question with the same 96 tokens as `pmetal infer --image`, token for token, from the same prompt tokens, even though the server decodes on the other engine; the text is the same over both endpoints, streamed or not, with continuous batching on
  - Llama 3.2 11B Vision (bf16) answers a picture and a question with transformers' prompt tokens and its greedy reply, token for token to the end of the turn. Its chat template now renders as transformers renders every template, with `trim_blocks` and `lstrip_blocks`, which removes a stray newline after `<|begin_of_text|>` from every Llama 3.2 Vision prompt; and its vision tower and text model now run a bf16 checkpoint in bf16, where f32 pixels had promoted both to f32
  - On a small fp32 model, the server's cached decode after an image or a two-frame video matches recomputing the whole sequence at every step to 1e-4 in each token's log-probability; decoding one position off fails that check
  - A prompt with media runs on the single-request path and never touches the prefix cache, since both key a prompt by token ids alone and an image's tokens are the same placeholder id whatever it shows; a dense model like Llama 3.2 Vision would otherwise have served one image's cached prefix for another
  - Remote URLs, file paths and uploaded file ids are refused with a 400 that says so (the server fetches and reads nothing on a client's behalf), as are video files, audio and `mm_processor_kwargs`. A model that can't read images answers with a 400 naming it instead of dropping them; Gemma 4 is among these for now. `detail` is accepted and has no effect, since the checkpoint's processor sizes every image
  - The body limit is 64 MiB and now holds: axum's own 2 MiB cap on JSON bodies sat inside the outer limit and refused any request carrying a photo, on `/v1/systemone` too. A 13 MB base64 photo goes through
  - Message content may also be a list of text parts, or null for an assistant turn that only calls tools, on every model
- **`pmetal dflash` runs DFlash 2 drafts**, such as `z-lab/Qwen3.8-27B-DFlash2` for Qwen3.8-27B on the native Qwen3.5 engine. A DFlash 2 draft wraps the attention and the MLP of each of its sliding-window layers in two-tap dynamic convolutions, and guesses with a candidate selector that walks one path through each position's 16 likeliest tokens rather than taking each position's argmax. The checkpoint's declared architecture says which generation it is, and the loader refuses a checkpoint with a tensor it has no place for or a parameter it leaves unfilled. Python's `DFlashGenerator` loads them too
  - Checked against the reference implementation's PyTorch model on a small fp32 draft, over five drafts in a row whose context outgrows the window: hidden states and logits agree to 1.4e-6 and 4.3e-6 and the selector picks the same tokens. Ten deliberate faults in the convolutions, the selector, the window, causality and the RoPE base each fail the check
  - Qwen3.8-27B in bf16 on an M4 Max, four chat prompts with thinking off: 7.61, 4.40, 2.60 and 3.15 tokens per verify step on Fibonacci code, a C hash map, a short story and an explanation for a child, at 53.4, 30.2, 17.6 and 20.9 tok/s against 9.1 tok/s for `pmetal infer` (5.9x, 3.3x, 1.9x, 2.3x). The reference implementation's decoder takes 7.61, 4.47, 2.58 and 3.19 on the same prompt tokens, and accepts exactly as many tokens as pmetal at every step until the two targets round a near tie differently: on the Fibonacci prompt that is every step, with the same tokens
  - The output is greedy decoding's: the same tokens on the Fibonacci prompt and on a 2,753-token prompt that runs past the draft's window, and on the other three the same until a token where the target's two likeliest logits are within two bf16 steps of each other (one is an exact tie), which a block-shaped forward and a one-token forward can round either way
  - `--compare-greedy` also decodes the prompt one token at a time with the same target, and reports the speedup, whether the tokens match, and where they don't, the target's two likeliest tokens there
  - First-generation drafts now read `block_size` from `dflash_config` first and honour `layer_types`, `sliding_window` and `is_causal`, as the reference does. `z-lab/Qwen3-4B-DFlash-b16` decodes the same tokens as before
- **`pmetal upload <PATH> <OWNER/NAME>` publishes a model directory to the Hugging Face Hub**: a fused model, an adapter, a quantized export. It creates the repository if needed (`--private`), commits the directory once (`--revision`, `-m`, `--create-pr`), and skips `.git`, `.DS_Store`, AppleDouble `._*` files and anything matching `--exclude`. It authenticates with `HF_TOKEN` or the token `hf auth login` stored
  - `pmetal_hub::upload_model` now runs on `hf-hub`'s upload support, which asks the Hub how each file must be stored and streams large ones to Xet storage. The hand-written client it replaces, which nothing called, sent every file under 10 MiB inline (a LoRA adapter's weights included), read large files whole into memory, and had no Xet path. It takes `UploadOptions` and an optional token and returns the repository and commit URLs
  - A fake Hub on loopback checks the requests an upload makes: the repository settings, the token, and which files are offered and committed, byte for byte
- **Granite 4.0 and 4.0-H run, and so do the Granite 3.x MoE models** (`model_type` `granitemoehybrid`, `granitemoe` and `granitemoeshared`, on the `Granite` architecture beside plain `granite`). The 4.0-H models interleave Mamba-2 layers with attention layers that carry no positional encoding, and the H Tiny and H Small releases add 64 and 72 routed experts beside a shared MLP; the 4.0 models without the H are attention in every layer, with RoPE. The Mamba-2 layers keep their conv and SSM state in the caller's `MambaCache`, so `infer`, `serve`, LoRA training and continuous batching (each slot with its own state) work as they do for the other hybrids
  - Five small fp32 models, one per combination a release uses (hybrid with experts, hybrid without, all attention with RoPE, `granitemoe`, `granitemoeshared`), match transformers to 7.2e-7 on every layer's output and 1.8e-7 on logits, over the whole prompt and through the caches one token at a time, with the Mamba scan crossing chunk boundaries. Eight deliberate faults (a per-group gated norm, no skip term, state dropped between chunks or between calls, RoPE on a NoPE model, no shared MLP, router weights not renormalized, gate and up swapped) each fail the check
  - `ibm-granite/granite-4.0-h-350m` in fp32 gives the first token's top five logits within 1e-5 of transformers', in the same order, on a plain and a chat prompt, and its final hidden states over 640 tokens agree to 2.6e-6 relative. In bf16 its greedy chat replies are transformers' word for word
  - Every released `granite`, `granitemoe` and `granitemoehybrid` checkpoint in the 3.x, 4.0, 4.1 and 4.2 lines, and the MLX conversions of 4.0-H, puts every tensor its safetensors headers list in place, checked from the headers alone. Both layouts load: the released one, and the MLX conversion's split experts, renamed dense MLP and `[C, K, 1]` conv
- **Qwen3.5, 3.6 and 3.8 run static YaRN** (`rope_parameters.rope_type: "yarn"` in `config.json`, or a legacy `rope_scaling`), the long-context setting their cards give for inputs past 262,144 tokens, on both engines and the bundled MTP head, prefill, cached decode, tree verification and prompts with media. The frequencies are transformers' `_compute_yarn_parameters` (defaults filled in, `attention_factor` from the factor or the `mscale` pair), and the attention factor scales only the rotated quarter of each head, as transformers' `cos` and `sin` carry it. The context window the output budget is bounded by becomes `original_max_position_embeddings * factor`. The CPU hybrid engine refuses it by name
  - A third fixture of the Qwen3.5 parity suite carries the card's YaRN block shrunk to an original length of 16, with the prompt running to 70: both engines and the MTP head match transformers within the suite's 5e-5 over the prefill and every cached step. Dropping the attention factor, applying it to every channel, or keeping the unscaled frequencies each fails it
- **Dense Qwen3 runs YaRN on the native engine** (`pmetal infer`, `serve`), from the `rope_scaling` block the Qwen3 card gives for contexts past 32,768 tokens (`factor` 4) or a `rope_parameters` one. It had run every Qwen3 checkpoint with plain RoPE whatever its config said, which for a YaRN config such as DeepSeek-R1-0528-Qwen3-8B's is wrong at every position, not only past the original window. A `rope_type` the engine does not know is refused by name
  - A small fp32 Qwen3 with the card's block shrunk to an original length of 64 matches transformers' `Qwen3ForCausalLM` to 2.8e-6 on the logits over a 96-token prompt and on every step of a cached decode that starts inside the window and crosses it
- **Chat template keyword arguments reach the template, as transformers' `apply_chat_template(messages, **kwargs)` passes them.** Qwen3.8's `reasoning_effort` (`xhigh`, `medium`, `low`) and `preserve_thinking`, gpt-oss's `reasoning_effort` (`low`, `medium`, `high`) and `model_identity`, and anything else a template reads. `pmetal infer` takes `--reasoning-effort`, `--no-preserve-thinking` and `--chat-template-kwargs '<json>'`, as do the infer job spec, the GUI, the MCP `generate` tool and the Python `infer()`. `/v1/chat/completions` takes OpenAI's top-level `reasoning_effort` and a `chat_template_kwargs` object, streaming or not, and `/v1/messages` maps `thinking` to `enable_thinking`. A level the model refuses is an error (a 400 on the server), and on the CLI so is a control the model's template doesn't have
  - An assistant turn's thinking travels as `reasoning_content` (on `/v1/chat/completions` messages too), which is what templates that render thinking themselves read. A `<think>…</think>` left at the head of `content`, as a thinking model generates it, is split off the way Qwen3's own template splits it, for those templates only
  - 28 renders of Qwen3.8's and gpt-oss's templates over a multi-turn history with thinking, tool calls and tool schemas, at every reasoning effort with preserved thinking on and off, match transformers 5.19 byte for byte, and the level Qwen3.8 refuses is refused
- **Ring links between ranks are authenticated and encrypted with a cluster secret.** Anything on the LAN could join a ring and feed numbers into every gradient average: the only credential was a "namespace key", the SHA3-256 of the version string, sent in clear after a fixed magic. The version string is public, so a peer that had never seen the cluster computed the key, was admitted as the previous rank, and nothing protected the traffic afterwards. Every link now runs a Noise `NNpsk0` handshake (X25519, AES-GCM, SHA-256) keyed by a 256-bit `ClusterSecret` that never crosses the wire, then carries AEAD frames whose counters must advance by exactly one, so tampering, replay, reordering, dropping and truncation are detected, and ephemeral keys per connection mean a secret that leaks later does not expose earlier traffic. The handshake also exchanges channel, world size, rank and a digest of the ring membership, so two machines configured with different node lists refuse each other and say why. `pmetal cluster keygen` creates the secret (`~/.pmetal/cluster_secret`, mode 600; or `PMETAL_CLUSTER_SECRET`), and auto-discovery admits a peer to the topology only once its identify message carries a keyed proof of the secret bound to its peer id. The pipeline harness's result channel, which accepted any TCP connection, goes through the same handshake.
  - Over loopback, an encrypted two-rank all-reduce of 128 MiB moves 1.8 GB/s per rank against 3.1 GB/s in plaintext (3 ranks 1.6 against 4.2, 4 ranks 1.9 against 3.7; provisional, the machine was shared). The default AES provider ran in software at 0.05 GB/s; `snow`'s ring-accelerated provider is 36 times faster.
  - A raw-socket attacker presenting the old namespace key is no longer admitted (it was, before); a proxy that flips a bit, replays, reorders, drops or truncates frames gets an error on the next read; sizes at every frame boundary, a wrong secret, garbage connections, and membership, rank and channel mismatches all behave as described.
- **The ring has `reduce_scatter`, `all_gather`, variable-size `all_gather_bytes` and `broadcast`, and `all_reduce_f32` takes a typed slice.** They share one implementation (`collective::RingCollective`) with the all-reduce and the barrier; every collective announces itself in a header, so ranks that disagree about its kind, size or order fail with an error saying so. A step is sent as 8 MiB segments, reduced as they arrive. The sum order of each chunk is fixed by the ring, so repeated runs and all ranks produce the same bits; the tests demand bit-for-bit equality with a sequential reference for 1 to 5 ranks and lengths from 0 up.
- **ZeRO stages 1 and 2 shard the optimizer for real** (`ZeroLayout`, `ZeroOptimizer`, `ShardedAdamW`, behind the `zero` feature). The flat parameter vector is cut into equal shards, a step is reduce-scatter, a local update of the owned shard by an optimizer holding state for that shard alone, and all-gather: one all-reduce's traffic with optimizer state and update work divided by the world size, optionally with global-norm clipping every rank derives identically. Five AdamW steps on 1 to 4 ranks give parameters bit-identical on every rank to a single device updating with the mean gradient.
- **DiLoCo low-communication training** (`diloco::DiLoCo`, behind the `diloco` feature): workers take H inner steps alone, then average their pseudo-gradients once and step the global parameters with an outer SGD with Nesterov momentum (Douillard et al., 2023: lr 0.7, momentum 0.9). Traffic falls by H; the pseudo-gradient can be sent compressed. Four rounds on 2 and 3 workers match the outer optimizer written out bit for bit.
- **`pipeline_training::simulate` runs pipeline schedules against each other on a model clock** and reports a deadlock (which stage waits at which action) or the timeline, from which the bubble, micro-batches in flight and weight halves owed follow. 1F1B's measured bubble is exactly (P-1)/(M+P-1) with a stage holding at most P minus its index micro-batches.
- **`layer_assignment::assign_layers` partitions layers exactly within each node's memory, and `best_ring_order` arranges nodes by link speed.** The solver is a dynamic programme over contiguous stages (the embedding and head counted against the first and last node, node speeds weighted) and fails with the reason when the model does not fit; on 200 random instances it agrees with exhaustive search on feasibility and on the optimum. `best_ring_order` picks the cyclic order whose slowest link is fastest, exhaustively to 10 nodes.

### Changed

- **Every assistant turn of a conversation trains by default, and plain-text completions end in EOS.** The loss covered the last assistant turn only (earlier ones were masked as prompt); without a chat template it masked everything up to the last user message and trained the `Assistant: ` header. Without a template an assistant's words and the blank line after them train, the header does not; with one, each assistant turn is found by rendering the conversation up to it (a turn the template renders differently in context is left out, with a warning, instead of trained on a guess). `AssistantLoss::LastTurn` keeps the old scope. Documents, Alpaca/prompt-completion/reasoning completions and templateless chats now end in one EOS (unless the tokenizer already appends it), so a model fine-tuned on them learns to stop. Text a chat template rendered is encoded without the tokenizer's special tokens, so a BOS the template writes is no longer doubled.
- **Format detection prefers the structured layout.** `messages`, `conversations`, `instruction` with an answer, `problem`/`thinking`, then a prompt column with a completion column, and `text`/`content` last: `tatsu-lab/alpaca` ships a pre-rendered `text` column beside `instruction`/`input`/`output` and was trained whole, prompts included. The reasoning and custom-column formats put the separator after the prompt in the prompt (it is what the model is shown), and a `text` column named with a `prompt` column is custom-column loading, as asked.
- **A sample whose trained tokens were all truncated away is dropped with a warning**, instead of reaching the loss as a row of `-100`s; `train_val_split` clamps its ratio instead of underflowing, and `shuffle` no longer clones every sample.
- **Packed batches carry labels for every sample and pad to `pad_to_multiple`; a sequence longer than a row is truncated, not dropped.** A batch of samples without labels fell back to the input ids shifted by one, which the loss then shifted again (training on `t+2`, and letting a document's last token predict the next document's first). Samples without labels train on every token. `PackedBatch.labels` is no longer optional and `PackedBatch` has a `padding` count; `PackedTrainingBatch::attention_mask` is built on the device from per-token block ids, not an `n x n` host matrix, and the two mask builders no longer duplicate each other.
- **`resolve_dataset_path_pub` returns the directory when a dataset is several shards** (the file when it is one), which the loaders read whole. The order a seed gives an epoch, and `TrainingDataset::shuffle`, changed with the stable generator.

- **`MetalContext` asks the device what it is.** The GPU family comes from `supportsFamily`, the generation from the Metal architecture name (`architecture_gen` is now the number in `applegpu_g16s`: 13 for M1 through 17 for M5, where M3 and M4 used to share one value), the GPU core count from the IO registry (a 32-core M4 Max reported 40), and `has_nax` follows the gate the runtime applies to the same hardware (generation 17 on macOS 26.2 or later). Peak memory bandwidth is a table of Apple's published figures keyed by chip, tier and core count, tested chip by chip; the old table had the M3 Pro at 273 GB/s (150), the M5 at 120 (153.6) and gave every binned Max part the full chip's figure. The die count no longer spawns `sysctl`.
  - The bandwidth the context reports is still a measured GPU copy, now timed by the GPU's own clock and accepted only within reach of the chip's peak. A failed or implausible probe used to write its spec-table fallback into `~/.cache/pmetal/context/memory_bandwidth.json` as if it were a measurement, and every later start read it back without measuring again; only measurements are cached now, under a versioned key that carries the core count.
- **The autotuner's results are scoped to the device and the kernels, and chosen reproducibly.** Cached results live under `~/.cache/pmetal/tuna/<scope>/`, where the scope names the chip (with its core count) and a digest of the compiled shader library, so a different machine or a rebuilt kernel starts from an empty cache; scopes unused for 30 days are removed. Each candidate is the median of five timed runs, and a challenger must beat the heuristic by 3 percent to replace it, so noise cannot flip a choice between runs. Concurrent processes merge their results instead of overwriting each other's. `Tuner::with_persistent_cache` is replaced by `Tuner::for_device` and `Tuner::with_cache_dir`.
- **`pmetal_metal::bridge` is `pmetal_metal::view`.** It only wraps memory something else owns (a runtime array) as a Metal buffer; the runtime is the bridge to MLX. The types and the crate-root re-exports are unchanged.
- **Expert buffers are rounded to the page size, not 2 MiB, and cannot shrink their pool.** A buffer a caller drops instead of releasing returns to its pool, `pread` retries interrupted calls and continues after short reads, and new buffers are zero-filled. `ALIGN_2MB` is replaced by `page_size()`.
- **Shader builds are parallel and incremental.** A script run that rebuilds every shader went from 7.9 s to 5.8 s on a busy M4 Max, and a run with nothing to rebuild skips the compiler.
- **`ModelConfig` and `DatasetConfig` hold only the keys a trainer reads.** `ModelConfig` is `model_id` and `use_flash_attention`; `DatasetConfig` is `dataset_id` and `shuffle`. `dtype`, `quantization`, `device`, `max_seq_len`, `trust_remote_code`, `revision` and `hf_token` (a plain `String` that a derived `Debug` would have printed) left `ModelConfig`, and `split`, `text_column`, `max_samples` and `seed` left `DatasetConfig`: no code path ever read them, so a config setting one was asking for something that did not happen. `TrainingConfig::cosine_num_restarts` is documented as the number of cosine cycles (1 is a single cosine), as transformers' `num_cycles`.
- **`pmetal-distill`'s loss API.** `DistillLoss` has one required method, `weighted_loss`, whose teacher argument is already detached (the public `compute`, `compute_weighted` and `compute_masked` stop its gradient first), and a provided `temperature_scale` (`T²` by default; logit MSE and hidden-state losses return 1). `TaidConfig` carries the paper's `t_start`, `t_end`, `alpha`, `beta`, `epsilon` and `TaidDistiller` is stateful (`observe`); `LossType::MiniLlm` is `LossType::SkewKl`; `MiniLlmLoss` is the policy-gradient loss, not a `DistillLoss`. A top-k cache is written as `CompressedLogits::TopKSparse` and a 4-bit one as `Int4PerToken`; caches written before still load.
- **`normalize` applies to the sparsifying merge methods.** DARE, DELLA and Breadcrumbs documented `normalize` (default true) and ignored it, summing the task vectors instead; they now divide by the total weight, and TIES's disjoint mean divides by the agreeing weights, as its paper does. Task arithmetic keeps the paper's sum unless `normalize: true` is set. Merges with several models and the default weights therefore move the base by the mean of the task vectors, not their sum: set `lambda` to the number of models to get the old result.
- **`pmetal fuse` folds adapters with the streaming merge of `pmetal-merge`.** The command had its own full-model fold that applied `alpha / r` to every layer and ignored rsLoRA and DoRA; it now runs the memory-mapped, f64 fold the `--accurate` flag selected before, keeps the base's shard layout and sidecars, and takes `--alpha` and `--rank` as overrides of the adapter's own scale.

- **BigVGAN synthesis is 2 to 4 times faster.** Profiling a second of 24 kHz audio on BigVGAN-v2 showed the convolutions at 21 ms and the anti-aliased activations (upsample, Snake, low-pass, decimate) at 44 ms of a 65 ms forward: about twenty kernel launches per call on tensors of at most 2 MB, 108 calls per forward, so the GPU was launching rather than computing. `BigVGAN::prepare_for_inference` (which `from_dir` and `from_pretrained` call) folds weight normalisation into the weights and runs each activation as one fused Metal kernel; a threadgroup computes the activated interpolated signal its low-pass window needs once, into threadgroup memory, because the `sin` is the expensive part. The kernel has no gradient, so training uses the differentiable op path (`prepare_for_training` switches back).
  - A single activation call on a 2 MB tensor drops from 0.45 to 0.07 ms. On an M4 Max, best of three with other work loading the machine (provisional), random weights: v2 24 kHz, 1 s of audio 68 to 34 ms in float32 and 29 ms in float16; 5 s 469 to 168 ms (133 ms in float16); base 24 kHz, 1 s 48 to 14 ms (11 ms in float16)
  - The kernel handles any resampling ratio and kernel size, both activations, float32 and half precision (the activations always compute in float32); it is checked against the op path over ratios 1 to 4, odd kernels, log and linear scale, 1 to 96 channels and lengths 1 to 100 (2e-5), against the reference activation, and through the whole generator. On the released v2 checkpoint float32 still matches the reference to 1.9e-5 (SNR 106 dB); float16 gives SNR 43 dB and bfloat16 26 dB, so float16 is the half-precision choice. The convolutions, now most of the time, run at about 9 TFLOPS (an im2col matmul is no faster)
- **`pmetal-vocoder`'s layers are channels-last, and its API follows the reference.** `nn` layers take `[batch, time, channels]` (MLX's layout); `BigVGAN::forward` keeps the reference's `[batch, mels, frames]` to `[batch, 1, samples]`, and `forward_nlc` skips the transposes. `WeightNormConv1d` and `WeightNormConvTranspose1d` are `Conv1d` and `ConvTranspose1d` (weight normalisation is now a property, `NormedWeight`, that `fuse_weight_norm` removes and `normalize` restores), `Conv2d` is new, `Activation1d` is no longer generic over the activation, `AMPBlock` and `AMPBlockSnake` are one `AMPBlock` with the reference's two layouts, and every module implements `ModuleParameters`. Discriminators return `[batch, n]` logits and channels-last feature maps; the generator-loss function takes discriminator banks and a precomputed mel loss; `hann_window` takes `periodic`; the preset constructors match the released models (`v2_44khz_128band_512x`, `base_24khz_100band`, and so on) and `BigVGANConfig::validate` checks that the upsample rates multiply to the hop size.
- **`pmetal-bridge` is now `pmetal-runtime`.** The crate holds pmetal's array type, its ops, compiled graphs, the native inference engines, the decode sampler, the optimizers, the TurboQuant KV cache and pmetal's own Metal kernels: the tensor runtime every other crate computes on, with MLX as the backend under it. Depend on `pmetal-runtime` and import `pmetal_runtime`. Renamed with it: `BridgeError` and `BridgeResult` are `RuntimeError` and `RuntimeResult`, `expect_bridge_errors` is `expect_runtime_errors`, the build metadata is `DEP_PMETAL_RUNTIME_*`, and errors are logged as `[pmetal-runtime]`. The `PMETAL_RUNTIME_LOG_ERRORS` environment variable replaces `PMETAL_BRIDGE_LOG_ERRORS`, which is still read when the new one is unset
- **`pmetal-mhc`'s API is fallible, reproducible and owns no locks.** `MhcLayer` constructors and every method that takes arrays return `Result<_, MhcError>` (a new type that also carries `MhcConfigError`) instead of panicking on a bad configuration or shape; `MhcLayer` owns its parameters rather than holding them behind an `Arc<RwLock>`, so `backward` returns the parameter gradients (`MhcGradients`) for the caller to accumulate and `apply_gradients_sgd` takes `&mut self`, and the `parking_lot` dependency is gone; `MhcParams::with_rng` and `MhcLayer::with_rng` initialize from a caller's generator, where `new` drew from the thread's. The three projections `φ_pre`, `φ_post` and `φ_res` are one `phi` of shape `[nC, 2n + n²]`, the released models' layout and one matrix product per token instead of three (`phi_pre()`, `phi_post()` and `phi_res()` are views), and the RMSNorm's weight, which only duplicated `φ`'s rows, is gone. `SinkhornConfig` is `{ iterations, epsilon }`; `SinkhornResult` lost `iterations` and `converged`. Removed because nothing read them: `MhcConfig::{use_mixed_precision, fuse_kernels, recompute_block_size}`, `MhcCache`, `MhcLayer::{with_layer_idx, deep_clone}` (and the layer index `MhcTransformerBlock::new` took) and `MhcParams::random`. Added: `sinkhorn_knopp_backward_batch`, `apply_pre_mapping_backward`, `apply_post_res_mapping_backward`, `MhcParams::identity_init`, `MhcConfig::mixing_dim` and `sinkhorn_config`, and `CollapseMode: FromStr`. The README's configuration table listed defaults the code never had (3 streams, 10 iterations) and is corrected
- **Gemma 4's native sliding-window layers keep a window of keys instead of the whole context.** Every layer's KV cache held every token, though a sliding layer never reads further back than its window (512 to 1024 tokens), so the cache of a long context was mostly keys nothing would read again: for a model with 50 sliding layers of 16 KV heads, about 0.8 MB a token and 105 GB at 128k tokens, against under 1 GB for the windows. A sliding layer's buffer now holds the last `sliding_window` keys and a slack of 256 slots (`LayerCache::base` is the absolute position of its first slot, moved forward when the slack runs out, once per 256 tokens of decode); a prefill pass attends the tail of the cache plus its own keys and leaves exactly those behind, and the compiled decode and KV-sharing blocks take the base to place a new key and to mask by absolute position. Full-attention layers are unchanged. On Gemma 4 E4B 4-bit (which shares KV between layers, so it has the least to save) the 16384-token prefill peaks at 7.2 GB instead of 7.65 GB and the 32768-token one at 8.6 GB instead of 9.7 GB, at the same speed within the machine's load noise. Checked against transformers on the small fp32 E2B/E4B-layout model, decoding one token at a time to 40 tokens against a window of 8 with the buffer moving every token, every few and not at all, and with and without trimming the cache before the decode: every step within 2e-8 of the reference's row, and the sliding layers' buffers stay within the window plus slack however long the decode ran
- **Gemma 4's native prefill attends only the keys a sliding layer can reach, and no longer compiles and keeps a graph for every prompt length.** A pass of more than one token ran each layer through a compiled block traced per cache length and prompt shape, so every request with a new prompt length compiled a graph that was never freed. The block also wrote the new keys by scatter and masked the whole cache, so a sliding layer's pass over a long prompt scored every cached key and discarded all but `sliding_window` of them. Passes of more than one token now run per op (the compiled block stays for single-token decode, whose shape is fixed): the write is a slice update, a full layer takes MLX's causal mode, and a sliding layer slices the cache to the keys its window reaches and masks only those, over a mask built on the device instead of on the host. KV-sharing layers (E2B and E4B) use the same attention. On an M4 Max under load, provisional, with the two builds alternating over an 8192-token prompt on Gemma 4 E4B 4-bit: 11.3 and 12.5 s down to 9.9 and 10.5 s, peak 8.8 GB down to 6.65 GB. Checked against transformers on a new small fp32 model with the E2B/E4B layout (six layers, the last two sharing KV, per-layer input embeddings, 40 tokens against a window of 8), whole and in passes of 1 to 100 tokens, and against a host softmax over the allowed keys for full layers and for windows that do and do not reach the start of the cache
- **The Qwen3 and Gemma 4 native engines prefill a prompt in passes of 2048 tokens and compute only the last position's logits.** A prompt went through as one forward pass whose LM head ran over every position, and the logits of all but the last were thrown away: over Gemma 4's 262144-token vocabulary that is 4 GB of bf16 logits (and a softcap over them) per 4096 prompt tokens, and a graph over the whole prompt that grew with it. `forward_step_last` (both engines) applies the head to one row, `pmetal_runtime::decode::prefill_last_logits` runs the passes against the cache each one fills, and `prefill_first_token` uses them (a TurboQuant cache keeps the single pass, since its later passes attend to dequantized earlier ones). Qwen3.5-family images prefill through `forward_embeddings_last`. On an M4 Max under load, provisional, with the old and new prefill alternating over a 4096-token prompt: Gemma 4 E4B 4-bit 5.9 to 6.6 s down to 5.1 to 5.9 s, peak 10.3 GB down to 6.9 GB; Qwen3-1.7B bf16 1.19 to 1.36 s down to 1.13 to 1.17 s, peak 5.7 GB down to 4.5 GB.
