Against transformers on the small fp32 fixtures (Gemma 4's sliding window shorter than the prompt, a GDN layer's convolution and recurrent state, YaRN), passes of 1, 2, 7, 33, 64 and 69 tokens and more give the same last logits and the same cached decode afterwards, to the parity tolerances; a 16384-token prompt peaks at 1.2 GB in passes against 5.2 GB in one
- **A routed (MoE) layer's prefill groups its token-expert pairs by expert before the matmuls, 3 to 12 times faster per layer.** `pmetal_runtime::native_moe::switch_glu`, which the Qwen3.5-family, Gemma 4, gpt-oss, Llama 4 and DeepSeek native engines and the packed-expert path of `DynamicModel` run their experts through, fed MLX's gather matmuls the `(token, expert)` pairs in the order the router chose them, so every row was its own small matmul against a different expert. From 64 pairs up it now sorts them by expert, runs each expert's rows as one run with the gather kernels' `sorted_indices`, and puts the rows back, as MLX's reference `SwitchGLU` does (`switch_glu_grouped` takes the choice explicitly). An activation that adds a per-expert term (gpt-oss's biases) gets each row's expert ids through `switch_glu_with`. A decode step is below the threshold and unchanged. On one layer shaped like a 128-expert, top-8 model (hidden 2048, expert width 768), router order against grouped on an M4 Max under load, provisional: 11.2 against 3.2 ms for 128 tokens, 169 against 13.8 ms for 2048 in bf16, and 77.7 against 15.7 ms in 4-bit. Checked against a per-row f64 reference on dense experts, and on packed experts carrying a tensor scale each, where the scale has to follow the expert a sorted pair went to; returning the rows in the wrong order fails both
  - One layer of 64 experts (2048 wide, 1408 intermediate, top-6, bf16) takes 23 ms for 128 tokens and 92 ms for 512 grouped into 2.9 and 5.8 ms, and 403 ms for 2048 tokens into 61 ms; decoding a single token stays on the per-row path, where grouping is slower (1.4 against 0.6 ms). Best of five interleaved runs on an M4 Max under a load average of 30, so provisional
  - End to end, DeepSeek-V2-Lite in bf16 on an M4 Max prefills 512 tokens at about 1,950 tok/s and 2,048 at about 1,750, against about 520 and 465 without it (grouped, ungrouped, grouped again, under a load average of 11 to 44, so provisional); decoding, about 70 tok/s, does not change
  - The grouped result matches a loop that runs one expert on one row at a time to 1e-5, with per-expert biases in the activation, and so do the gradients of the input and every expert weight
- **The native engines' TurboQuant KV cache (`pmetal_runtime::turboquant`, behind `--kv-turboquant` on Qwen3, Qwen3.5 and Llama 4) stores what it claims to store, packed, and attends straight from it.** It kept one byte per coordinate whatever the bit width, a second copy of the indices transposed beside the first, the QJL sign words, the norms and several shadow layouts: 528 to 688 bytes per token per KV head at head dimension 128 with 3-bit, 4-bit and 8-bit keys and values, more than the 512 bf16 takes. It now keeps `ceil(D * bits / 32)` words of bit-packed indices and one f32 scale per vector, per KV head and token: 136 bytes at 4 bits (3.8x smaller than bf16), 104 at 3 (4.9x), 72 at 2 (7.1x) and 264 at 8
  - Decode attention reads the packed cache directly, in two kernel launches (a block pass that serves every query head of a KV head from one read of the tokens, and a merge that also rotates the accumulator back), over the sinks, the packed history and the recent ring together. Before, only 8-bit keys and values at head dimensions 128 and 256 had a fused kernel; every other width (4 and 3 bits, the ones anyone uses) dequantized the whole cache to f32 and ran SDPA, and the generic kernels took the context length as a template argument, so they compiled a new Metal kernel at every decode step of a cache that quantized each token as it arrived. Any head dimension (tested from 32 to 576, with 576-wide keys and 512-wide values, as DeepSeek's latent attention has them) and every width from 1 to 8 bits runs the same kernels, keys and values may differ in both, a KV head serving more query heads than one launch holds (32 at head dimension 128) is split over several launches, and a layer can attend to a suffix of the sequence (a sliding window, a chunk) or add a per-head sink logit (gpt-oss's)
  - The first `sink_tokens` (default 4) and the newest `recent_window` tokens stay uncompressed, as before for the window; the sinks are new. The first tokens of a sequence draw attention whatever the query is, so noise in them is multiplied by the largest softmax weights
  - Attention output against f64 attention over the original keys and values, on keys with outlier channels, a logit spread of 3 and a 1,537-token context (ten seeds, head dimension 128, mean relative error): 8-bit 0.9% (3.0% before), 6-bit 4.7% (7.7%), 4-bit 15.6% (23.4%), 3-bit 27.5% (47.2%). Every extra bit now halves it, which the tests check at head dimensions 64, 128 and 256 with grouped-query heads; the fused kernel matches f64 attention over the dequantized cache to 3e-3 (the queries are held as halves in threadgroup memory) across widths, head dimensions, lengths around every tile and block edge, wrapping rings, sinks, sliding windows and bf16/f16/f32
  - While nothing is quantized yet (a context shorter than the recent window), attention is MLX's fused SDPA over the dense tokens in place, exact to f32 arithmetic. A prompt longer than the window is quantized 4096 tokens at a time, not all at once: appending 60,000 tokens of 8 KV heads at head dimension 128 needed 593 MB beyond the cache for the f32 rotation of the whole prompt, and now needs under 160 MB, whatever the length
  - Keys and values are stored with the least-squares gain of their reconstruction folded into the one scale: a Lloyd-Max reconstruction is a little shorter than its vector by an amount that varies from vector to vector, which scales the logit of every key by a different factor. Over the same context it takes 4-bit key-only attention error from 19% to 8% on the CPU model of the quantizer, at no cost in storage
  - A dequantized cache comes back in the dtype it was filled in, not f32
- **Debug and release builds share one MLX build.** A debug build compiled MLX itself unoptimized and linked it statically, and a release build compiled it optimized and, in this repository, linked it shared, so a checkout kept two differently-configured MLX builds of the same revision and its debug tests ran on an unoptimized MLX. MLX is now always compiled optimized, and `PMETAL_MLX_STATIC` alone decides how it links, in either profile: statically by default, and shared in this repository's own builds, which therefore share one MLX build. Shipped binaries are unchanged (release, static). The build that first fills the shared MLX cache also links against the cache now, as every later build does, rather than against its own CMake tree, which another variant's rebuild or `cargo clean` could delete from under the binary
- **Every sampler applies top-k, top-p and min-p through one function, after the temperature, in transformers' order.** There were three implementations: the native engines' `sample_token`, `Sampler`, and the free filters `sampling_log_probs` (which speculative decoding verifies against) used. They now all draw from `pmetal_runtime::decode::filtered_log_probs`, which scales the logits by the temperature in f32 and then applies top-k, top-p and min-p in that order, as transformers' `TemperatureLogitsWarper`, `TopKLogitsWarper`, `TopPLogitsWarper` and `MinPLogitsWarper` do. What changes:
  - `Sampler` and `sampling_log_probs` filtered the untempered distribution, so a temperature changed which tokens top-p and min-p kept only on the native engines; it now does on every path
  - The native engines' top-p summed a tempered distribution that wasn't normalized, so its cut was wrong at any temperature but 1
  - Top-p reads the distribution renormalized over what top-k kept, keeps every token tied at its cut, and always keeps the most likely token; top-k keeps every token tied with the `k`-th. Ties no longer fall to index order
  - Native sampling runs in f32 whatever the logits' dtype, and `min_p = 1` is accepted (it keeps the most likely tokens only)
  - All three filters together over a 151,936-token vocabulary take about 0.6 ms on an M4 Max, down from 1.7 to 2.4 ms: no sort for min-p and no scatter back through an inverse permutation for top-p
  - Public API: `filtered_log_probs` and `GenerationConfig::sampling_params` are new; `Sampler::apply_filters_fused` and the free `top_k_filter`, `top_p_filter` and `min_p_filter` are gone
  - Worked examples check each rule (temperature before top-p, top-k ties, top-p renormalized after top-k, a tie at the top-p cut, min-p after the temperature, per-row f32), and `Sampler` draws from exactly the distribution `sampling_log_probs` reports under every filter, the temperature and the penalties, with 4,000 seeded draws within 0.03 of it. Moving the temperature after the filters, dropping top-p's renormalization or letting `sampling_log_probs` diverge from `Sampler` each fails them
- **`generate`, `generate_cached` and MTP decoding read their penalties from the same GPU history as every other decode path.** `Sampler::sample` applied the repetition penalty by uploading the whole prompt and output on every token and the frequency and presence penalties from a host-side count map it kept itself, and the MTP loop carried its own copy of both, cloned for every draft and verify step: the last penalty implementation beside `pmetal_runtime::decode::DecodePenalties`. `Sampler::sample` now takes the generation's `DecodePenalties` and samples through `sample_array_penalized`, which takes each token into them. The MTP loop keeps the emitted sequence's penalties, drafts and verifies each round on a clone of them (a clone shares the history's arrays), and records only the tokens it emits, so accepted drafts enter the history in order and rejected ones never do; its greedy verification takes the argmax of the penalized logits, as the `Sampler` does, rather than of their log-softmax. Public API: `Sampler::sample(&self, logits, Option<&mut DecodePenalties>)` replaces `sample(&mut self, logits, history)`, `sampling_log_probs(logits, Option<&mut DecodePenalties>, config)` replaces `sampling_log_probs_with_counts`, `GenerationConfig::decode_penalties` builds a generation's penalties, `DecodePenalties` is `Clone` and gains `record_id`, and `apply_repetition_penalty`, `apply_frequency_presence_penalty`, `count_token`, `Sampler::update_counts` and `Sampler::reset_counts` are gone
  - Greedy MTP under a repetition penalty of 1.3, frequency 0.5 and presence 1.5 emits the tokens plain penalized greedy decoding does: on the dynamic and native Qwen3.5 test checkpoint and on Gemma 4 with their own heads, and on the dynamic model with a head that drafts the penalized continuation (every draft accepted) and one that gets every second or third draft wrong. Recording rejected drafts into the history, not recording accepted ones, or verifying a position without the drafts before it each fails it
- **`pmetal serve`'s continuous batching keeps each request's penalty history on the GPU.** The batched path applied the repetition, frequency and presence penalties from a host-side copy of each request's prompt and output, which it cloned and uploaded for every request on every step, a second implementation beside the one the native engine, the `DynamicModel` loops and the single-request path share. Each request now gets its own `DecodePenalties` when it takes a batch row, started from its whole prompt (including any part restored from the prefix cache), and every token sampled for it is folded in on the GPU before the host reads it; the history goes when the request leaves its row, so the next request to take it starts clean. `Sampler::sample_array_with_penalties`, which only the batched path called, is gone
  - Three greedy requests with different prompts and penalty settings (a repetition penalty of 1.3; 1.1 with frequency 0.8 and presence 0.3; a reward of 0.7 with presence 0.6), two decoding at a time so the third takes the row the second frees, each pick the tokens `Sampler::sample` picks for them alone, and different ones than without the penalties. Not recording sampled tokens, leaving the prompt out, sampling every request with one request's history, or letting a reused row keep the previous request's history each fails it
- **Native Qwen MTP rewinds a partial accept instead of running the kept tokens again.** After a verify kept only some of its drafts, `infer --mtp` on the native engine went back to a copy of the cache and ran the kept tokens through the whole model a second time. It now moves the attention caches back and replays only the GDN layers' state update over the kept tokens from the inputs the verify recorded (`qwen3_native::rewind_verify`, the rewind the DFlash target already used; replaying cached inputs is the activation replay of Wu et al., *Snakes and Ladders*, PMLR 262, 2024)
  - The rewound engine matches running the kept tokens, for every prefix of a block, to 1.9e-6 on the fp32 test checkpoint (bit for bit except when one token is kept), and greedy MTP output still equals plain greedy decoding token for token there. Leaving the GDN state unrewound fails both
  - Qwen3.5-0.8B on an M4 Max, 384 greedy tokens, eight interleaved runs each: median decode 1.97 s to 1.82 s (195 to 212 tok/s), faster in seven of the eight pairs; on a busier machine 2.11 s to 1.96 s. Plain decoding runs at about 210 tok/s on the same prompt, so this checkpoint's head (1.4 tokens a verify) roughly breaks even. In bf16 the MTP reply is now the plain greedy reply on that prompt, all 384 tokens; running the kept tokens again had left it in the first sentence
- **Cut cross-entropy runs as fused Metal kernels, at a fraction of the full logits' memory and, on bf16 heads, faster than them.** `LmHead::cut_cross_entropy` (and `train --cut-cross-entropy`) built the loss from MLX ops a vocabulary chunk at a time and rebuilt each chunk for the backward pass, which kept memory down but cost about 1.6 times the full logits' time. The forward is now one kernel that multiplies 64x64 tiles of hidden states and head rows in simdgroup matrices, accumulating in f32 and rounding each logit once to the head's dtype as the model's head does, and folds every tile into each token's logsumexp and target logit, so the logits never reach memory. The backward filters the gradient as the cut cross-entropy paper (arXiv 2411.09009) and its reference implementation do, by 8-token group and 64-row vocabulary tile: the forward records each group's largest logit per tile, which is enough to leave out, without recomputing them, the tiles where the group's softmax stays below the threshold; the rest are recomputed, checked again, and reduced into the hidden states' and the head's gradients by two block-sparse kernels, one vocabulary chunk of scratch at a time. The loss is one op with a custom VJP, so gradients reach the hidden states, the head and an adapter on it, with Gemma's softcap and Cohere's and Granite's logit scales. A second derivative, which the kernels have no VJP for, goes through MLX ops, and a device without Metal or a dtype other than f32, f16 or bf16 takes the chunked path with a warning
  - The threshold is the head dtype's machine epsilon over 512 (2⁻¹⁶ for bf16; `CutCrossEntropyConfig::filter_eps`). The reference implementation's epsilon over 32 (2⁻¹²) left the hidden states' gradient on Qwen3-0.6B over 4,096 tokens of varied text 1.25e-2 from f32, against 9.6e-3 for the bf16 full logits; at 2⁻¹⁶ it is 9.7e-3, and on the repeated sample text 1.10e-2 against the full logits' 1.11e-2 (the head's gradient 1.05e-2 against 1.13e-2)
  - A fused forward and backward step on an M4 Max, Qwen3-0.6B with its bf16 head, the fastest of five rounds with the paths interleaved: at 2,048, 4,096 and 8,192 tokens, 126, 248 and 418 ms for the hidden states' gradient (full logits 133, 294 and 652; the chunked graph 214, 478 and 908) and 146, 266 and 439 ms with the head's (full logits 190, 440 and 913; chunked 411, 749 and 1,248), peaking at 160, 320 and 641 MB (full logits 1,249, 2,498 and 4,996; chunked 1,692, 1,446 and 1,851) and 627, 633 and 951 MB with the head's (chunked 3,211, 3,924 and 5,308)
  - Qwen3.5-0.8B, whose head computes f32 logits (f32 hidden states) and so filters at 2⁻³², matches f32 to 1.5e-4: 471, 817 and 1,762 ms for the hidden states' gradient (full logits 416, 825 and 1,864) and 532, 1,005 and 2,060 ms with the head's (540, 1,072 and 2,307) in 261, 523 and 1,045 MB (full logits 5,094, 13,239 and 17,325 MB)
  - The skipping depends on how peaked the softmax is. Over 4,096 tokens of varied text (this changelog) Qwen3-0.6B takes 370 ms for the hidden states' gradient against the full logits' 320, and 424 against 422 with the head's
  - The tests hold the loss and the hidden states', head's and bias's gradients to the full logits in f32 over ragged tiles and several backward chunks with every logit transform, and to Richardson-extrapolated central differences (steps H, H/2, H/4); a tile below the threshold gets no gradient, decided by the recomputed softmax where the forward's bound can't. Breaking the lane merge of the logsumexp, the softcap derivative, the target term, the recomputed filter, the bound, the head gradient's transpose, the second-derivative route or the rounding to the head's dtype each fails at least one of them
- **AdamW updates as `torch.optim.AdamW` does on every training path, so a config trains the same model whichever loop runs it.** The Metal fused optimizer, which `pmetal train` runs by default, left out Adam's bias correction while the standard loop applied it, so the first step moved each weight by about 3.16 times the learning rate on the default path and by the learning rate on the other. Both correct now, which makes the default path's early steps smaller than before: about a third of the old size for the first few hundred steps, 0.8 of it at step 1000 and 0.97 at step 3000. The other differences went the same way:
  - The standard loop's AdamW decayed the updated weight rather than the old one, the Metal 4 kernel corrected the moments only when its caller passed the step, and DiffusionGemma's trainer kept an AdamW of its own, now the shared one. The ANE trainer ignored `--weight-decay` (0.01 unless set); it now decays the projection and embedding weights and not the norms, as the GPU loops do
  - The step's scalars are computed in f64 and rounded once, as PyTorch computes them, and the moments keep the parameter's dtype (a bf16 parameter updated by the MLX-op AdamW became f32 after its first step)
  - Against eight steps of PyTorch 2.14.1's `torch.optim.AdamW` under a warmup-then-cosine schedule on three random parameters, the Metal fused kernel, the MLX-op AdamW behind every optimizer and parameter group, `AdamWBuilder` and the ANE trainer's CPU update each land within 2.4e-7 of it after every step. Leaving out the bias correction misses by 3.6e-2 at step 1, and decaying the updated weight by 2.8e-5. SGD with momentum (against `torch.optim.SGD`), Lion (against its authors' implementation) and Adafactor (against the external-learning-rate configuration of transformers' `Adafactor`, since `torch.optim.Adafactor` always takes relative steps) already matched and are held to it the same way, within 2.1e-7
  - The fused kernel runs at the same speed: one dispatch over 10M parameters takes 0.9 to 1.2 ms before and after on an M4 Max
- **An empty LoRA `target_modules` adapts every linear layer except the output layer, as PEFT's `all-linear` does.** It adapted the LM head as well; name it (`lm_head`) to adapt it. The head is found as the layer that projects onto the vocabulary, whatever it is called, and a model that ties its head to the embedding has none to leave out. MoE routers and routed experts stay unadapted as before. On every architecture the dispatcher builds, the empty list now adapts exactly the projections that naming each one does, less the head, and naming only the head adapts only the head
  - An adapter file holding a tensor the model has no adapter for now fails to load and names the tensors, where it used to be skipped and the model served without it. An adapter trained with an empty list before this release carries an LM-head adapter, so loading it needs `lm_head` named in `target_modules`
- **Full-parameter ANE training caps the learning rate at 2e-5, not 5e-6.** The cap keeps a LoRA-sized rate from reaching a full fine-tune; its comment said 2e-5 while the code used 5e-6, which also quartered the 2e-5 the GUI picks for ANE training. 2e-5 is the full fine-tuning reference rate in TRL's documentation (against 2e-4 for LoRA) and what published full SFT of models up to 8B uses; a lower rate is kept as given
- **QLoRA leaves the output layer in the compute dtype whatever it is called.** `quantize_base` recognised it by the name `lm_head`; it now finds it the way an empty `target_modules` does, as the layer that projects onto the vocabulary (`DynamicModel::output_projection_path`), so a head named otherwise stays as loaded and a projection called `lm_head` that is not the head is packed. `quantize_base` takes a `DynamicModel`
- **QLoRA trains through DiffusionGemma's packed experts with MLX's fused `gather_qmm`, not by unpacking them.** The experts are frozen, so all their backward pass owes the adapters is the gradient with respect to the tokens, and MLX's `GatherQMM::vjp` has it (MLX 0.32.3, the pinned version; it refuses only the indices and the packed weight). The training forward instead unpacked every expert of the layer and gathered each token's weights, which on one expert layer of the 26B-A4B model (128 experts, 2,816 by 704, 4-bit) took 39 GB and 413 ms for 256 tokens and 76 GB and 852 ms for 512. The fused kernel takes 4.4 GB and 52 ms for 256 tokens, 105 ms for 512 and 4.5 GB and 438 ms for 2,048 (gradient with respect to the tokens). A test holds its gradient to the unpacked layer's (1e-4) and to the loss's slope along it
- **`pmetal train-diffusion` trains the same LoRA as `pmetal train`, and packs the same QLoRA base.** DiffusionGemma's attention had a second adapter mechanism of its own (`Gemma4AttnLora`, with `QuantLinear` for its packed base) beside the adapters every other architecture carries on `Linear`. It is gone: `train-diffusion` attaches adapters with `AdaptedModel` and packs with `quantize_base`, so `--lora-targets` may name any projection, the NF4, FP4 and int8 schemes all apply, and the routed experts are packed alongside (4-bit affine for nf4 and fp4, 8-bit for int8). `--qlora`, `--qlora-bits` and `--qlora-group-size` are replaced by `pmetal train`'s `--quantization`, `--quant-block-size` and `--double-quant`. The adapter file keeps its keys (`{encoder|decoder}.layers.{i}.self_attn.{proj}.lora_{a,b}`), now with an `adapter_config.json` beside it, and a test loads it the way `infer --lora` does (attach to a fresh base, read, merge) and gets the trained forward back. A failing forward is a step error rather than a NaN loss
- **Native decode keeps its compiled single-token attention graph under scaled RoPE.** The graphs of the dense Qwen3, gpt-oss and Llama 4 engines took a scalar RoPE base and nothing else, so a YaRN or Llama 3-band model (every gpt-oss release, Llama 4 Scout, Qwen3 with the card's YaRN block) ran the per-op path on every token. They now take the period table and the attention factor the per-op path rotates with, through MLX `fast::rope`'s `freqs`. On a small fp32 Qwen3 with YaRN the two paths agree to 1.3e-6 on the logits of every cached step and both match transformers; leaving out the attention factor moves them by 0.25. Decode speed on Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-4B with YaRN is the same on both paths within run-to-run noise (about 275 and 54 tok/s on an M4 Max), so on those models the graph saves CPU dispatch that asynchronous pipelining already hid. `PMETAL_DISABLE_COMPILED_DECODE=1` runs the per-op path for comparison
- **A model whose maker publishes no sampling settings generates as transformers would, not with a Qwen preset.** Without a family preset, `pmetal infer`, `pmetal serve` and everything that loads sampling defaults used temperature 0.7, top-p 0.8 and top-k 20 for whatever `generation_config.json` left out, which is Qwen's non-thinking card applied to every model. They now read `generation_config.json` the way transformers' `generate` does and take transformers' `GenerationConfig` defaults for the rest: greedy unless the file sets `do_sample: true` (a temperature without it is ignored, as transformers ignores it), and temperature 1.0, top-p 1.0 and top-k 50 when sampling. A model with no `generation_config.json` decodes greedily. Family presets from model cards still come first, and an explicit request or CLI value still wins
- **`pmetal infer` without `--max-tokens` uses the model's output budget, not 256 tokens.** That is `max_new_tokens` from its `generation_config.json`, else its `max_length` (which counts the prompt, as in transformers), else 32,768 tokens for a thinking model, the length Qwen's cards recommend for most queries, else 256, never past the context window. 256 tokens stopped every thinking model mid-thought. `pmetal serve` gives a request without `max_tokens` the same budget (and takes OpenAI's `max_completion_tokens`), as do the infer job spec, the GUI, the MCP `generate` tool and the Python `infer()`; an explicit value always wins
- **Sampling presets follow each model maker's published settings, by model family rather than by chat template.** Qwen3 (0.6 thinking, 0.7 / 0.8 non-thinking), the Qwen3-2507 and Qwen3-Next Instruct and Thinking models, Qwen3.5, 3.6 and 3.8 each get their own card's values, where one Qwen preset used to stand in for all of them (giving Qwen3 thinking a temperature of 1.0); Gemma 4, gpt-oss, Mistral Small 3.x, Magistral, Phi-4-reasoning, DeepSeek-R1, DeepSeek-V3-0324 and Nemotron Nano 2 get theirs. The family is read from `config.json` and the model's name; families whose makers publish nothing keep `generation_config.json` and the fallback. Cards that name an output length (Qwen's 32,768 for most queries, 16,384 for Qwen3-2507 Instruct, Magistral's 40,960) set the default output budget when `generation_config.json` does not
- **`pmetal serve` fills the sampling a request leaves out the same way**, instead of decoding greedily: the maker's settings for the model and its thinking mode, else `generation_config.json`, else the fallback. Qwen's cards say not to decode thinking models greedily. `"temperature": 0` still asks for plain greedy decoding, with no default penalties
- **`pmetal serve` without `--max-seq-len` sizes the context from the model, not 4096 tokens.** It takes the model's context window (YaRN-stretched when configured), capped so the weights plus a full-length fp16 KV cache per sequence (per slot under continuous batching) fit in 70% of the device's working set; never under 4096. Qwen3.5-0.8B gets its whole 262,144-token window on a 128 GB Mac, and a 255,025-token prompt there finds a needle from its middle. 4096 left no room for a thinking model's answer or for a large photo on a Qwen3.5-family vision model (up to 16,384 tokens each). The serve job spec, GUI, TUI and MCP `start_serve` leave it to the server unless set
- **Thinking follows each chat template's own default unless asked otherwise.** Every prompt passed `enable_thinking: true` when no flag said otherwise, which turned thinking on for Gemma 4 and Qwen3.5-0.8B, whose makers ship it off. Now an unset control stays undefined in the template, as it does in transformers, and `--no-thinking` or `chat_template_kwargs` set it. Whether a model thinks (for the output budget and the sampling preset) is read from its template the same way
- **QLoRA trains the model `pmetal serve` runs, on every architecture, with its base packed on the GPU.** `pmetal train --quantization` now packs the projections of the same model LoRA trains and attaches the adapters to it, where it used to build one of fourteen per-architecture QLoRA copies that dequantized each base weight on the CPU and copied it to the GPU in f32 on every forward pass. QLoRA gets what LoRA has: every architecture the dispatcher loads, sequence packing, gradient checkpointing, the fused optimizer and the forward pass inference runs. The LM head, MoE routers and routed experts stay as loaded. On Qwen3-0.6B (24 conversations up to 512 tokens), against the old path run back to back: NF4 went from about 110 to 325 tokens/s, FP4 from 105 to 430 and int8 from 210 to 405 (unquantized LoRA: 440); peak GPU memory fell from 6.3 GB to 2.1, 1.9 and 2.2 GB (unquantized: 2.6 GB) and the process from about 4 GB resident to 1.5 GB
  - `nf4` is NF4 as the QLoRA paper defines it, with the codes unpacked on the GPU (MLX has no NF4 kernel). Its loss on that set is 2.648 against 2.604 unquantized, the same as the old CPU path's 2.650, and better than MLX's 4-bit affine format at the same 4.5 bits (2.684)
  - `fp4` is NVFP4: E2M1 values with an E4M3 scale per 16 weights and an FP32 scale per tensor (loss 2.660). It was E2M1 scaled per 64 weights, a format no kernel reads. `--quant-block-size` doesn't apply to it
  - `int8` is MLX's 8-bit affine format over `--quant-block-size` weights (loss 2.601), where it was symmetric over the block's absmax
  - `--double-quant` packs NF4's per-block absmax values to 8 bits, 4.13 bits a weight against 4.25, at no measurable cost (loss 2.647). It's refused for `fp4` and `int8`, which have no absmax to pack. A QLoRA run on a GGUF checkpoint is refused with a pointer to the safetensors one
- **`TrainingConfig.warmup_steps` defaults to 0**, as `pmetal train --warmup-steps` and the reference trainers do. It defaulted to 100, which the commands that build a `TrainingConfig` from defaults (`grpo`, `rlkd`, `distill`) inherited; now that their schedules span the run, a 12-step GRPO run would have spent all of it warming up. With `--config`, the `warmup_steps` a YAML file sets is no longer replaced by the CLI default of 0
- **`GrpoLossType` is `Grpo`, `Dapo` (the default) and `DrGrpo`**; `Bnpo` and `Reinforce` are gone (no CLI flag named them). `GrpoTrainer::compute_grpo_loss` returns a `GrpoLoss` with the ratio and clip fraction beside the losses, `train_step` takes the learning-rate setter so it can schedule each of its updates, and `GrpoIterationStats` gains `clip_fraction` and `iterations`
- **`max_steps` counts optimizer steps in the SFT loops**, as it does in the preference loop and the reference trainers. With gradient accumulation it used to count micro-batches, so `max_steps: 100` with 4 accumulation steps stopped after 25 updates
- **`AdamWGroups` is `ParamGroupOptimizer`** (with `ParamGroupOptimizerBuilder::new(kind, lr)`), since its groups run whichever optimizer the config names, and `TrainingLoop::build_optimizer` builds the one every loop variant uses. The compat `AdamW`'s public `lr` array is gone: it was never read; use `set_lr` and `lr()`
- **`--ane-max-seq-len` is the largest context the ANE engine compiles for**, prompt plus output, and defaults to 4096 (was 1024). Each request gets the smallest power of two from 512 that fits it, so short requests stay fast; a longer one recompiles the model once at the larger size. A prompt that doesn't fit is refused with the flag named
- **A Qwen3 `config.json` without `tie_word_embeddings` means an untied head on the native engine**, as it does in transformers and on the `DynamicModel` path. The native engine assumed tied, so such a checkpoint ran with the embedding table as its head and its own `lm_head.weight` dropped. Every Qwen3 release sets the key, so they load as before
- **The `infer --benchmark` helpers have plain names**: `benchmark_trial` in each native engine (`pmetal_runtime::{qwen3_native, llama4_native, deepseek_native, gpt_oss_native}`), `pmetal::native_inference::benchmark_native` returning `BenchmarkTrialMetrics`, and `InferenceRunner::benchmark`. They were named after another tool's benchmark, whose workload shape they reproduce. Behaviour and output are unchanged
- **Default models download from their makers' own Hugging Face repositories**: `Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B` for `bench-gdn --model`, the `hybrid-qwen3next` and `hybrid-qwen35-steady` bench presets and the DFlash capture example, and `nvidia/NVIDIA-Nemotron-3-Nano-4B-BF16` for the `moe-nemotronh` preset. They had pointed at a third-party re-upload; the weights are byte for byte the same

- **Every dependency is at its latest release**, with all lockfiles regenerated from scratch. Majors: `hf-hub` 1.0, `safetensors` 0.8, `base64` 0.23, `pyo3` 0.29, `dirs` 7
  - `hf-hub` 1.0 is a rewrite. Downloads now use Hugging Face's Xet transfer protocol and retry 429 and 5xx responses on their own. The Xet client's per-request INFO logging is held to warnings in the CLI
  - Training, merge and distillation YAML configs are read and written with `serde-saphyr` in place of `serde_yaml_ng`, which has had no release since 2024 and parses through a machine translation of libyaml's C. It reads YAML 1.1 as before, so `yes` and `no` are still booleans
  - Bundled MLX moved to upstream `main` (`bebd642`, 96 commits past v0.32.3). Training through `fast::scaled_dot_product_attention` without an array mask now runs MLX's fused Metal attention backward instead of the unfused one (`MLX_SDPA_VJP_FALLBACK=1` restores the old path). MLX also sends two cases down that path that it can't finish, 8 queries or fewer and attention sinks, and both threw during the backward pass; the bridge keeps them on the unfused path, and a test holds every case's gradient to the unfused one. It also brings f32 accumulation for split-K quantized matmuls, `gather_qmm` over expert offsets, a threadgroup-size fix for attention on M1 and M2, and fixes for reductions over very large arrays and negative-stride masks. Its latest fixes are in kernels pmetal runs: the batched row count in the split-K quantized matrix-vector product, a stream dropped in the index cast `gather_mm` and `gather_qmm` share and in the layer-norm and attention fallbacks, temporaries `pad` and `fill` released too early, and the convolution bounds and gradient shapes. Earlier in this cycle it had moved to `99f109b`, and before that to `09e67c6`, for a Metal fence deadlock fix, lower SDPA memory use at head dims 256 and 512, and a faster gather matmul
- **MSRV**: Raised workspace `rust-version` to 1.95. The 1.89 it declared no longer built: `ordered-float` needs 1.90, and `hf-hub`'s Xet client needed 1.91 and now, through `sysinfo` 0.39, needs 1.95, verified with `cargo +1.95 check`. The pinned toolchain is 1.99
- **TensorBoard logging is always built** and writes its event files with pmetal's own writer (`pmetal_trainer::tensorboard::EventWriter`, on `prost` and `crc`). The `tensorboard-rs` crate it replaces had been unmaintained since 2022 and pulled in `protobuf` 2.28 (RUSTSEC-2024-0437) and 15 other crates. The `tensorboard` feature is now a no-op, kept so manifests that name it still resolve. Each metric is now its own tag in one event file (`train/loss`, `train/epoch`, `epoch/loss`, `epoch/perplexity`). `tensorboard-rs` wrote each metric to its own run subdirectory (`log_dir/train/loss/`) under the shared tag `train`, which drew loss and epoch number on one chart
- **The GUI uses bun alone.** The release build installed from `pnpm-lock.yaml` while local builds and preflight used `bun.lock`, so a release could ship frontend dependencies nobody had built locally. `pnpm-lock.yaml` is gone, and `packageManager` in `package.json` pins the bun version CI installs
- **Local builds take far less disk.** The root, GUI and fuzz workspaces share one `target/`. MLX is compiled once per configuration and reused by every `pmetal-runtime` variant, where each variant used to compile its own (~800 MB and several minutes apiece). Dev builds keep line tables rather than full debug info (`CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_DEBUG=true` restores it). A full `just preflight` from clean leaves 19 GB
- **Dependency policy is enforced by `cargo-deny`** (`deny.toml`, `just deny`, in `just preflight` and a new CI job) for both the workspace and the GUI. Licenses are permissive-only; the six MPL-2.0 crates in the tree (`option-ext`, `colored`, and in the GUI `cssparser`, `cssparser-macros`, `dtoa-short`, `selectors`) are named exceptions, so a new copyleft crate fails the check. `just audit` now runs the same advisory check with `deny.toml`'s reviewed ignores
- **Distributed runs need a cluster secret** (breaking). `RingBackend`, `AutoDiscoveryBackend`, `PipelineHarness` and `pmetal train --distributed-*` refuse to start without `PMETAL_CLUSTER_SECRET` or `~/.pmetal/cluster_secret` (create one with `pmetal cluster keygen` and copy it to every machine). `DistributedConfig`, `AutoDiscoveryConfig` and `PipelineHarnessConfig` gained `secret` and `allow_plaintext` fields. `DistributedBackend` gained `all_reduce_f32` as its primary method (the byte-buffer `all_reduce` is now provided, and no longer rejects a `Vec<u8>` at an address that is not 4-aligned), plus `reduce_scatter_f32`, `all_gather_f32_in_place`, `all_gather_bytes` and `broadcast`.
- **`ring_attention_forward` takes a `RingAttentionConfig`** (scale, causal, mode, `SequenceLayout`, total length) in place of the scale and mode, and `gather_sequence` takes the sequence axis, layout and total length; `ZBAction::Send` and `Recv` are `SendActivation`, `RecvActivation`, `SendGradient` and `RecvGradient`; `solve_layer_assignment` and `assign_layers_proportional` return a `Result` instead of panicking.
- **A pipeline stage's `wire_dtype` is the dtype of the tensors it hands over.** Each message carries the dtype of its payload, and `ActivationMessage::into_dtype` converts a received one.
- **`FusedAdamW::new` returns a `Result`.** It allocates the table of runs the update walks and refuses a parameter set a 32-bit index cannot address; the trainer's two call sites propagate the error.
- **The dense TurboQuant rotation and projection of the shared KV cache are one Accelerate SGEMM.** A head dimension that is not a power of two applied its matrices with a Metal kernel that copied the rows to the GPU and back, or with a scalar loop when that was unavailable. SGEMM does the product faster than either, with no round trip to the GPU. A test checks the rotation and projection of a 96-dimensional core against a float64 product and that the inverses undo them.

### Removed

- **`TrainingDataset::from_jsonl` and `from_parquet` (deprecated stubs that returned an empty dataset), `PackingError` (nothing produced it), `DataCollator` (a label-less second batch builder nothing used), and `FixedSizeImageProcessor::{process_image_gpu, init_gpu_arrays}`**, a second, inexact implementation of the normalisation chain for three multiply-adds per pixel.
- **`Sample::attention_mask`.** Every sample stored a mask of ones, four bytes a token (as much again as the ids themselves); nothing read it, and the loaders build the batch mask from the lengths.
- **The CPU hybrid Qwen3.5 engine** (`pmetal_metal::ane::inference_hybrid`, `pmetal_models::{generate_cached_hybrid_cpu, generate_cached_hybrid_cpu_streaming, is_hybrid_cpu_compatible, hybrid_cpu_inference_config}`, and the serve and `infer --ane` branches that called them). It was an f32 CPU re-implementation of the Qwen3.5 forward pass that only a top-level `model_type` of `qwen3_5_text` or `qwen3_next` could reach; the Qwen3.5 checkpoints are `qwen3_5` with the text config nested, and the one `qwen3_next` is an MoE it refused, so no released checkpoint ran on it, and where one would it decoded several times slower than the GPU engine. Qwen3.5 runs on the GPU engines, as it did.
- **`pmetal-metal`'s attention, RoPE, SwiGLU, LoRA and GEMM kernels, and with them the per-shape backend timing in `fused_sdpa`.** `FlashAttention`, `FlashAttentionVarlen`, `MppFlashAttention`, `FusedRoPE`, `FusedSwiGLU`, `FusedMLP`, `FusedLora`, `FusedNormLora`, `MppGemm`, `MppDwGemm` and the `Mpp*` variants of the rest are gone, with their shaders, their tuners and cache files in `tuna`, the GEMM tile recommendations that sized them (`DeviceProperties::{recommended_tile_size, mpp_tile_config, should_consider_mpp_gemm}`, and the device tab's tile-size line), the matching `pmetal bench` cases and the invented numbers on the TUI device tab's kernel-tuning panel. Each was measured against the tensor-runtime op it would replace and lost, and four of them were wrong: the attention kernel is off by up to 0.29 against a float64 reference at 2048 tokens and by 1.0 at 100, aligns a causal mask to the top-left where a decode query must see its whole prefix, and accepts head dimensions 80, 96 and 256 that its shader has no variant for; the fused LoRA forward returns output as wrong as the output is large, and its backward cannot run once `out_features` passes about 2000; and the "Metal 3" GEMM called a kernel that refuses to run without the M5 neural accelerators. None was reachable from a workload: gradient-carrying attention already took the runtime's fused SDPA, and `fused_sdpa` had timed the Metal kernel against it and kept the runtime at every prefill length from 512 to 16384 tokens. `fused_sdpa` now calls the runtime's SDPA (and the manual path for logit softcapping, which it has no fused form for), and `pmetal-mlx` drops its persisted backend-choice cache and the `dirs` dependency only that used.
- **`pmetal-metal`'s kernel-backend layer**: `KernelDispatch`, the `KernelBackend` trait, `Metal3Backend`, the Metal 4 backend with its command-allocator pool, residency manager and MTL4 command buffer, `BatchEncoder` and the `mpp_bench` helpers (about 3,500 lines). The one caller read a single capability bit from it; no kernel was ever dispatched through it. `MetalContext::supports_mpp()` answers that question, and `MetalContext::dispatch()` (which panicked on a context made with `new()`) is gone.
- **Unused public API in `pmetal-core`**: the `Dataset`, `Quantizer`, `Optimizer`, `LrScheduler` and `ConfigLoader` traits (no type implemented them), `Device` (its default flipped to the Neural Engine with a default feature), `ModelOutput`, `SchedulerBuilder` (a second copy of `LearningRateScheduler`'s builder methods), and the `defaults` constants that contradicted the config types' own defaults (`BATCH_SIZE` 4, `MAX_SEQ_LEN` 2048, `WARMUP_STEPS` 100) or that nothing read. The scheduler no longer clamps a stable ratio or polynomial power; `ConfigValidator` rejects them.
- **`pmetal-distill`'s `CombinedLoss`, `run_distillation` and the old TAID machinery.** `CombinedLoss` was a parallel, weaker copy of `Distiller::compute_loss` (no ignore index, no f32 normalization) that nothing used; `run_distillation` validated a config and then always returned an error pointing at another crate; `TaidSchedule`, `TaidLossType` and the difficulty, temperature and label-smoothing fields belonged to a method that was not TAID (see Fixed). `RationaleLoss` lost the marker strings, flag and epsilon nothing read.
- **The batched, async, GPU-stacked and int8 merge pipelines are removed**: `run_merge_batched`, `BatchedMerger`, `AsyncMergePipeline`, `GpuMerger`, `Fp8Merger`, the host-side `quickselect` sparsifier and its batch helpers, `ZeroCopyLoader`, `SouperMerge` and the unused `int8_mask` parameter. Each re-implemented TIES by hand and so carried the same sign-vote defect, none was reachable from `run_merge` or the CLI, `run_merge_batched` never finalized its writer so a model under one shard was never written, and the int8 path quantized the inputs before merging. `run_merge` is the one pipeline (MLX already runs the method implementations on the device) and `SafetensorsLoader` is the one loader.

- **The bridge TurboQuant cache's QJL residual, per-block outliers, full-byte pack mode, Hamming skip-list, warm tier and the `tq-ablation` feature** (`TurboQuantQjlMode`, `TurboQuantOutlierMode`, `TurboQuantPackMode`, `TurboQuantWarmTier`, `skiplist_threshold`, the `turboquant_qjl_ablation` bench and three microbenches). The QJL sketch of the paper's inner-product quantizer is unbiased, and the variance it adds is what a softmax amplifies: with the same bits, the codebook alone gave the lower attention error at every width measured (3-bit keys and values: 0.30 against 0.47 with QJL, 4-bit: 0.13 against 0.23, head dimension 128), which independent implementations also report, and the repository's own ablation had found its contribution close to zero. The others stored per-token metadata larger than the indices they protected, or approximated attention. `pmetal`'s conversion from the shared cache configuration drops the knobs the native cache no longer has. The native cache takes uniform widths only for now: a mixed-precision configuration (the `q2_5` and `q3_5` presets) runs on the shared model path's cache, as an unsupported cache mode does, where it used to run natively through dequantize-and-SDPA
- **`pmetal_mlx::speculative::SpeculativeDecoder`** (with `SpeculativeConfig`, `SpeculativeResult`, `SpeculativeStats`, `estimate_optimal_k` and `is_speculative_beneficial`). Nothing used it: speculative decoding runs through `pmetal_models`' decoders, which draw from the seed. Its acceptance tests drew from a generator seeded by the clock, so no seed could reproduce them. `SpecCapture` stays
- **`pmetal_metal::FusedSampler`** (with `FusedSamplerConfig`, its `SamplingParams` and the `fused_sampler.metal` shader), also in the `pmetal` prelude. Nothing has called it since sampling moved onto MLX's graph (`pmetal_runtime::decode`), and the `metal-sampler` infer backend that last reached for a Metal sampler is gone. `pmetal_metal::AsMetalBuffer` now names the buffer trait the kernels take (`buffer::AsMetalBuffer`); the sampler had its own copy under the same name, and `FusedLora` takes the shared one
- **pmetal-lora's `metal-fused` feature and `pmetal`'s `lora-metal-fused`**, which forwarded to it. No code read either: turning them on only linked `pmetal-metal` into pmetal-lora, and LoRA trained the same way. The READMEs and the feature-flag docs listed a ~2x LoRA speedup for them
- **`infer --backend compiled`, `metal-sampler` and `minimal`.** None did anything `standard` doesn't do as well or better. All three refused to run on the native engine, which `infer` uses for Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama 4, DeepSeek, gpt-oss and Gemma 4 (`run_with is not available in native mode`), so on those models they were an error. On the other models: `compiled` compiled nothing and decoded at `standard`'s speed; `minimal` always took the most likely token, whatever the temperature, top-k, top-p and penalties asked for, at the same speed; and `metal-sampler` printed nothing until it had finished and was slower. On Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct (4-bit) on an M4 Max, 256 tokens took 1.69 s greedy and 2.06 s at temperature 0.7 with `metal-sampler`, against 1.52 s and 1.62 s with `standard`. `--backend` takes `auto`, `standard` and `ane`, and so do `InferSpec`, the MCP `generate` tool and the TUI. `pmetal_models::{generate_cached_compiled, generate_cached_metal, generate_minimal_async}`, `pmetal_models::sampling::MetalSampler` and `InferenceGenState::run_with`, which only these paths used, are gone with them
- **The Mixture-of-Depths router on Llama 4** (`use_mod`, `mod_capacity`, `mod_layers` and `mod_layer_interval` in `Llama4TextConfig`, `Llama4ModRouter`, `mod_aux_loss`). It is not part of Llama 4 and no released checkpoint carries it. It was off by default, its token selection sat in the autograd graph without being cut from it, its auxiliary loss was stored but never added to any loss, and its routed tokens attended to each other in both directions. A config that still sets the keys loads as before; they are ignored
- **Kernels that computed outside autograd's graph and that nothing called any more**: `pmetal_metal::FusedDistill` and its MPP twin `MppFusedDistill` (with `FusedHiddenAlign`, the backend's `fused_distill_loss` and `has_distill`), whose SIMD forward also kept one SIMD group's share of the softmax normalizer and so returned a KL about four times off for vocabularies over 1024; `pmetal_mlx::kernels::fast_lora` (its MPP GEMM path and the optimized LoRA forwards) and `pmetal_mlx::kernels::quantized_matmul`; and `pmetal_mlx::moe::{MoELayer, MoERouter, MoEConfig}`, which read routing weights back to the host and rebuilt them as constants. DeepSeek, the one model that held a `MoELayer`, held it only for its experts and routed through its own gate; it now keeps the experts directly, under the same parameter paths. A model or trainer that differentiates its losses goes through MLX's graph, which is what these could not do
- **The per-architecture QLoRA models** (`DynamicQloraModel` and the fourteen `*QloraForCausalLM` types for Llama, Mistral, Qwen 2 / 3, Qwen 3.5, Qwen3-MoE, Gemma, Gemma 4, GPT-OSS, Granite, Llama 4, DeepSeek, Nemotron-H, Phi and Cohere) and `QLoraLinear`. `quantize_base` with `QLoraConfig` and `QLoraScheme`, and `DynamicLoraModel::from_pretrained_quantized`, replace them
- **The code only the per-architecture QLoRA models used**: the six LoRA model stacks they were built from (`Gemma4LoraForCausalLM`, `GptOssLoraForCausalLM`, `Llama4LoraForCausalLM`, `NemotronHLoraForCausalLM`, `Qwen3MoELoraForCausalLM`, `Qwen3NextLoraForCausalLM` and their layers), the standalone adapter layers `LoraLinear`, `DoraLinear` and `LinearAdapter`, `lora_helpers`, the hand-written LoRA backward passes in `autograd` (with `pmetal_trainer::CustomAutogradModel`, which nothing implemented), `sanitize_loaded_weights` and `effective_rank`, GPT-OSS's own LoRA types in pmetal-models (`GptOssLoraForCausalLM`, `GptOssForCausalLM::into_lora`), and `pmetal_mlx::quantization`, the CPU NF4, FP4 and int8 quantizers. LoRA and DoRA live on `Linear` and every architecture trains through `AdaptedModel`
- **`pmetal_metal::BatchedLora`** (with `BatchedLoraAdapters` and `BatchedLoraConfig`, also in the `pmetal` prelude). Its two kernels, `batched_lora_forward` and `batched_lora_forward_uniform`, were never written, so every forward failed to build its pipeline
- **`pmetal_metal::FusedTrainingCoordinator`.** Nothing constructed it: the training loops drive `FusedAdamW` through `MlxMetalOptimizer`, and the coordinator only bundled that with a gradient clipper and a cross-entropy kernel behind getters
- **The old ANE inference engine** (`pmetal_metal::ane::inference`, `AneInferenceEngine`) and the `inference_ane` example that drove it. `AneLm` replaced it behind `infer --ane` and `serve --ane`, and nothing else called it. Its sampler moved to `pmetal_metal::ane::lm::sample`
- **`infer --ane-real-time` and `serve --ane-real-time`.** The flag reached only the old engine, so with `AneLm` it did nothing, and the private real-time path it asked for never ran on macOS 27 and failed on earlier releases. It's gone from `InferSpec`, `ServeSpec`, the TUI's Serve form, the GUI and the MCP `infer` and `start_serve` tools, along with `GenerationConfig.ane_real_time` and the runtime's real-time and loopback-chaining probes (`AneModel::evaluate_real_time`, `prepare_loopback_chain`)
- **The static ANE trainer and the code only it used** (`pmetal_metal::ane::{trainer, budget, pipeline, profiler}`, the static kernel generators in `ane::kernel`, and the dynamic `ffn_w2`, `wo_bwd`, `qkv_bwd`, `rmsnorm`, `softmax` and attention-only kernels). `DynamicAneTrainer` replaced the static trainer and runs those layers through its projection kernel; nothing called them. `PMetalError::AneCompileBudgetExhausted`, which nothing raised, is gone too
- **`infer --backend dflash` and `DraftBackend`.** The backend only ever refused to run and pointed at `pmetal dflash`, which is still the way to run DFlash on the GPU (`infer --ane --draft-model` drafts for the ANE engine). It's gone from `InferenceBackend`, `InferSpec`'s backend options and the MCP `infer` tool. `DFlashConfig.draft_backend` had one working value; its other, `DraftBackend::Ane`, returned "not yet implemented"
- **`infer --compiled`, `--metal-sampler` and `--minimal`.** Each picked the same path as `--backend compiled`, `metal-sampler` or `minimal`, and they only took effect with `--backend auto`, so two settings could disagree about one choice. `--backend` is the one way to pick a generation path now. The flags are gone from `InferSpec` (with its `compiled`, `metal_sampler` and `minimal` fields) and the MCP `generate` tool's `compiled` option, and `--backend`'s help no longer lists the removed `dflash`
- **`qwen3_native::{QwenDecodeBackend, canonical_decode_backend, generate_canonical, benchmark_trial_canonical}`.** With the C++ decode path gone the backend had one variant, so the two `_canonical` functions only forwarded to `generate` and `benchmark_trial`, which callers now use directly
- **`pmetal_mlx::offloading`** (`ActivationOffloader`, `GradientOffloader`, `FrozenParameterManager`, `OffloadedEmbedding` and their configs). The module described itself as not yet integrated, and nothing in pmetal used it
- **`pmetal_trainer::SftTrainer`**, a legacy trainer whose `train`, `evaluate`, `save_checkpoint` and `load_checkpoint` only returned "not implemented", along with its `TrainingState` (a second type of that name beside `pmetal_core::TrainingState`) and `lm_loss`. `TrainingLoop` is the trainer
- **`CompressionStrategy::PowerSGD`** in pmetal-distributed. It logged a warning and sent the gradients uncompressed, while the crate README listed it as a working strategy
- **pmetal-trainer's compile and fused-step prototypes** (`explicit_state_compile`, `jit_compile`, `ffi_compile`, `metal_fused` and `lora_trainer`, with `ExplicitStateTrainer`, `CompiledTrainStep`, `CompiledTrainingStep` and `LoraTrainer`). Nothing called them. The three compile modules wrapped their step in a `compile` that returned the function unchanged, so nothing was ever compiled, and `TrainingLoop` already says so when JIT is requested and runs its eager fused path. `metal_fused` and `lora_trainer` were earlier versions of what `TrainingLoop` and `MlxMetalOptimizer` do
- **The library-only RL trainers `GspoTrainer`, `DapoTrainer`, `PpoTrainer` and `OnlineDpoTrainer`, and `reasoning_template`.** None could train a model, and the README listed three of them as working. `GspoTrainer` did not implement GSPO: it reweighted GRPO's token losses and swapped the group mean for a median, with no sequence-level importance ratio. `DapoTrainer` was a set of helpers that `GrpoTrainer`'s DAPO mode (`pmetal grpo --dapo`) already covers. `PpoTrainer` computed GAE and the clipped loss but had no rollout or value model to feed them. `OnlineDpoTrainer` sampled without a KV cache, stopped on token id 2 whatever the tokenizer, and ranked completions by length. The code-execution reward in `reasoning_template` was a stub that scored fenced blocks and substrings
- **The LLaDA-style masked-diffusion trainer** (`pmetal_trainer::diffusion`: `DiffusionTrainingLoop`, `DiffusionConfig`, `forward_process_gpu`, `diffusion_loss_gpu` and the sampler). It trained an ordinary causal LM, whose attention can't see the unmasked tokens to the right of a masked one, so it never learned the bidirectional denoiser LLaDA is. It sat behind the `experimental-trainers` feature, which nothing enabled. DiffusionGemma's block-diffusion training (`pmetal train-diffusion`, `DiffusionGemmaTrainer`) is unchanged, and the README's method table now names it
- **`Adam8bit` and `ScheduleFreeOptimizer`**, which no training loop could use and the READMEs listed as optimizers. `Adam8bit` copied every moment to the CPU and back on each step and quantized the second moment linearly, which loses the small values Adam divides by (8-bit Adam quantizes it on a log-like scale for that reason). `ScheduleFreeOptimizer` replaced Schedule-Free's running average of the iterates with a fixed-weight blend and left out the second moment's bias correction, so it was not the paper's algorithm
- **pmetal-lora's unused adapter variants and helpers**: `QBLoraLinear` and `QBLoraConfig`, the GaLore projector (`GaloreProjector`, `GaloreConfig`, `GaloreParamState` and friends), `ModelPatcher`, `AdapterManager` and the `LoraArchitectureConfig` trait. Each described itself as not yet integrated or had no caller. Q-BLoRA was a standalone layer beside the `Linear`-attached adapters every model trains through. GaLore projected gradients but no optimizer or training loop ever called it, so it saved no memory. The crate README's module and architecture tables, which still listed per-architecture LoRA modules deleted earlier, now describe the crate as it is
- **The separate preference trainers `DpoTrainer`, `SimpoTrainer`, `OrpoTrainer`, the old `KtoTrainer`, `PairedPreferenceTrainer`, and the `experimental-trainers` feature.** `PreferenceTrainer` and the new `KtoTrainer` replace them. The old ones each ran a loop of their own with no learning-rate schedule, accumulation or clipping, and several losses were wrong: ORPO added the summed negative log-likelihood, so on a 200-token answer the odds-ratio term counted for about 1/200th of what the paper weights it; IPO multiplied its margin by β before regressing it to 1/(2β); `DpoLossType::Robust` was a sigmoid-plus-hinge mix that is not Robust DPO; and KTO measured rewards against a reference point of zero instead of the KL estimate. `SimpoTrainer` duplicated SimPO, which `DpoLossType::SimPo` also implemented. `preference_data`'s loaders became `preference::{load_preference_pairs, load_kto_samples}`
- **`pmetal cluster train` and `pmetal cluster serve`.** Both only exited with an error: `cluster train` pointed at `pmetal train --distributed-auto`, which is how to train across the cluster, and `cluster serve` needed per-layer model execution that doesn't exist. `cluster up`, `status`, `bench` and `pipeline-bench` are unchanged
- **The Pixtral, Qwen2-VL and Whisper modules** (`pmetal_models::architectures::{pixtral, qwen2_vl, whisper}`). The docs listed them as implemented architectures, but none could run a released model: Pixtral and Qwen2-VL wrapped the Mistral and Qwen2 text models and dropped the vision weights, and Whisper had no weight loader, audio frontend or decoding loop. Nothing called any of them. CLIP, T5 and the VAE, listed beside them, are the Flux pipeline's components and stay
- **Unused modules in pmetal-mlx**: `attention` (a dispatcher nothing dispatched through), `grouped_gemm_moe` (which copied a whole expert weight per token), `sequence_packing` (pmetal-data packs), `kv_cache::paged` (no attention kernel read its blocks), `quantization::group` with the `QuantScheme::Group*` variants (no GPTQ or AWQ checkpoint could be loaded through it), and in `kernels` the `cross_entropy`, forward-only `metal_cross_entropy`, unfused `metal_norm_lora`, `training_attention` and `swiglu` modules. Training's losses, attention and caches are unchanged
- **pmetal-distributed's `election`, `health`, `collective`, `cloud_bridge` and `solver` modules**, with the error variants and metrics only they would have produced. Nothing started an election, fed the heartbeat monitor, implemented the tree all-reduce or used the cloud bundle export, and `solver` wrapped `layer_assignment` with a made-up latency estimate. The cluster commands, the ring all-reduce and the pipeline harness are unchanged
- **`pmetal_gguf::vec_dot`**, NEON dot products for a CPU matmul pmetal does not have. GGUF weights are dequantized at load and run on the GPU
- **pmetal-mhc's Metal kernels and its `metal` and `cuda` features.** Nothing dispatched the kernels (the mHC layer runs on ndarray), and `cuda` was empty. pmetal's `mhc` feature no longer pulls in `core` and `metal`
- **pmetal-distill's `metal` feature and `is_gpu_available`.** The fused Metal forward they switched on returned each loss as a constant, so it could not train anything (see Fixed), and for any vocabulary over 1024 entries the value was wrong too: its reduction kept one SIMD group's share of the normalizer, which put the KL about four times too high. The losses run on the GPU through MLX
- **pmetal-mlx's FlashAttention training cache**: `compute_attention_gradients`, `AttentionCache` and `differentiable_attention`. Nothing ever called the backward, and against MLX's own attention gradient it was off by 5% on queries and by 40 to 120 times on keys and values for grouped-query heads, while running 1.2 to 5 times slower at 2048 and 4096 tokens. `with_training_mode` and `TrainingContext` remain, as a plain training flag
- **`ZeROPartitioner`, `reduce_scatter_gradients`, `all_gather_params`, `ActivationCodec::SparseColumns`, `assign_layers_bandwidth_aware` and the expert-placement ZeRO helpers.** The partitioner gave whole tensors to ranks by name (101,000 optimizer-state elements on one rank against 2,000 on the other for a 100k-element embedding and three 1k layers), the gradient functions did an all-reduce and discarded the rest, and stage 2's gather never fetched what it had dropped. `SparseColumns` claimed to keep the top columns by norm and did nothing. The bandwidth-aware solver costed a stage as layers over link bandwidth and ignored memory, though with nodes in a fixed order no split changes what a link carries.
- **`pmetal-metal`'s kernels that nothing ran, or that the runtime does better.** Cross-entropy: `FusedCrossEntropy`, `FusedLinearCrossEntropy`, `MppFusedCrossEntropy` and the training step's fused cross-entropy (none is an op in the runtime's lazy graph, so none could be differentiated, and the linear one had no backward at all; the runtime's `cut_cross_entropy_fused` covers the same op and is an order of magnitude faster), with the cross-entropy autotuner and its cache file. MoE: `MoeKernel` and the Metal 4 fused and grouped MoE kernels (f32 only, and an order of magnitude slower than the runtime's sorted `gather_mm` on a gate+up projection at 4096 tokens, 128 experts, top-8). Metal 4 quantized GEMM and AdamW (M5-only, so never run by the tests; MLX has tensor-op quantized kernels of its own). The merge kernels (`pmetal-merge` merges through runtime ops and elects TIES signs by mass, where these voted by count), the GGUF Q4_0 / IQ4_XS dequantization kernels (they interleaved nibbles that GGML splits into two halves of the block; the loader uses the CPU path), the FP8 training kernels (no caller, and a subnormal that rounds up to the smallest normal came out as zero), `FusedGradientClipping` (the runtime clips), and `TurboQuantTransform`. The `KernelBackend` methods for them, the `bench-corpus` fused linear cross-entropy and fused merge cases, and `MlxMetalBridge::dequantize_q4_0` / `dequantize_iq4_xs` go with them. With the last kernels it tuned gone, the autotuner (`pmetal_metal::tuna`, `MetalContext::tuner()`, the build script's shader digest that scoped its cache) is removed, as are the single-pass dispatch helper and the Metal 4 dispatch scaffolding that only these kernels used.

### Fixed

- **The first token of every answer was masked under a tokenizer that appends an end-of-sequence token.** The prompt/completion split was found by tokenizing the prompt alone and masking that many tokens, so a post-processor that adds `</s>` to every encoding put the mask one token late. The mask is decided in bytes of the rendered text and carried to the tokens by their offsets (a token is trained when it starts in a trained span; tokens that stand for no text follow the token before them).
- **A dataset directory loaded one file, which could be the test split.** Discovery returned the first candidate in `read_dir` order; a repository sharded as `train-00000-of-00004.parquet ...` trained on one shard. The train split's shards load in order.
- **Sharding an epoch across ranks gave them different batch counts**, so a rank with one more batch waited forever on a collective its peers never joined, and `DataLoader::num_batches` counted the whole dataset's batches, not the rank's. Each rank serves exactly `n / world_size` samples of the epoch's order.
- **Labels that do not line up with the tokens shifted every later label onto the wrong token** (the loader padded them as if they did); it is an error now. A zero batch size no longer divides by zero.
- **`PackedDataLoader::reset` reshuffled the rows it had already shuffled**, so an epoch's order depended on how many came before it and an interrupted run could not reproduce the epoch it stopped in. An epoch is a function of its seed.
- **`FixedSizeImageProcessor` was one ULP off the reference on most pixels** (an f32 rescale where the reference multiplies in f64). It runs the shared `PixelNormalizer` and is exact against transformers' SigLIP processor at atol 0 on RGB, RGBA and gray inputs.
- **A pretraining shard cut short mid-record read as the end of the shard**, so training quietly dropped the corpus tail; it is `StreamError::TruncatedRecord`. `write_shard` writes beside the target and renames, and rejects a document too long for its `u32` length header instead of wrapping it.
- **Every assistant turn but the last was masked, and preference data was tokenized by the same prompt-length trick.** `TrainingSampleBuilder::build_tokenized` (the preference trainers' tokenizer) shares the new rendering, and the preference trainers read their rows through `RowReader` (Arrow, CSV and shard directories too); `pmetal-trainer` no longer depends on `arrow` or `parquet`.
- **Every ANE evaluation kept its IOSurfaces for the life of the process.** The framework hands back the request, a wrapper per surface (which holds the surface) and its index arrays autoreleased, and the ANE thread has no autorelease pool, so each pass added a reference to every input and output: 200 requests over two surfaces left their retain counts at 201. A served model's KV cache and activation buffers were never freed, and an engine that was dropped and reloaded left the old ones resident. Each evaluation now runs in its own pool, and the IOSurface property dictionary each surface creation leaked is released.
- **`max_new: 0` on the ANE generated a token, a drafter's extra guesses failed the pass, and a prompt longer than the KV cache failed partway through the prefill.** `AneLm::generate` returns nothing for a zero budget, cuts a drafter's guesses to the budget it was asked for, and refuses a prompt the cache cannot hold before any pass runs. The loop now runs on a CPU stand-in in tests: greedy output with no, prompt-lookup, always-right, always-wrong and partly-right guesses at widths 1 to 16 equals the one-token-at-a-time output, a history-reading sampler is only shown positions that follow its own tokens, and a tapping drafter sees every fed token once, in order.
- **ANE inference read RoPE only from `rope_theta`, took logits from the embedding table when the head was missing, and ran any config the compiler would accept.** A config in the newer `rope_parameters` layout ran with plain RoPE whatever its type, and a plain one written as `{"rope_type": "default"}` was refused; both layouts are read now and a scaled type (yarn, llama3, linear, ...) is refused by name, as is any `layer_types` entry other than full attention. A config with `tie_word_embeddings: false` and no `lm_head.weight` is an error, a KV capacity past `max_position_embeddings` is refused, and every tensor the model needs is checked present before the first compile instead of after the LM head has compiled.
- **ANE training rotated every position at the wrong frequency for a config that writes `rope_parameters`.** The orchestrator read `rope_theta` and fell back to 10 000, so a Llama-3-style config in the new layout trained with 10 000 instead of 500 000. `DynamicAneTrainerConfig::from_model_config` reads the model shape from either layout, and the orchestrator uses it.
- **The ANE trainer's learning rate climbed back up after the last step.** It had its own cosine whose progress was not clamped, so a run past `max_steps` followed the cosine's next arch; it uses `pmetal_core`'s `LearningRateScheduler` now, which holds the floor.
- **A killed ANE training run could leave a truncated checkpoint.** `save_safetensors` wrote the destination in place; it writes beside it and renames over, so the previous checkpoint survives a crash or a full disk. Loading shares the inference engine's checkpoint reader (shards, dtypes, missing-tensor errors).
- **A full boot disk killed an ANE compile with "Failed to allocate memory for file backing".** The system's compiled-program cache lives on the boot volume and cannot be moved, and the error names neither the disk nor a size. A kernel that is not already cached is not compiled unless the volume has three times its weights plus 4 GiB free, and the error says what it saw and needed.
- **ANE training at a model width of 256 or more computed every weight gradient from the wrong operands.** At that width (`GPU_DW_MIN_DIM`) the trainer runs its `dW = dY^T X` GEMMs on the GPU, queued into one command buffer per step that executes after the backward pass, and it copied each GEMM's operands into one shared pair of scratch buffers as it queued it. By the time the buffer ran, the pair held the last GEMM's operands, so every GPU weight gradient in the step (the seven per layer) was a product of whichever activations were copied last, at its own shape. Each queued GEMM now stages its operands in buffers of its own (`DwGemm::queue_gemm_accum_staged`, with `ScratchPool` now handing out a distinct buffer per call until it is reset at the start of the next step), at the cost of holding a step's operands (about 30 MB per layer at width 1024 and 512 tokens) until the step ends. A test queues four GEMMs of different shapes, two with operands of equal length, into one buffer and compares each with the CPU result; it was off by 23 on the shared scratch and is exact on the staged one. The end-to-end ANE training tests all run at width 64, which is below the threshold, so they never reached this path.
- **`DwGemm`, the ANE trainer's f32 weight-gradient GEMM, no longer reads `C` when `beta` is 0, and runs on `simdgroup_matrix` fragments.** `C = alpha * A B^T + beta * C` multiplied the old contents of `C` by `beta` even when `beta` was 0, so a fresh, uninitialized output buffer that held a NaN came back as NaN. The kernel is now a 64 x 64 x 16 tiled SGEMM that feeds `simdgroup_multiply_accumulate` from lane-resident fragments and fetches the next k-block while the current one is multiplied. A float64 reference test covers shapes on and off the tile grid (down to 1 x 1 x 1 and up to 384 x 512 x 300) with four `alpha`/`beta` pairs each, and a NaN-filled output with `beta == 0`; the NaN test fails on the old kernel, and breaking the kernel's K-tail zero-fill fails the first.
- **Every Metal kernel dispatch leaked its command buffer.** Metal returns the command buffer autoreleased and a Rust thread has no autorelease pool, so each dispatch kept its buffer, encoders and resource retains alive until the thread exited: about 1.8 KiB per call, 18 MiB per 10,000 dispatches of the fused cross-entropy forward, without bound in a training or serving loop. Command buffers are now created inside a pool (`CommandQueueExt::new_command_buffer`); the same loop grows by 1.6 MiB.
- **The Metal pipeline cache could serve a pipeline built for different constants.** The key printed each function constant's value, so `UInt(1)` and `Float(1.0)` shared an entry, as did `0.0` and `-0.0`. The key carries the type and the exact bits.
- **The autotuner could pick a variant the GPU rejected.** The LoRA and merge benchmarks ignored the command buffer's error, and a rejected buffer completes almost at once, which made it look fastest. They return the error and the candidate is skipped; a heuristic that no candidate could confirm is no longer written to disk as a measured result.
- **`accelerate`'s safe wrappers read and wrote past short slices in release builds.** Their length checks were `debug_assert!`s, and `gemm` checked only the output. They are checks in every build, covering all three `gemm` operands, the 32-bit BLAS dimension limit and cross-entropy targets outside the vocabulary.
- **Empty `MetalBuffer`s, oversized zero-copy views and cleared private buffers.** `MetalBuffer::new(.., 0, ..)` and `from_slice(&[])` failed (Metal cannot allocate zero bytes), `zeros` on a private buffer never cleared it, and `metal_buffer_from_ptr` multiplied its length unchecked and trusted that Metal aliased the memory; it now rejects overflow and a copy.
- **Dynamic quantization treated every attention output projection as the output head, never saw GGUF layer numbers, and missed its bit budget.** `blk.N.attn_output.weight` contains "output", so each layer's attention output was held at the fallback precision as if it were `output.weight`; the attention and edge-layer options matched only Hugging Face names (`q_proj`, `layers.N.`), so with GGUF names (what `pmetal quantize` passes) they never applied; the bits-per-weight table was rounded up to whole numbers (Q4_K as 5 bits where it is 4.5, Q6_K as 7 for 6.56), so a `--target-bpw` was judged against figures 6 to 14 percent high; and the budget pass moved each tensor down one step at most, so a target a few steps below the calibrated types was reported as unreachable. The budget is now met by repeatedly taking the downgrade with the least added loss per bit saved across all tensors and scored types, and the bits are `8 x block bytes / block elements`.
- **Q4_0 and Q5_0 quantization had twice the error it should, and every quantizer padded a ragged tail with zeros.** The Q4_0 quantizer rounded `x/d + 8.5` to the nearest integer where the format's reference truncates it (and Q5_0 `x/d + 16.5`), which is a whole step away: every weight was rounded up, and the round-trip RMS error on a Gaussian tensor was 2.07 times the correct one (0.00350 against 0.00169 at sigma 0.02). The K-quant quantizers used an older, weaker scale search than the reference quantizers (the search over 20 candidate scales weighted by the block's own magnitude), Q8_K scaled by -128 instead of -127, and Q8_0/Q8_1 scaled by `127/amax` instead of the reference's `1/(amax/127)`, so their rounding differed at ties. All are rewritten after the reference algorithms; Q4_0, Q4_1, Q5_0, Q5_1, Q8_0, TQ1_0, TQ2_0 and MXFP4 now produce the reference's bytes exactly on a test tensor that includes zero, constant and outlier blocks and exact ties (`tests/quantize_reference.rs`; the reference implementation's own error bound for every format holds on its test signal). A tensor whose rows were not a whole number of blocks used to have its last block silently zero-filled, so `pmetal quantize` could write a model with zeroed weights; it is now an error, and `quantize::fit_to_row_length` picks the smaller-block type the reference falls back to.
- **A hostile or truncated GGUF file can no longer make the reader allocate what its header claims.** Counts, string lengths and array lengths are checked against the bytes the file has left before anything is reserved (a 24-byte file naming 100,000 tensors, a 512 MiB key or a billion-element array is refused at once; nested arrays are bounded the same way), and once the header is parsed every tensor's data range is checked against the file length, the alignment and, for quantized types, the block size of its rows. A tensor declared as 2^40 elements used to open fine and abort the process with a 4 TiB allocation when first read, and a download cut short opened without complaint and failed on whichever tensor came last; 25 of the 25 truncations of a small file were accepted. `general.alignment` must be a `uint32` power of two (the old reader ignored any other type and computed the wrong data offset), a big-endian file is reported as one, a bool other than 0 or 1 and an array of an unknown element type are errors, and strings are no longer stripped of trailing NULs, which silently changed tokenizer entries. The string and array caps are raised to 1 GiB and 16M elements, since the file size is now the guard (a full `tokenizer.json` is larger than the old 10 MB). `GgufContent` gains `tensors_by_offset` (read in disk order), `tensor_data_range`, `alignment` and `file_len`.
- **GGUF tensors in Q4_K, Q5_K and every IQ format decoded to the wrong values.** The Q4_K and Q5_K decoders read the nibbles in a layout of their own (low then high nibbles of the same 16 bytes, where the format puts the low nibbles of all 32 bytes first and the high nibbles after), and the Q5_K fifth bits from the wrong bytes; IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS interleaved the nibbles of each byte instead of taking the low nibbles for the first 16 elements; IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS, IQ2_S, IQ3_XXS, IQ3_S, IQ1_S and IQ1_M subtracted a bias the grids do not carry and unpacked scales and signs in invented layouts (several returned half the tensor). A GGUF model quantized to any of them, by any tool, loaded as noise. Every decoder is rewritten from the reference block definitions and checked bit-for-bit against the reference's output on pseudo-random blocks for all 26 decodable types (`tests/dequant_reference.rs`; 11 of them failed before). Legacy Q4_0 to Q8_1, Q2_K, Q3_K, Q6_K, Q8_K, Q1_0, TQ1_0/TQ2_0, MXFP4 and NVFP4 were already exact.
  - `dequant::dequantize` rejects a shape whose innermost dimension is not a whole number of blocks (a block never straddles a row) instead of truncating, adds `dequantize_into` to decode into a caller's buffer, and decodes large tensors on all cores, one block per work item.
- **Cosine with restarts ends at the floor.** The last step of a run, and every step after it, restarted at the peak learning rate, and cycle lengths that did not divide the run drifted from the reference. The schedule now follows transformers' hard-restart curve, `0.5 * (1 + cos(pi * ((cycles * progress) mod 1)))`, and sits at `min_lr` from progress 1 on. A grid test against the reference formula covers six run shapes, and a second one checks the linear, cosine and polynomial schedules.
- **A diverged run's events can be read back.** `serde_json` writes NaN and infinity as `null`, and `StepMetrics`, `EvalMetrics`, `TrainingState` and the bench, serve and inference metrics refused to read `null` as a number: `parse_event` failed on exactly the event a monitor needs when the loss goes NaN, and a checkpoint whose `TrainingState` had a NaN loss could not be loaded. Those fields now read `null` as NaN, and the metric structs fill in a missing field.
- **A job event is one write, and a write failure is not lost.** `write_event` gave `serde_json` the raw writer, which made 34 `write` calls for a one-line log event, so a `--log-events` file or a pipe shared by several processes could be read or interleaved mid-record. It now builds the record and writes it once. `JsonlSink` dropped every write error; it keeps the first.
- **`lora.loraplus_lr_ratio` reaches the trainer.** The orchestrator read it from the config and built the training loop with `None`, so LoRA+ never ran. A test on the loop config fails with the ratio forced back to `None`.
- **`--lr-schedule wsd` on `train-mtp`, `train-draft` and the Python MTP trainer trains with WSD, and a mistyped schedule is an error.** They read every name but `constant` and `linear` as cosine, as did the TUI and GUI for any unrecognised name. The arguments are typed `LrSchedulerType`, so clap rejects an unknown name and lists the valid ones.
