- **The GUI trains with the DoRA, rsLoRA and dropout its form asked for.** `start_training` built the `LoraConfig` from rank and alpha alone, and matched an unknown compression strategy to none. It calls the spec's own `lora_config`, `training_config` and `distributed_config`.
- **`pmetal train-diffusion --corrupted-only false` parses.** The flag was set-true with a default of true, so the documented whole-canvas loss could not be selected.
- **TAID is the paper's method (Shing et al., ICLR 2025).** The module mixed teacher and student in probability space with the student still in the autodiff graph, started near the teacher and moved toward the student (the reverse of the method), and "adapted" by a fixed schedule plus a per-sample sigmoid of the KL; on a two-token fixture its loss was 0.190 where the paper's objective at the same mixing weight is 0.146. `TaidDistiller` now mixes in logit space with the student detached (`softmax((1−t)·z_S + t·z_T)`), trains forward KL against that target, and moves `t` from `t_start` toward `t_end` by the momentum of the objective's relative improvement, floored by a linear schedule. Tests check the objective, `t = 1` equal to `KL(teacher || student)`, the gradient `(q − p_t)/N`, masked tokens and the `t` trajectory against the update rule in float64.
- **Universal Logit Distillation follows the paper and can be differentiated.** The loss compared cumulative sums of the sorted probabilities and averaged over the vocabulary; Boizard et al. (2024, eq. 5) sum the absolute differences of the sorted vectors themselves. It could not train at all: the student's sorting permutation sat inside the autodiff graph and MLX refuses the gradient of a gather's indices, so any `value_and_grad` over it failed. Checked against float64 for equal and unequal vocabularies, with and without top-k, and against finite differences.
- **Every `DistillLoss` detaches the teacher, divides a weighted mean by the total weight, and `Distiller` stops scaling logit MSE by `T²`.** A teacher forward inside the differentiated function sent gradient into the teacher through every loss. TVD, skewed JSD, MiniLLM, ULD and both ranking losses divided by `max(Σw, 1)`, so weights summing to less than one shrank the loss by their sum. Logit MSE was multiplied by `T²` although it never softens anything: sixteen times too large at `T = 4`. The generalized JSD (Agarwal et al., ICLR 2024) had its limits documented backwards and clamped `β = 0` and `1` to `1e-6`, which made the loss vanish; they are the forward and reverse KL now.
- **Hidden-state losses mask padding, survive zero vectors and honor `with_hidden_loss`.** They averaged over every position, the cosine loss took a bare `sqrt` whose gradient at a zero vector is NaN (one all-zero hidden vector poisoned the whole step), the reductions ran in bf16, and `DistillerBuilder::with_hidden_loss` was ignored in favour of the config's loss type. A layer pair beyond the layers the model returned is an error rather than a warning logged every step. Attention-map losses reduce in f32 too (a bf16 MSE came back as 0.0791 against 0.0788).
- **The hard-label loss is the runtime's f32 cross-entropy.** `Distiller` took `log_softmax` in the logits' dtype and gathered the result, so a bf16 student's loss was rounded to bf16 (0.017 nats off on a 61-token vocabulary). It also validates its config on build (a zero or NaN temperature built a divider by zero), checks the logit shapes before any op (a mismatch otherwise became a placeholder array and a number), and masks its telemetry.
- **Offline caches quantize per token, keep their shape, and refuse corrupt input.** 4-bit codes used one scale for the whole tensor, so a token spanning 60 logits and one spanning 1 shared sixteen levels sized for the former; a NaN or infinity set the scale of its row and was stored silently; a top-k cache returned `[batch·seq, vocab]` for `[batch, seq, vocab]`; an unknown compression name in the metadata loaded as uncompressed logits; a crash mid-write left a truncated sequence that `has_sequence` accepted. Verified by an error bound per token's own range, round-trips and the refusals.
- **Cross-vocabulary alignment drops teacher ids the student cannot score.** Where the teacher's vocabulary is larger, a top-k id past the student's was masked on the student side only, leaving the teacher's probability on a token with student probability zero: a loss of 998,551,424 where the support-restricted value is 0.058. The docs now say that ids are matched by value and mean something only for models sharing a tokenizer (`UniversalLogitLoss` is for the others).
- **Rationale distillation weights tokens by themselves.** Each token's entropy was normalized by the largest entropy in the batch, padding included, so a sample's loss changed with its neighbours; it is `1 + w·H/ln V` now. `generate_reasoning_mask` took `cumsum(start) − cumsum(end)`, which one stray closing marker (any window that begins inside a span) shifted for the rest of the row, hiding every later span; it is "the latest marker is a start" now.
- **Merge methods follow their papers.** Each was checked on hand-computed tensors from the paper's formula, and several did not match.
  - TIES elected the sign by counting votes and summed the agreeing entries; it elects by total mass (`sgn(Σ τ̂)`) and takes their mean over the agreeing set, as in Algorithm 1 (Yadav et al., 2023). DARE-TIES, DELLA and Breadcrumbs used the same consensus step.
  - DELLA was a different algorithm: it dropped large entries more often, derived probabilities from one softmax over the whole tensor and ignored `epsilon`. It is now MAGPRUNE (Deep et al., 2024): per row, the keep probability rises linearly with magnitude rank from `density − epsilon` to `density + epsilon` and each survivor is divided by its own probability.
  - Breadcrumbs ignored `density` and `gamma` for fixed percentiles and rescaled the survivors by `1/density`, which the paper does not; it removes the smallest `1 − density` and the largest `gamma` fraction of each layer's task vector and adds the rest.
  - Model Stock's two-model path projected the average onto a plane that contains it and returned the plain average, and N > 2 was a softmax-weighted average; it is `w_0 + t·(w_avg − w_0)` with `t = N·cosθ / (1 + (N−1)·cosθ)` (Jang et al., 2024). Orthogonal task vectors now stay at the pre-trained weights.
  - Multi-SLERP's tangent-space projections sum to zero by construction, so it always returned the normalized weighted mean (NLERP, 18.4 degrees where SLERP at t = 0.25 of orthogonal vectors is 22.5). It is the weighted Karcher mean, found by Riemannian gradient steps, and for two models exactly SLERP; it runs on the device.
  - Fisher merging divided by `ΣF + ε`, which is zero where no model has information (unused parameters, embedding rows of unseen tokens), and ignored the model coefficients; the floor is on each `F_i`, so such positions are the weighted mean. RegMean multiplied `[in, in]` Gram matrices by weights as if `[in, out]`, but checkpoints store `[out, in]`: the matmul threw on a non-square layer (a placeholder array outside a strict build), and a square one merged the transpose. It is `(Σ W_iG_i)(Σ G_i + ridge·I)⁻¹` with the paper's off-diagonal scale, an f64 Cholesky solve and an error where the old inverse silently returned the identity.
  - DARE was nondeterministic unless a seed was set (nothing set one) and, when seeded, every model and tensor reused one stream, so all models dropped the same entries; masks are keyed by seed, tensor name and model slot.
  - `weight`, `density`, `t` and `lambda` given as `filter` lists (attention vs MLP) were resolved against an empty tensor name and so always fell back to the default; they resolve against the tensor being merged.
- **The merge pipelines no longer hold whole checkpoints in memory, and the output is a loadable, atomic model.** `SafetensorsLoader` read every file of every model into memory before merging and panicked on a valid file whose tensor data was not element-aligned; it memory-maps, decodes without alignment assumptions and follows a `model.safetensors.index.json`. The writer buffered a 5 GB shard in memory, named later shards `model-00002.safetensors` with no index (which Hugging Face loaders cannot open), omitted the `format` metadata transformers requires, turned an unreadable tensor into an empty one and let float16 overflow become infinity; it streams, writes `model-0000i-of-0000N.safetensors` plus the index through renames, and errors on all three. A 1.5 GB, three-model merge peaked at 0.60 GB of memory against 2.34 GB (provisional, 1.2 s against 1.1 s). Merged tensors are checked on the device and a runtime op that threw (which leaves a placeholder array) fails the merge; sidecar copying leaves `.bin`, `.gguf` and hidden weight files of the source behind and a failed copy is an error.
- **Sliced merges (frankenmerges) work through `run_merge` and keep their config honest.** `run_merge` sent a sliced configuration down the flat path, which wrote nothing. The slice pipeline read a task-vector method's base at the output layer instead of the source layer (a wrong merge, silently), remapped a vision tower's layers, took the final norm and `lm_head` from the first slice, and left `config.json` with the source's layer count; it now writes `num_hidden_layers` and remaps `layer_types`, takes the output side from the last slice, and rejects layer ranges the model lacks and sources of unequal length.
- **LoRA folding honors rsLoRA, DoRA, `alpha_pattern`, both adapter layouts and quantized bases, and fails loudly.** The fold applied `lora_alpha / r` to PEFT-named adapters only: rsLoRA adapters (`alpha / sqrt(r)`) were folded at a scale `√r` too small, DoRA magnitude vectors were ignored, adapters written by `pmetal train` were not found, a quantized base failed on its `u32` dtype, and an adapter whose modules matched nothing in the base produced "success" and an unchanged model. DoRA renormalizes each output row to its magnitude, quantized weights are dequantized, folded and quantized again with their own group size and bits, a half pair or an unmatched module is an error, and outputs are atomic. Hand-computed folds for each case, all failing before, and a 4-bit base checked against the dense sum to within a quantization step.

- **`pmetal-vocoder`'s generator is BigVGAN, and the released checkpoints reproduce the reference waveform.** The anti-aliased activation never filtered (upsampling inserted zeros without interpolating, downsampling kept every other sample), SnakeBeta divided by `beta` without the reference's `1e-9` guard so a zero parameter gave `inf`, the AMP blocks were a different network (a plain chain per dilation with one residual around it and no dilation-1 convolution after each stage), the preset configurations were wrong (the 44.1 kHz model had seven stages and the wrong rates; the base model had the large model's), and a release `config.json` with `"fmax": null` did not even deserialise. The transposed convolution's initialisation used the wrong fan-in. Activations now use the reference's Kaiser-windowed sinc filters, blocks are `AMPBlock1` and `AMPBlock2` with their checkpoint tensor names, configurations are read as released, and the 14,025,154-parameter count of BigVGAN-base is the reference's.
  - The base and v2 24 kHz checkpoints reproduce the reference's waveform on a fixed mel to 6.4e-6 and 2.2e-5 (SNR 112 and 106 dB), and tiny random-weight generators in both block layouts, both activations, with and without final bias and tanh, match to 1e-4. A resampler at ratio 1 returned its input instead of filtering like the reference; its test failed with a 4.2 absolute error before the fix
- **`pmetal-vocoder`'s mel front end, STFT and window are the reference's.** The Hann window was symmetric where `torch.hann_window`, `scipy.signal.get_window` and so `torch.stft` use the periodic one (a 256-point STFT's magnitudes were off by up to 3% of the spectrum), there was no BigVGAN front end (the reference reflect-pads by `(n_fft - hop) / 2`, takes an uncentred STFT, forms `sqrt(re^2 + im^2 + 1e-9)` and clamps the log at `1e-5`; the old path centred the STFT, giving 24 frames where the reference gives 23 for 6000 samples, and used the bare magnitude), and `dynamic_range_compression` computed `log(1 + c x) / log(1 + c)` instead of `log(max(x, clip) c)`. `MelFrontEnd` reproduces the pipeline; the STFT frames with one gather and the inverse overlap-adds with shifted-block sums instead of a graph node per frame.
  - STFT magnitudes, a masked-spectrogram iSTFT, four filterbanks (Slaney, HTK, unnormalised, 5 bins) and the front end match `torch.stft`, `torch.istft`, librosa and the reference `mel_spectrogram`
- **`pmetal-vocoder`'s discriminators and losses are the reference's.** The MPD and MRD were one-dimensional stand-ins that treated the folded period or the frequency axis as channels; they are now the real two-dimensional networks, load `bigvgan_discriminator_optimizer.pt`, and have the reference's parameter counts (MPD 41,105,770, MRD 280,902, MBD 1,413,990). Feature matching averaged over all feature maps where the reference sums (about 40 times too small for the full model), and the multi-scale mel loss averaged three scales with the wrong windows and bins where BigVGAN-v2's sums seven (5 to 320 mel bins, 32 to 2048 windows, hop a quarter of the window). The MRD uses a rectangular window and the MBD removes DC and peak-normalises, as the reference does; digital silence gives finite gradients through both magnitudes.
  - Logits, feature maps, the three losses and the gradient of the generator-side loss with respect to the generated waveform match PyTorch's autograd (1e-4); the multi-scale mel loss and its gradient match too. Changing the MRD window or the MBD's DC removal fails them
- **`pmetal-mhc`'s gradients are exact, and a fresh layer is a plain residual connection.** Three of the layer's backward pieces were wrong and none of the old tests could tell, since they checked shapes and that the gradient was non-zero. The Sinkhorn backward carried an extra factor of the matrix entry in both normalizations' adjoints (finite differences on a 3x3 input gave -0.212 where it returned -0.183); the mappings' input gradient, through the RMSNorm backward that lost a factor of 1/rms, came out as 1.1 where finite differences gave 4.2 on a stream of RMS ~2; and `MhcLayer::backward` never differentiated through `h_in = H^pre x`, passed a zero gradient for `H^pre`, and handed back `grad_h_out` as if it were the sublayer's input gradient. All three are rebuilt: the Sinkhorn backward replays the forward and runs each step's adjoint in reverse, and `backward` takes the sublayer's backward pass as a closure, because it has to run between the post-res and pre halves. The initialization was also off: `b_pre = 1/n` and `b_post = 1` are inputs to a sigmoid, so a fresh layer scaled its input by 2.2 and its output by 1.46, and `b_res = I` projected to a matrix with a 0.475 diagonal; they now give `H^pre = 1/n`, `H^post = 1` and an `H^res` within 3e-4 of the identity per entry (diagonal 0, off-diagonal -8, the initialization the published variants use)
  - The Sinkhorn forward is the released DeepSeek-V4 recipe: a row softmax in place of `exp`, which overflowed `f32` (NaN) for logits above 88 and is now stable to 1e30, `+ ε` as the release adds it (to `H^pre` too), and a fixed iteration count, so the early-exit tolerance and the `converged` flag are gone and the gradient is that of what ran
  - `tests/reference_gradients.rs` checks the forward, the input gradient and every parameter gradient (`α`, `b`, `φ`) of the mappings and of a whole layer around a `tanh` sublayer, in dynamic and static mode, against central finite differences of an independent float64 transcription of the paper's equations, to 5e-4 relative; the Sinkhorn adjoint on its own at 1, 2, 5, 7 and 20 iterations. Reverting each of the three fixes fails those tests, and the old code fails them. `tests/properties.rs` adds property tests (finite, non-negative, columns exact for logits to ±60; invariant to row shifts) and checks bf16-rounded streams, scales from 1e2 to 1e30 and zero input
- **`#[derive(JobSpec)]` handles generic specs, and rejects attributes it used to ignore.** A generic spec failed to compile with an error inside the expansion, a `r#type` field was named "r#type" in its descriptor, a NaN passed a bounded float field's validation (`NaN < min` and `NaN > max` are both false), and a `kind` that was not an identifier panicked the macro. Attributes that did nothing were accepted silently: `invert` without `flag`, `min` on a string, `enum_options` without `kind = "enum"`, a `Vec<String>` with `argv` and no `csv`, a repeated key, two defaults. Each is now a compile error at the key at fault, and all of a struct's errors are reported in one compile; float fields reject NaN and infinities, integer bounds keep all 64 bits, and `required` covers `Option<_>` and `Vec<String>`. The unused `default_const` key is gone. Unit tests cover each rejection, an integration test in `pmetal-core` derives specs under `#![deny(warnings)]`, and 12 compile-fail snapshots (trybuild) pin the messages and their spans
- **`eval()` no longer swallows the error of the op before it.** `eval` read the pending bridge error into the token it returns, which clears it from both error channels, and nearly every caller drops the token (`x.eval();`), so a failed op followed by an eval of its result lost the failure for good: the placeholder evaluated, nothing was reported, and the loss that came out of it was a number from a different computation. `eval` and `async_eval` now only look at the last-op slot; a dropped token leaves the error for the next check, and a token that is read still reports it, once. Two tests (a dropped token, a read one) pin both; the first failed on the old code.
- **A second Gemma 4 model in the process no longer replays the first one's compiled blocks.** The compiled attention, shared-KV attention, MLP and per-layer-input blocks trace once per cache key and replay the trace on a hit, and each key left out values the trace bakes in: the three norm epsilons, the RoPE base and the rotated width. A model (or a second layer type) with the same geometry and different values of those ran the first one's, silently. The keys are now one struct per block that holds every baked value, compared with a defaulted `operator==`, so a value added to the block is added in one place.
  - Thirteen tests run a baseline, then one changed value, then the changed call under a key that differs for an unrelated reason (a longer KV capacity the attention mask makes irrelevant, or a second batch row), and require the second to equal the third. All thirteen failed on the old keys (the replayed output differed from a fresh trace by 1.6e-4 to 1.9) and pass on the new ones.
- **A scalar combined with an integer array no longer truncates, and bad axes, section counts and dtype codes are errors instead of panics, allocations or float32.** `mul_scalar(0.5)` on an int32 array cast the 0.5 to int32 first and multiplied by zero, silently; a fractional scalar (or one the dtype can't hold) now stays f32 and promotes the array, as NumPy does, while a whole number that fits keeps the integer dtype (float and bf16 peers are unchanged). `swap_axes` with an out-of-range axis panicked on a Rust index and now goes through MLX's `swapaxes` and its error; `split_sections(0)` indexed an empty buffer, a negative count wrapped to a huge allocation and a billion sections asked for 128 GB, and all are now MLX's error with an empty result; a dtype code outside the table (`zeros(&[2], 99)`) came back as float32 and is now an error. `InlineArray` is `repr(transparent)` over its buffer, which the code that passes a `Vec<InlineArray>` to C++ as a run of buffers had been assuming.
  - Each case is a test with hand-computed values (`[1, 2, 3] * 0.5` is `[0.5, 1.0, 1.5]`, `u8 + 300` promotes instead of wrapping), and `build.rs` now reruns when `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET` or the FetchContent variables change, which it did not.
- **Saving safetensors is atomic, writes the path it was given, and says when it failed.** `Array::save_safetensors` returned `()`, so a missing directory or a full disk went unnoticed by every caller that wrote a checkpoint, fused model or optimizer state; it now returns a `Result` and every caller propagates it. MLX's own path overload also appends `.safetensors` to a name without it, so the best-snapshot writer's `best_snapshot.safetensors.tmp` was written to `best_snapshot.safetensors.tmp.safetensors` and the rename that followed failed on a file that was never there. The file is now written beside its destination as `<path>.partial`, forced to disk (`F_FULLFSYNC` on macOS) and renamed over it, so an interrupted save leaves the previous file intact and the partial one is removed on failure. A duplicate tensor name, which the map the save builds silently dropped, is an error.
  - Tests cover the exact-path write, bit-for-bit round trips of f32, bf16 and u32, a duplicate name leaving the previous file untouched, a rename failure removing its partial file, an unwritable directory and a NUL in a name. The trainer's best-snapshot writer drops its own temp-and-rename, which the bridge now does.
- **The compiled fused ops and decode blocks are safe to call from several threads.** MLX keeps its compiled traces in one process-wide table it does not lock, and the bridge's own per-block caches are plain vectors of the same kind: two threads building graphs through `fused_swiglu`, the compiled attention and MLP blocks or a `CompiledFn` at once could corrupt either, and an entry is marked filled before its tape is built, so a second thread that found it in between replayed an empty tape. Every entry point that calls a compiled function now holds one recursive lock while it builds the graph (evaluation, which is where the time goes, stays outside it), so a server with two model threads, or `cargo test` running tests in parallel, no longer shares an unguarded cache. A 16-thread test (half on their own streams, half on the default one) runs every op on three dtypes through first calls and replays and checks the values against the formulas; I could not make it fail on the old code, the race window being a few microseconds, so it is a guard rather than a reproduction.
- **A panic in a loss, a checkpointed block or a compiled closure reaches the caller instead of aborting the process, and none of the three leaks arrays any more.** MLX calls these Rust closures from inside its own C++ frames, and a panic that unwinds out of an `extern "C"` trampoline aborts: a bug in a loss killed a training run with no chance to save a checkpoint. The trampolines now catch the panic, MLX unwinds the abandoned call with an exception the bridge reports, and the panic continues from `value_and_grad`, `checkpoint_apply` or `CompiledFn::call` with its original payload. Separately, `value_and_grad` gave C++ a loss slot and one slot per parameter that already held live scalar arrays and C++ constructed over them without destroying them, so every step leaked one array per parameter plus one; `CompiledFn::call` did the same with its `n_outputs_max` output slots on every call (the compiled Qwen3-Next decode path uses it), and its trampoline wrote past the output buffer when a closure returned more arrays than `n_outputs_max`. All the slots now start uninitialised, and a closure that returns too many outputs is an error.
  - Active MLX memory over 200 steps of 32 parameters grew by 26,400 bytes (200 x 33 x 4) before and by 0 after; over 5,000 `CompiledFn` calls with four output slots it grew by 40,008 bytes before and by 0 after. The panic tests abort the test binary ("non-unwinding panic. aborting") with the catch removed.
- **Reading a scalar back from a bf16, f16 or integer array gives its value, and the array constructors no longer read past the slice they are given.** `item_f32` and `item_u32` took the first four bytes of the buffer whatever the dtype, so a bf16 `3.0` came back as 2.3e-41 and an int32 `7` as 1e-44: any loss, norm or sampled id logged from a half-precision array was garbage. They now convert like `astype` (and there is an `item_i32`, which `item::<i32>()` uses, so a negative integer no longer rides through `u32`). Separately, `from_f32_slice`, `from_u32_slice`, `from_u8_slice` and `from_u16_bits_slice` handed MLX a pointer and a shape and MLX copied `product(shape)` elements, so a slice shorter than its shape was an out-of-bounds read; `slice` and `slice_set` read `start.len()` entries from `stop`'s pointer the same way. All of them now panic with the shape and the lengths. `dim` with an axis out of range threw a C++ exception through an `extern "C"` frame and aborted the process; it panics like indexing, and `shape` costs one FFI call instead of two.
  - Hand-computed checks: every dtype from int8 to int64 and bf16/f16 read back `3.0`, `-5.5` and `-5` exactly, and each short-slice and bad-axis case fails with its message. The new tests fail on the previous code (bf16 `item_f32` returned 2.3e-41; the slice cases did not panic; `dim(3)` aborted the test binary).
- **`pmetal quantize` keeps a Qwen3.5 model's shared-expert gate out of the low-bit allocation.** The quantizer saves MoE router gates (`*.gate.weight`) at full precision as critical tensors, but the shared expert's gate (`*.shared_expert_gate.weight`, `[1, hidden]`, one logit per token scaling the whole shared expert in every layer) does not end in `.gate.weight`, so it went through the bit allocator and could land at 3 or 4 bits. `mlx_lm` keeps it at 8 bits beside the router; it is a critical tensor here now
- **Gemma 4 E2B and E4B prefill ran in f32 after the first layer.** A multi-token pass through a layer with per-layer inputs computed the gate's activation with `gelu_approx`, whose f32 scalars promoted a bf16 residual stream to f32, and every layer after it ran its matmuls on f32 activations (casting its bf16 weights per op) and returned f32 logits. The activation and its product with the layer's input now go through `fused_geglu_tanh`, which keeps the gate's dtype, as the compiled single-token block does. A test casts the dense, per-layer-input and MoE fixtures to bf16 and checks that a multi-token pass returns bf16 logits and leaves bf16 keys and values in every cache; the per-layer-input one failed on f32 logits. On Gemma 4 E4B 4-bit, with the prompt in passes of 2048, 4096 tokens prefill at 1150 to 1200 tok/s in 5.2 GB, against 860 to 940 tok/s in 6.2 GB before this and 730 tok/s in 10.3 GB before the passes, on an M4 Max, provisional
- **The native Qwen routed layers pick their experts from an f32 softmax, as transformers does.** The router softmaxed in the model's dtype, so on a bf16 checkpoint the probabilities were rounded to 8 bits before the top-k was taken and the kept ones rounded again when renormalized; transformers' `Qwen3MoeTopKRouter` and `Qwen3_5MoeTopKRouter` compute the softmax in f32 (`softmax(logits, dtype=torch.float)`), renormalize in f32, and cast the weights to the model dtype last. `pmetal_runtime::native_moe::route_top_k` does that for the Qwen3.5 family's prefill and its compiled single-token layer alike. A test routes bf16 logits and compares the weights to f64 on the host rounded to bf16 once: they match exactly, where the same logits through a bf16 softmax and sum land on other bf16 values (the test checks that they would)
- **The native Qwen engine loads the checkpoints `mlx_lm` writes: stacked experts, quantized gates, and the float quantization modes.** A routed Qwen3.5-family checkpoint in MLX's layout (what every `mlx-community` conversion is) did not load: `mlx_lm` stacks the experts as `switch_mlp.{gate,up,down}_proj`, and the loader only knew the fused layout and one module per expert, so it failed on "missing expert weight". Once it did, the router and the shared expert's gate, which `mlx_lm` quantizes at 8 bits (group size 64) through the model's `quant_predicate`, were read as dense matrices (`uint32` bytes), and a checkpoint quantized in mxfp4 or nvfp4 was read as affine, which wants biases it does not have. The loader now follows MLX's rule for every module, the embedding and the head included: a module is packed exactly when `{module}.scales` is in the weights, read with the mode, bits and group size its entry in the `quantization` block gives (affine has `biases`; mxfp4, nvfp4 and mxfp8 are scales alone), and a block and weights that disagree are an error naming the module, on this engine and Gemma 4's. Experts load from the fused layout, `switch_mlp`, or one module per expert, dense or packed, through one path (the per-expert one stacks with `LayerWeight::stack`, tensor scales included), which replaces three hand-written stacking branches. `NativeWeights` holds its embedding as an `EmbeddingWeight` (`embed_w`), as Gemma 4's does, in place of the `embed_w`, `embed_scales`, `embed_biases` and `quantization_config` fields and the four copies of the quantized embedding and tied-head code in the forward passes, DFlash's native target and the decision backbone, which now call `embed_tokens` and `lm_head`. Checked on the small fp32 routed model: its transformers-written fused layout, one module per expert, `mlx_lm`'s stacked layout, and `mlx_lm`'s 4-bit affine and mxfp4 conversions (gates affine at 8 bits) against the same weights dequantized to dense, all within 1.2e-5 of the base's logits, where the three MLX layouts did not load before; and against transformers' `Qwen3_5MoeForConditionalGeneration` over a whole prompt and in passes, to the parity tolerance. NVIDIA's NVFP4 Qwen3.6-35B-A3B and the 4-bit Qwen3-1.7B load and pick the same first token as before the change
- **The Qwen3 and Gemma 4 native engines refuse configs they would have run as a different model, and read a Gemma 4 `config.json` the way transformers does.** A Qwen3 or Qwen3.5-family config with `use_sliding_window: true` ran with full attention in every layer, and one with `attention_bias: true` loaded without its attention biases; both are now refused by name. For Gemma 4, `use_bidirectional_attention: "all"`, `attention_bias: true` and a `hidden_activation` other than tanh-approximate GELU (checked only when per-layer inputs were on) are refused, and the keys a config leaves out take `Gemma4TextConfig`'s defaults rather than the engine's own: no final-logit softcap (the engine applied 30), a sliding window of 512 (it used 1024), and layers in the default five-sliding-to-one-full pattern with the last forced to full attention. The released checkpoints name every one of these keys, so they are unaffected
- **The native Llama 4 engine loads the released checkpoints.** It refused Scout's and Maverick's `config.json` before reading a weight (`missing field hidden_size`): the multimodal config nests the text model under `text_config`, and the parser also demanded its fields at the top level. A nested config now resolves to one text config, with `tie_word_embeddings`, which the release keeps at the top, applied to it
  - The engine now reads the layouts the releases and MLX conversions ship through the shared checkpoint reader: `language_model.model.*` beside vision tensors, transformers' fused experts, MLX's stacked `[E, out, in]` projections, and every projection, the router, the experts and the embeddings packed (the tied head runs on the packed table). It ignored `num_experts_per_tok` and routed every token to one expert; it now sums the gated outputs of the top `k` as transformers' router defines (the releases use 1). Packed attention decodes through the per-op path, since the compiled single-token graph takes dense matrices. The trunk dtype no longer comes from `embed_tokens.weight`, which is the packed payload on a quantization
  - The prefill's chunk mask is built on the device: only the two chunk-id vectors come from the host, where it was an `s x s` loop and a 268 MB upload at 8192 tokens, followed by one conversion to an additive bias per layer. Building it takes 2.5 ms at 8192 tokens against 33 ms
  - A small fp32 model in the released layout (nested config, tied head, vision tensors, a dense layer among MoE ones, top-2 routing, chunked attention) matches transformers to 3e-7 over a prompt and a cached decode through the compiled graph and the per-op path; routing to one expert only moves its logits by 0.23. A 4-bit MLX-style quantization of it gives its dequantized dense twin's logits to 6.3e-7
- **The native DeepSeek engine computes what transformers' DeepSeek-V2 and V3 compute.** It had never run a MoE layer: the routed experts were gathered with `[E, out, in]` weights where `gather_mm` takes `[E, in, out]`, an op that throws and hands back a placeholder array; the group-limited routing that V3 and R1 ship (8 groups, 4 kept) crashed on its first top-k; prefill attention had no causal mask, so every prompt token saw its future; and a V2 checkpoint (no `e_score_correction_bias`, softmax scores) could not load. The engine now takes both families' routing from the config, sigmoid with the correction bias and group-limited `noaux_tc`, or softmax with the plain or the group-max top-k, with the gate and the scores in `f32` as transformers computes them
  - The attention cache is in the model's dtype (it was always bfloat16, so an fp32 or fp16 model lost precision or mismatched), the expert and projection weights load packed from an MLX quantization (the router gates, which MLX writes at 8 bits, and `kv_b_proj`, which MLX conversions dequantize before splitting, are unpacked at load), a V3-style config's `n_group: null` reads as 1, and a trunk tensor the engine does not read (DeepSeek-V3.2's sparse-attention indexer, attention biases) is refused by name instead of ignored. Layers past the trunk (V3's multi-token-prediction layer) are skipped by index rather than by the literal `61`
  - Checked against transformers 5.19 on three small fp32 models in the layout the releases ship (per-expert tensors, a trailing prediction layer, YaRN with `mscale` 2): V3 with 16 experts in 4 groups, 2 kept; V2 with the plain and with the group-limited top-k and a direct Q projection. Logits agree to 1e-6 over a whole prompt, a cached decode, and prefill chunks that start past position 0; dropping the causal mask moves them by 1.2 and dropping the group limit by 0.17. A 4-bit MLX-style quantization of the V3 model (embeddings, projections, gates at 8 bits, stacked experts) gives its dequantized dense twin's logits to 1e-6
  - `mlx-community/DeepSeek-V2-Lite-Chat-4bit-mlx` runs packed (experts, shared experts, `kv_b_proj` and embeddings in 4 bits) and matches the MLX implementation that wrote it on the same weights: per-position logit cosine at least 0.9998 over 16 tokens, and the greedy reply to a chat prompt, "The capital of France is Paris.", token for token
  - On the released DeepSeek-V2-Lite in bf16 (27 layers, 64 experts), the engine is as close to transformers' fp32 as transformers' own bf16 run is, at 2, 4, 8, 14, 20 and 27 layers: the worst position's cosine to fp32 is 0.99979 at full depth against bf16 transformers' 0.99985, and 0.99963 against 0.99922 at 8 layers
- **TurboQuant's Lloyd-Max codebooks above 4 bits were four to five times above the optimum distortion.** The Beta-density codebook was solved on an 8192-point grid over [-1, 1] with 64 sweeps; a coordinate of a unit vector in 128 dimensions has a standard deviation of 0.088, so an 8-bit cell held about two grid points and the solver converged to the fixed point of the grid, not of the density. The 8-bit codebook at dimension 128 had a distortion of 1.86e-4 where the optimum is 4.0e-5, and from 5 bits up the distortion stopped falling by four per bit (`pmetal_runtime::turboquant::beta_codebook`, also used by pmetal-mlx's cache). It now integrates the density on a million-cell grid over nine standard deviations and sweeps to a billionth of a standard deviation, symmetric
  - Against an independent evaluation each centroid is the mean of its own cell to 2e-4 standard deviations, and the distortion matches the tabulated Gaussian Lloyd-Max optimum to 0.5% for 1 to 6 bits; each bit above 6 divides it by at least 3.9 at dimensions 64, 128 and 256
- **TurboQuant keys at 4 bits or fewer wrecked a real model's perplexity.** On Qwen3-0.6B over 1,600 tokens of text with a 64-token window, the 4-bit cache raised perplexity by 273% (top-1 agreement with the bf16 cache 50%) and the 3-bit one by 999%, though attention error on random keys had looked fine. Real keys carry a large component shared by every token of a head, which takes most of each key's norm, and channels whose spread differs by an order of magnitude from the queries' own. The quantizer's error is as large in every direction, so it buried the logits' small spread. The cache now calibrates a frame per KV head on the first tokens it quantizes: the keys' mean is taken off, and each channel is divided by its spread (limited to 16x either way of the head's typical one). The query is multiplied by the same spread, so the logits do not change, and the mean's contribution (a constant added to every logit of a head) is taken off the dense sinks and recent tokens' scores and the sink logits inside the kernel, where the softmax does not see it. With the frame, the same text costs +0.3% at 4 bits (96% agreement), +2.3% at 3 bits (92%), and 0.0% to -0.2% at 6 and 8 bits (`center_keys` and `scale_key_channels` on `TurboQuantConfig`, on by default; `QuantizedKvCache::dequantize_keys` returns the keys undone). A first run shorter than 16 tokens is stored without a frame, since a frame taken from a few tokens is mostly those tokens
- **TurboQuant decode attention from a position past the last cached token panicked (a wrapped index in release builds) instead of returning an error.** It now returns "no cached token to attend to"
- **SGD, Lion and Adafactor train a bf16 or f16 parameter as AdamW now does, and the InfoNCE loss picks the labelled similarity.** The three optimizers did their arithmetic in the parameter's dtype (Lion and SGD) or rounded back to it every step (Adafactor), so a bf16 weight whose update was smaller than half a bf16 step never moved: 300 steps of lr 1e-4 left one at exactly 1.0 under each. They now share AdamW's f32 master copy (`MasterWeight`) and live beside it in `pmetal_runtime::compat::optimizers`, where the trainer's re-exports find them; the plain `Sgd` the tests use no longer reads its learning rate back from the GPU on every update. SGD takes `with_nesterov`, checked against `torch.optim.SGD` over three steps with and without it. `info_nce_loss` (embedding training) went through a bridge helper documented to take class indices that computed the soft-target loss: it weighted every row's log-probabilities by the *labels* 0, 1, 2, ... instead of picking the labelled one, so on two orthonormal pairs at temperature 1 it returned 0.813 where the loss is log(1 + 1/e) = 0.313. It now calls the language-model loss, and a test checks a three-row batch against its definition.
- **AdamW trains a bf16 or f16 parameter instead of rounding its updates away.** The optimizer did its arithmetic in the parameter's dtype: a learning-rate-sized update to a bf16 weight near 1 (bf16 steps are 2⁻⁸ wide there) rounded back to the weight every time, `1 − lr·λ` and β₂ = 0.999 both rounded to 1.0, and the moments were bf16 too. In a test, 300 steps of lr 1e-4 left a bf16 weight at exactly 1.0 where AdamW takes it to 0.97. The moments are now f32 and a half-precision parameter is updated through an f32 master copy it is rounded from (`AdamState::master`), as mixed-precision training does; a parameter replaced between steps (a checkpoint load, an adapter merge) starts the master over from the new value. f32 parameters, which is every LoRA adapter, are untouched. A test follows a bf16 parameter 300 steps against `torch.optim.AdamW` written out in f64 (the master agrees to 1e-4), and checks the dtypes and the reseeding.
- **`GroupNorm` groups consecutive channels, as PyTorch's does, so the image VAE normalizes the groups its weights were trained on.** The layer defaulted to MLX's own layout, which interleaves the channels (channel `c` joins group `c % G`) and normalizes a PyTorch-trained network's activations over the wrong sets of channels; the VAE behind the Flux pipeline builds its norms with the default. The interleaved layout and its `pytorch_compatible` switch are gone, the statistics are taken in f32 and the output keeps the input's dtype (the affine step promoted a bf16 activation against f32 parameters). A test checks the layer against `torch.nn.GroupNorm` written out in f64 on a `[2, 3, 4, 8]` input with four groups, which the old default missed by 0.12 on the first element. The unused `compat::losses::CrossEntropy` (documented as taking class indices, it computed the soft-target loss), `nn::cross_entropy` and the `Sequential` stub that held no layers are removed.
- **The language-model loss is computed in f32 and clipping keeps gradients in their dtype.** `causal_lm_loss` and `cross_entropy_loss` took the `logsumexp` of bf16 logits in bf16, which rounds the normalizer to 8 mantissa bits: on a 100,000-token vocabulary single tokens' losses were off by up to 0.07 nats (0.12 on 152k), and the softmax in the backward by as much as 2%. They now read each row once in f32, whatever the logits' dtype, as `transformers` does by upcasting. A Metal kernel does it (`pmetal_runtime::inline_array::logit_row_stats`: each row's logsumexp, target logit and logit sum, with the logits' gradient written once, in their dtype), so no f32 copy of the `[tokens, vocab]` logits or of their gradient is ever held: on 4,096 tokens of bf16 logits over a 151,936-token vocabulary a forward and backward took 12.8 ms and a peak of 2.5 GB (the logits are 1.24), where the bf16 version took 29 ms and 3.7 GB and an f32 version built from MLX ops 72 ms and 7.5 GB (an M4 Max under other load, so provisional). `clip_grad_norm` squared and summed gradients in their own dtype, so an f16 gradient above 256 overflowed its square and made the global norm `inf` (every gradient then clipped to zero), and it promoted bf16 and f16 gradients to f32; the norm is now summed in f32, returned as f32, and each gradient is scaled in f32 and rounded back to its dtype. Tests check the loss of 100,000-class bf16 logits against f64, the kernel's three statistics and its gradient for f32, f16 and bf16 over vocabularies that don't divide the kernel's 256 threads, a second derivative against the op-built one, and clipping of f32, bf16 and f16 gradients.
- **LoRA dropout under gradient checkpointing is recomputed with the mask the forward used.** A checkpointed block runs its forward again during the backward pass, and the dropout mask was drawn from MLX's global key, which had moved on: the backward differentiated a network with another mask than the one the loss came from (in a one-layer test the two gradients differed by 87 where the gradients are of order 10). Adapter dropout now draws from a counter-based stream of its own, `compat::random::dropout_mask`, and `compat::checkpointed` starts every recompute from where the forward's draws began and hands the stream back as it found it; `random::seed` resets it. A test compares a dropout adapter's gradients plain and checkpointed from one seed, and checks that two seeds draw different masks.
- **`relu` and `leaky_relu` return a bf16 or f16 input's dtype, and `leaky_relu` is leaky for any slope.** Both compared against an f32 zero or slope, which promoted the activation's output to f32, and `leaky_relu` computed `max(x, slope·x)`, which is `slope·x` below zero only for a slope of at most 1 (for 2.0 it returned `x` on the negatives). A test runs every activation but GELU on bf16 and f16 and checks the dtype, and `leaky_relu` against its definition at four slopes.
- **A LoRA-adapted layer returns the activations' dtype, and DoRA no longer differentiates its norm.** The adapter's f32 tensors promoted every adapted layer's output to f32 on a bf16 model (as did the dropout mask), so the residual stream ran in f32 from the first adapted layer on, with the activations the backward keeps twice as wide. The adapter still computes in f32, as PEFT's does, and the layer's output is rounded once, to the base product's dtype. Merging an adapter into a bf16 weight left an f32 one for the same reason, and an adapter on an FP8 base weight multiplied by its bytes instead of its values; the base product and the merge now unpack the weight first. DoRA took the gradient through `‖W + s·B·A‖`, which the paper (section 4.3) and PEFT hold constant, and for that built a dense `[out, in]` copy of the update on every forward and kept it for the backward; the norm is now a constant to autograd and is computed from `‖wᵢ‖² + 2s·bᵢ·(A·wᵢ) + s²·bᵢ(AAᵀ)bᵢᵀ`, so no dense update is formed; the folded weight cut cross-entropy multiplies by over an adapted LM head holds the norm constant too, where it gave `A` a different gradient (0.81 against 0.91 on one element in a test). Tests check an adapted bf16 layer's dtype with and without DoRA and dropout, DoRA's output and its `A`, `B` and magnitude gradients against the paper's definition written out densely with a constant norm, bf16 merging and unmerging, and an FP8 base against its unpacked weight.
- **A LoRA `A` matrix starts uniform on `±1/√in_features`, as PEFT's does.** It started 1.7 times wider (`±√(3/in_features)`), and under rsLoRA on `±1/√rank`, which does not depend on the layer's width at all: 16 times wider than PEFT's at rank 16 on a 4,096-wide layer. `A` sets the size of `B`'s gradient, so those runs trained `B` at a different effective rate than the recipe they followed. rsLoRA changes the adapter's scale (`alpha/√rank`) and nothing else, per the paper. A test draws `A` at 4,096 and 512 input features and checks its bound and its variance against the uniform distribution's.
- **The ANE and CPU hybrid engines ignored most sampling settings.** `infer --ane`, `serve --ane` and DFlash on the ANE (`AneLm`), and the CPU hybrid engine they use for dense Qwen3.5, sampled greedily or top-k at the request's temperature with an RNG of their own, and silently dropped top-p, min-p and the repetition, frequency and presence penalties; the CPU hybrid engine also kept the seed of the first request it served. Both now hand each position's logits to `pmetal_models::HostLogitsSampler`, which samples them through the GPU path's `Sampler` and `DecodePenalties`, so a token is sampled and penalized as `generate_cached` would sample it, and the `n`-th token of a seeded generation takes the same draw. Verifying guesses (DFlash, prompt lookup) asks the sampler for each position only while it agrees with the guess before it, so every position is penalized for exactly the tokens before it and a rejected guess never reaches the history: guesses still change only the speed. Public API: `AneLm::generate` and `Qwen3NextInferenceEngine::generate_cached{,_streaming}` take a `&mut dyn TokenSampler` (`pmetal_metal::ane::lm`, with `Greedy` for an argmax); `GenerateOptions` and `Qwen3NextInferenceConfig` lose `temperature`, `top_k` and `seed`, `set_generation_params` takes only the token limit and end token, and `pmetal_metal::ane::lm::{sample, sampling_rng}` are gone
  - On Qwen3-0.6B (fp16 weights), greedy decoding on the ANE under a repetition penalty of 1.3, frequency 0.4 and presence 0.3 picks the tokens `generate_cached` picks on the GPU, and other ones than without them; with prompt-lookup guesses it picks the same tokens as without. On Qwen3-4B with the 8-bit DFlash drafter, greedy decoding under penalties and seeded top-k/top-p sampling under penalties each emit the same tokens with and without drafting. On the tiny Qwen3.5 fixture, the CPU hybrid engine picks the greedy tokens of every other engine under each penalty setting, and a seeded top-k/top-p/min-p generation under penalties draws `generate_cached`'s tokens. Not recording sampled tokens, or sampling a position past a rejected guess, fails these tests
  - Sampling on the GPU costs little next to an ANE pass. On Qwen3-4B (int8, M4 Max, 41 ms a token), greedy decoding under penalties takes 0.84 ms a token to sample and top-k/top-p sampling under penalties 2.4 ms, against 0.23 ms for a host argmax. Greedy decoding without penalties, which has nothing to apply, still takes the argmax on the host
- **`--repetition-penalty` did nothing on the native engine or on `pmetal infer`'s `DynamicModel` path.** The native decode loop (Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama 4, DeepSeek, gpt-oss, Gemma 4) applied only the frequency and presence penalties, and those one token late and never counting the first generated token; the pipelined `DynamicModel` loop applied none of the three, and `pmetal serve`'s single-request loop penalized each token for every earlier one but the last. All three now apply the penalties on the GPU (`pmetal_runtime::decode::DecodePenalties`), with every sampled token taken into the history without a host sync, so the decode stays pipelined. The repetition penalty covers the prompt and the output, dividing a repeated token's positive logit and multiplying a negative one, as transformers' `RepetitionPenaltyLogitsProcessor` does; frequency and presence count the generated tokens. Model presets carrying a `repetition_penalty` in `generation_config.json` now take effect there too
  - On the tiny Qwen3.5 fixture, greedy decoding under a repetition penalty of 1.3, a reward of 0.7, and 1.1 with frequency 0.4 and presence 0.3 picks the same 24 tokens on the native engine, the pipelined loop and the `Sampler`'s one-token-at-a-time decode, and different ones than without the penalties. Leaving the prompt out of the history, or not counting the sampled tokens, fails it
  - The native engines' `prefill_first_token` returns the generation's `DecodeSampler`, which `generate` continues; the Qwen engine's temperature-only priming and decode variants, which no caller used, are gone
- **MTP heads drafted from the wrong hidden state.** Qwen3Next's bundled predictor and Gemma 4's assistant are trained to read the target's hidden state at position `i` beside the embedding of the token after it, `x[i + 1]`, and to predict `x[i + 2]` (DeepSeek-V3's multi-token prediction, and how the reference implementations serve these heads). pmetal paired that state with `x[i]` instead, so each round's first draft asked the head for the token the target was about to produce anyway, and the Gemma assistant also attended to a cache that already held the newest token. Teacher-forced on Qwen3.5-0.8B's bundled head over a greedy reply, the trained pairing predicts the token after next right 83% of the time; the old one got the next token right 58% of the time. A round now starts from the target's own next token, which it hasn't run yet, drafts the tokens after it, and runs that token and the drafts in one target forward, which emits the accepted drafts and one more token of the target's; the old loop spent a second target forward on that last token. Gemma 4 31B (NVFP4) with its released assistant writes a 192-token Fibonacci function in 35 target forwards instead of 63, in 70.7 s instead of 139.3 s, with the same text. The acceptance `infer` reports counts only the head's drafts now, not the target's own next token
  - Greedy MTP output is checked token for token against plain greedy decoding, recomputed from scratch at every step, on the dynamic Qwen3.5 model, the native Qwen engine and Gemma 4, with a head that drafts the target's own continuation (every draft accepted, and every state it is handed checked against the target's), one that gets drafts wrong on purpose, and the real heads checked against themselves recomputed from scratch. Swapping in the first position's state, verifying one row off, rewinding one token short, replaying the head's cache one state off, or the old pairing each fails them
  - The Qwen loops for the dynamic model, the native engine and adapted models (which rebuilt both models from scratch every round) and Gemma 4's greedy and sampling loops are one loop now, `pmetal_models::mtp`, over a target and a draft head
- **Greedy MTP decoding left out the repetition, frequency and presence penalties.** `infer --mtp` and Gemma 4's `--draft-model` checked drafts against the target's bare argmax and took its own tokens the same way, where plain greedy decoding picks the argmax after the penalties. `GenerationConfig::default()` sets a repetition penalty of 1.1 and Qwen3.5's model-card preset a presence penalty of 1.5, so with MTP on, `pmetal infer` gave a different reply than without it. Drafts, their verification and the target's own tokens now all take the penalized argmax, and a test checks greedy MTP output with the three penalties on against the `Sampler`'s plain greedy decode on both Qwen engines and Gemma 4
- **`pmetal train-mtp` trained heads on other inputs than the ones they draft from.** The Qwen predictor learned to read the target's state at `i` beside `x[i]` and the Gemma 4 assistant beside `x[i]` at position `i`, the pairing the decoder used to have, and the assistant's token embedding left out the `sqrt(hidden_size)` scale the decoder and the reference apply. Both now train on the state at `i` beside the next token `x[i + 1]` (scaled, at position `i + 1`, for the assistant), against the target's choice for `x[i + 2]`. A head trained by an earlier version drafts poorly with the corrected decoder; train it again. Batches need three tokens or more
- **Gemma 4 E2B and E4B decoded from the wrong positions on the `DynamicModel` path.** Their last layers keep no KV cache of their own and attend to an earlier layer's, and with a cache each of those layers placed its queries by its own empty cache, so after the prompt every decoded token's queries sat at positions 0, 1, 2 and so on rather than at the token's position. A cached decode drifted from recomputing the same tokens within one token on a small model with KV sharing. Those layers now take their position from the layer whose cache they read. The native engine, which `pmetal infer` runs these models on, counts positions once for every layer and was right; the `DynamicModel` path serves LoRA inference, `serve`, evaluation and Gemma 4 MTP. The cached-decode test gains a KV-sharing configuration
- **Flux's VAE attention never ran.** The VAE's mid-block attention passed the mask mode `"none"`, which MLX rejects, so every decode's attention block failed and handed back a placeholder in its place; the pipeline's test checked only the output shape and passed. The block now runs unmasked attention, and the test fails on any bridge error during generation
- **A seeded Flux generation reseeded the thread's random key.** `FluxPipeline::generate` with a seed called `random::seed` on the thread's key before drawing its initial noise, which reset every later unkeyed draw on that thread to the same sequence. The noise now takes its own key, `draw_key(seed, 0)`, so the same seed gives the same image whatever else draws on the thread, and the thread's key is left alone
- **DiffusionGemma training's noise ignored the seed it was given.** `uniform_categorical_noise` accepted a `seed` and drew from the thread's random key regardless, and `DiffusionGemmaTrainer` drew each step's corruption the same way, so a step's noise depended on every other random draw made on the thread before it. Each step's noise level and corruption now come from the config's seed and the step number alone, through the same per-draw keys seeded sampling uses: the same seed trains on the same noise at every step whatever else draws in between, and the thread's key is left alone
- **`--embedding-lr` trained BERT's attention and MLP output projections at the embedding rate.** The embedding group took every parameter whose name contained one of a few strings, `output` among them, so with `--embedding-lr` set the LoRA adapters on BERT's `attention.output.dense` and `output.dense` trained at the embedding rate. Names now match on whole path components, against the names `transformers` gives the token embedding and the output head (`embed_tokens`, `wte`, `word_embeddings`, `embed_in`, `lm_head`, `embed_out` and a few more), and the head is the layer the model reports as its head, found by identity as PEFT finds it, so it is in the group whatever it is called. GPT-2's position embedding `wpe` is no longer in it
- **Binaries and tests computed on placeholders once the build directory that first built MLX was gone.** When MLX isn't told which Metal library to load, its last resort is a path compiled into libmlx, which names the CMake tree that built it. The build reuses one MLX build (a prefix slot) across build directories, so that tree was often another directory's, and once it was deleted any test binary or program that didn't name a metallib itself could not build MLX's Metal device: every bridge op then returned a placeholder, which showed up as arrays of shape `[0]` and `[matmul]` errors rather than as a missing file. The bridge now points MLX at the metallib built with the MLX it links (in the prefix slot) as it loads, its build script reruns when that slot changes or disappears, and a Metal device MLX can't build stops the process with the reason and the library it tried. It also refuses to run against a libmlx of a different version than the headers it was compiled with
- **Installed pmetal versions overwrote each other's Metal library.** Every build of `pmetal-runtime` copied its metallib to one unversioned `~/.cache/pmetal/lib/mlx.metallib`, so the last build or install of any MLX revision decided which kernels every installed binary loaded, and the CLI also accepted a package manager's `lib/mlx.metallib`, built from some other MLX. The cache now holds one file per MLX revision and content hash (`mlx-<revision>-<sha256>.metallib`), each written to a temporary file, checked against the hash of the copy the binary embeds and renamed into place; a damaged entry is replaced, and entries no binary has used for 30 days are removed. Build scripts write nothing outside their output directory. The search is `pmetal_runtime::metallib`, shared by the CLI and the GUI, and `pmetal info` reports the MLX revision and the metallib in use. The download fallback is gone, since every build that has a metallib embeds it
- **An op that failed early in a computation could go unreported.** Code that builds a graph over many ops and checks the bridge's error once at the end read the most recent op's slot, which every op that succeeds clears, so a failure early in the graph left a result computed on its placeholder and no error. Those checks now read the slot that keeps the first unobserved error (`check_unobserved_error`): quantizing a base for QLoRA and `Linear::quantize`, loading ModelOpt and gpt-oss checkpoints, the continuous-batching step, the Llama 3.2 Vision prefill, chunked cut cross-entropy, DFlash drafting and decoding, the decision head, the Qwen3.5 vision tower, gpt-oss's fused experts and Llama 4's expert split, and the preference trainer's log-probs and steps. Every test that drains bridge errors checks the same slot
- **`cargo install pmetal` built a binary that could not start once cargo deleted its build directory.** Release linked MLX as a dylib whose install name pointed into that directory. Release builds now link MLX statically unless `PMETAL_MLX_STATIC=0`, which this repository's `.cargo/config.toml` sets for its own test builds; the jobs that ship a binary still set `PMETAL_MLX_STATIC=1`, and the Python wheel build now does too
- **The Python wheel ran only while the tree that built it was still on disk.** Its extension module links MLX statically, but MLX loads its Metal kernels from an `mlx.metallib` beside the binary that links it, and the wheel carried none, so MLX fell back to the build tree's copy. The wheel workflow now copies the metallib built with the MLX the module links into the package, after checking it against the SHA-256 the bridge's build script publishes, and checks the wheel carries it. A wheel built that way and installed into a fresh virtual environment generates text on SmolLM2-135M with the build tree's metallib moved away; with a Metal library lacking MLX's kernels beside the module instead, it fails with `Unable to load kernel`, so the copy in the package is the one MLX reads
- **`--seed` and the serve request's `seed` did not make sampling reproducible.** The native engine, which `pmetal infer` and `pmetal serve` run for Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama 4, DeepSeek, gpt-oss and Gemma 4, never received the seed. The shared-model path reseeded the thread's one MLX key, so under `serve --continuous-batch` every other stream's draws shifted a seeded stream's tokens. `--ane` and the CPU hybrid engine drew from an OS-seeded RNG. Each sampled token is now drawn with its own MLX key, made from the seed and the token's index (`pmetal_runtime::decode::draw_key`), so a seeded generation picks the same tokens whatever thread runs it and whatever else is sampling; the ANE and hybrid engines seed their RNG. The first two tokens of a native generation also go through top-k, top-p and min-p now, which they had skipped. `CompiledSampler`, which stored a seed it never applied and passed nothing to `mx.compile`, is gone: the RL generator samples through `pmetal_models::generation::Sampler`
- **The Metal fused optimizer trains the same parameter groups as every other loop, so a run no longer trains differently depending on which loop it lands on.** It gave every parameter one learning rate and one weight decay, where the standard, packed and compiled loops leave biases and normalization weights undecayed, train embeddings at `--embedding-lr` and LoRA B matrices at the LoRA+ rate; it warned that it ignored LoRA+ and ignored the embedding rate without a word. Each group's learning rate and weight decay now reach the kernel as scalars of their own (a group index per parameter, one set of AdamW scalars per group), and both paths take the groups from one `ParameterGroupConfig`. The fused optimizer also held state for the whole model rather than its trainable parameters: on a LoRA run it stepped the frozen base, decaying it every step since a missing gradient was taken as zero, and a bf16 base it refused, as it takes f32 only. It holds the trainable parameters now, and a parameter without a gradient stays exactly where it is, moments included, as on the standard loop and in `torch.optim`
  - Over eight steps of a warmup-then-decay schedule on a model with a parameter in every group (an embedding at a quarter of the base rate, a norm weight and a bias, LoRA A and B at a LoRA+ ratio of 16, two gradients missing on one step), the fused update lands within 3.4e-7 of the standard loop's after every step; without the groups it ends 0.44 away. Run end to end on a small Llama with adapters on `q_proj`, `v_proj` and the LM head, the two loops end 1.6e-7 apart after four steps and neither moves a frozen weight. On that Llama the fused optimizer used to hold state for 214,144 elements, 12,096 of them trainable
  - Which parameters skip weight decay is the split `transformers`' `Trainer.get_decay_parameter_names` makes: anything with `bias`, `layernorm` or `rmsnorm` in its name and anything under a module called `norm` or `*_norm`, and, standing in for its `nn.LayerNorm` type check, anything under a module whose name contains `norm` or is GPT-2's `ln_*`. Learned scales such as Gemma 4's `layer_scalar` decay now, as they do there; a `scale` pattern used to leave them out
  - The bridge `AdamW` keeps its embedding rate as a multiple of the base rate, so `set_lr` moves it with the schedule as its documentation said. It was a fixed 5e-5 whatever the schedule did, and 5e-5 when no embedding rate was set at all; without one, embeddings now train at the base rate, as they do with a PyTorch optimizer that has no separate group for them
  - The kernel is as fast as before: one dispatch over 10M elements takes 0.77 to 0.80 ms before and after on an M4 Max. A whole fused step over 392 LoRA-sized adapters (10M elements, host copies included) takes 4.57 to 4.84 ms against 4.53 to 4.68 ms before
  - `ParameterGroup`, `ParameterGroupBuilder`, `create_parameter_groups`, `get_param_lr` and `lr_map_from_groups` are gone from `pmetal_trainer`. Nothing called them, and they grouped differently again: a norm weight inside an embedding took the embedding rate
- **`--seed` and the serve request's `seed` did not make sampling reproducible.** The native engine, which `pmetal infer` and `pmetal serve` run for Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama 4, DeepSeek, gpt-oss and Gemma 4, never received the seed. The shared-model path reseeded the thread's one MLX key, so under `serve --continuous-batch` every other stream's draws shifted a seeded stream's tokens. `--ane` and the CPU hybrid engine drew from an OS-seeded RNG. Each sampled token is now drawn with its own MLX key, made from the seed and the token's index (`pmetal_runtime::decode::draw_key`), so a seeded generation picks the same tokens whatever thread runs it and whatever else is sampling; the ANE and hybrid engines seed their RNG. The first two tokens of a native generation also go through top-k, top-p and min-p now, which they had skipped. `CompiledSampler`, which stored a seed it never applied and passed nothing to `mx.compile`, is gone: `--backend compiled` and the RL generator sample through `pmetal_models::generation::Sampler`
  - `pmetal infer --seed 7 --temperature 1` run twice wrote two different stories on Qwen3-0.6B and on Qwen3.5-0.8B, and now writes the same one; seed 8 differs. A seeded `/v1/completions` stream under `serve --continuous-batch` returns the same 40 tokens alone, again, and beside an unseeded and a differently seeded stream
- **Seeded speculative decoding was not reproducible either.** The Qwen3-Next and Qwen3.5 MTP paths, on both engines, and Gemma 4's assistant MTP seeded the thread's MLX key once when they started and drew their acceptance tests from a thread-local generator, so anything else drawing on the thread between tokens (another stream under `serve --continuous-batch`) changed a seeded stream's tokens. Every draft, correction and bonus token and every acceptance test is now its own draw from the seed (`pmetal_models::generation::SpeculativeDraws`), keyed as the plain sampler's tokens are. On a small random Qwen3-Next with its MTP head, a seed-7 sampled decode of 32 tokens comes out the same alone, with other draws on the thread between its tokens, and on another thread; seed 8 differs
- **A seeded DiffusionGemma generation is reproducible whatever else draws on the thread, and leaves the thread's key alone.** `DiffusionGemmaForBlockDiffusion::generate` seeded the thread's one MLX key with its seed and drew its canvases, proposals and renoising from it, so another draw on the thread between two generations changed what a seed produced, and every generation reset the key for everything drawing after it. Each draw now takes its own key, `pmetal_runtime::decode::draw_key(seed, n)` for the `n`-th, as every sampler does (`compat::random::randint` takes a key for it). A test draws on the thread between two generations with one seed and gets the same tokens, finds the thread's key where it left it, and gets other tokens from another seed
- **The Metal fused optimizer, on by default in `pmetal train`, never ran its Metal kernel.** `MlxMetalOptimizer::update_model_fused` handed every step to an AdamW written as MLX ops (concatenate every parameter, update, split back), and the one-dispatch `fused_adamw` kernel ran only in tests. It runs the kernel now, and the MLX-op paths are gone. Over LoRA-sized parameters (392 adapters, 10M f32 elements) an optimizer step takes 4.3 ms instead of 25.5 ms on an M4 Max, with the same weights to 1e-5 after 33 steps. A gradient whose size doesn't match its parameter now fails the step instead of being copied past its slot
- **The TUI charted every running job's progress on the dashboard of the one it was showing.** Its job-started, metrics and phase messages carried the job's id, but the TUI dropped it, so a pretrain run beside a training run, or an earlier run still finishing after a new one started, pushed its loss into the dashboard and its step into the training tab, and a new pretrain run reset its own tab's step count to zero. Each message now goes to the job it names: the dashboard and training tabs take only the job they show, and a pretrain run updates its own tab
- **QLoRA packed Gemma 4's MoE router, and LoRA adapted it.** Gemma 4 keeps its router's projection in a field named `proj`, which the rule that leaves routers alone (their argmax picks which experts run) did not recognise, so `--quantization` packed it to four bits and an empty `--lora-targets` put an adapter on it. A path ending `router.proj` is now a router, for Gemma 4 and DiffusionGemma
- **`--cut-cross-entropy` with sequence packing (the default) computed the full logits anyway, and said nothing.** The adapted model had no hidden-state forward with positions, so every packed step fell back to the full-logits loss. It has one now (`DynamicModel::forward_hidden_with_positions`, the trunk `forward_with_positions` runs, on every architecture that takes packed positions), and a test holds cut cross-entropy on a packed batch to the full logits' loss and every adapter's gradient on 16 architectures, with positions that restart and stretch so a forward that dropped them would not match. Every training path now asks one place whether cut cross-entropy applies, and when it can't (NEFTune on an unpacked path, image batches, a model with no usable LM head) the run warns once and uses the full logits; a model that offers a head but no hidden-state forward fails the step instead of switching losses. `nn::value_and_grad` returns the loss function's error rather than a NaN loss that hid it, and the packed path, which adds no NEFTune noise, says so when NEFTune is set
- **Cut cross-entropy used more memory than the full logits it exists to avoid.** Differentiating its chunked forward kept every chunk's logits for the backward pass, in f32, and slicing the head made each chunk's gradient a full-size copy of the head. On Qwen3-0.6B at 4,212 tokens a step took 9.0 GB against 2.6 GB for the full bf16 logits (gradient with respect to the hidden states). Each vocabulary chunk now runs under a gradient checkpoint and is rebuilt in the backward pass, as the reference implementation (Wijmans et al., arXiv 2411.09009) recomputes its logits; the head is split, so its gradient is one concatenation; the target logit is read out of its chunk instead of gathered from the head; and the chunks are chained, so MLX builds one at a time instead of all of them side by side. The same step now peaks at 1.5 GB (the full logits take 2.6 GB in bf16, 6.4 GB with the f32 softmax that matches its precision), and 3.9 GB instead of 9.0 with an adapter on the head. It is slower: 480 ms against 344 before and 247 ms (bf16) or 297 ms (f32 softmax) for the full logits, the price of rebuilding each chunk in the backward pass and running the chunks one at a time. It also no longer reads the number of valid targets back to the host every step
  - Precision follows the reference implementation: each chunk's logits come out of the matmul in the model's dtype, as the model's own head produces them, and the scale, softcap and logsumexp run in f32. Against an f32 log-softmax over the same tokens, the loss is off by 6e-5 and the gradients by 1.1e-2 (hidden states) and 9.8e-3 (head), the same as the full bf16 logits with an f32 softmax. Before, the head's gradient was off by 1.4e-2 on bf16, and by 7.5e-3 even on an f32 model, where it is now 3e-5. An opt-in test (`PMETAL_CCE_CHECKPOINT`) holds a real checkpoint to this, and reports each variant's memory and time
  - `CutCrossEntropyConfig` lost the fields nothing read (`label_smoothing`, accepted and ignored; `token_chunk_size`; `compute_grad`; `use_online_softmax`), and the hand-written backward and the two unused convenience functions are gone. `CutCrossEntropy::forward` returns the loss
- **A model's standalone `chat_template.jinja` is its chat template.** transformers writes the template to that file since v4.43 and reads it ahead of `tokenizer_config.json`, which for the gpt-oss release carries none. pmetal read only the config, so gpt-oss prompts came from a hand-written fallback: 23 tokens without the harmony system message where the release's template gives 80, after which gpt-oss-20b stopped at its first token. With the release's template it answers "What is the capital of France?" with its reasoning in the `analysis` channel and "Paris is the capital of France." in the final one, stopping at `<|return|>`
- **Llama 4's RoPE layers attend within their chunk past 8,192 tokens, on both engines.** Llama 4 splits the sequence into chunks of `attention_chunk_size` positions (8,192 on Scout and Maverick) and its RoPE layers attend only within a query's own chunk (transformers' `create_chunked_causal_mask`, the reference implementation's `block_pos == 0` mask), while the NoPE layers see the whole prefix. The `DynamicModel` path had no chunking at all, and the native engine applied it in prefill but let every decoded token attend to the whole cache. The `DynamicModel` attention now adds the chunk mask (with the cache's offset, or with packed positions), and native decode reads keys from the start of the token's chunk, on the compiled single-token graph, the per-op path and both quantized caches
  - A small fp32 Llama 4 with `attention_chunk_size` 6 matches transformers to 3.3e-7 on both engines over a 20-token forward and every step of a cached decode that starts after a 7-token prefill, crossing three chunk boundaries. Dropping the chunk mask fails it by 1.4, attending to the whole cache in decode by 0.97
- **Llama 4's routed experts load from a transformers checkpoint and compute what transformers computes, on both engines.** The `DynamicModel` path loaded none of a MoE layer's weights: the checkpoint's fused `experts.gate_up_proj` `[E, hidden, 2 × expert_hidden]` and `experts.down_proj` `[E, expert_hidden, hidden]`, its `router` and `shared_expert` sit under `feed_forward`, where this model has per-expert `Linear`s under `moe`, and the loader dropped what it didn't recognise. They are now split per expert and renamed (`llama4::sanitize_checkpoint`), as are the `language_model.*` keys of a multimodal release. The native engine transposed `down_proj`, which is already `[in, out]`; cast the router logits to bool (dtype id 0) instead of f32 before the sigmoid, so every routed token got the gate `sigmoid(1)`; and gave `gather_mm` `[tokens, 1]` expert indices against a `[tokens]` batch. It now runs the shared routed-expert dispatch
  - A small fp32 Llama 4 with two MoE layers (4 experts, top-1, expert width 48 against hidden 32) beside two dense ones matches transformers' `Llama4ForCausalLM` to 3.3e-7 on the `DynamicModel` path and 3.0e-7 natively, over a 20-token forward and every step of a cached decode, the native compiled single-token graph included. Swapping gate and up fails it by 1.8e-2, transposing `down_proj` by 8e-2, the bool cast by 6.5e-2
- **Log-probabilities were computed in the model's dtype.** On a bf16 model, serve's `logprobs` and the sampler's top-k, top-p and min-p filters worked on a log-softmax rounded to the logits' own precision, steps of 1/8 for logits between 16 and 32, so reported values came out as -0.125, 0 or -1.5. Log-probabilities are now computed in f32, as transformers' `generate` upcasts next-token logits before scoring them
- **`pmetal infer --image` and `--video` pasted the media's placeholders into the prompt's text instead of giving the media to the chat template.** The template never saw an image or a video item, so `--chat-template-kwargs '{"add_vision_id": true}'` numbered nothing (Qwen's templates write `Picture 1: ` and `Video 1: ` ahead of each), and any template that places its media items another way was bypassed. The media are now items of the prompt's message, ahead of its text, rendered by the model's own template: what `pmetal serve` does with a request's image and video parts, and what transformers' `apply_chat_template` does with `[{"type": "image"}, ..., {"type": "text"}]`. Without a chat template the placeholders still precede the text
  - For a picture and a question, Qwen3.5-0.8B's prompt is the same 321 ids in `infer`, in `serve` and in transformers' processor, and for an eight-frame video the same 197 ids in `infer` and `serve`
  - `infer` and `serve` reply alike to both. They decode on different engines that each round the logits to bf16, and the picture's first token is a tie at that precision (`" A"` over `" The"` by 0.066 in f32, equal in transformers' own bf16 run), which the two break differently; from there on the server agrees with `infer` token for token. The opt-in real-weights test now allows exactly that: wherever `infer`'s token is not the server's argmax it must be within 0.3 of it in the server's log-probabilities, and the server continues from `infer`'s choice
- **Llama 4 decodes from a cache, and attends causally, on the `DynamicModel` path** (training, LoRA, `serve`). Its attention rotated every forward from position 0 and dropped the positions it was given, `forward_with_cache` ignored the cache, so a decode step attended to nothing but its own token, and without a mask attention ran both ways. It now rotates through the shared rope module at the cache's offset or the given positions (`RopePositions`, as the other architectures do), appends to the cache, attends causally unless a mask says otherwise, and scales the NoPE layers' queries by the same positions. The QK-norm runs at `rms_norm_eps`, as transformers and the reference implementation run it, not at 1e-6. A dense layer's `feed_forward.*` weights now load
  - A small fp32 Llama 4 with Llama 3 bands, a NoPE layer and temperature tuning stepping every 4 positions matches transformers' `Llama4ForCausalLM` to 3.6e-7 on the logits of a 20-token forward and of every step of a cached decode after a 7-token prefill. Restarting the rotation or the temperature at 0 in a decode step fails it by 0.3 and 6e-4, and QK-norm at 1e-6 by 1.5e-4. Two records packed into one row now come out as two separate runs
  - The native engine matches the same model to 2.4e-7, compiled single-token graph included. It needed the same QK-norm epsilon, and its prefill had applied no temperature at all: the scale was built by concatenating 0-d arrays, which MLX refuses, and the error was swallowed. It also reads `attn_temperature_tuning: true`, `moe_layers` and `no_rope_layers`, which every transformers Llama 4 config carries
- **The native gpt-oss engine computes gpt-oss.** It had no attention sinks, and it could not run a checkpoint at all: the experts were transposed across every axis, gate and up were taken as the two halves of the fused projection rather than its interleaved columns and then swapped in the GLU, the router ignored its bias and weighted experts by a sigmoid instead of a softmax over the chosen logits, and a prompt longer than the 128-token window attended with the wrong keys on every sliding layer.
